FOREWORD 

The events of two years since the publication of the last edition of the Foundation Guide for Payroll Taxes has served to high light the trends in social security legislation for the next few years. What remains to be done in the immediate future in the fields of unemployment compensation and old-age insurance is to extend and simplify the program. 

No changes of major importance are to be contemplated in the ascertainable future. It is virtually certain that payroll taxes will remain the fundamental basis for raising the necessary funds. The rate of tax will be changed (perhaps in the present session of Congress) for old-age insurance; some form of pay-as-you-go financing will probably be substituted for the present plan of building up a tremendous reserve fund; and the plan of tax offsets for unemployment compensation may be liberalized to obviate the possibility of double taxation. The fundamentals of both programs, however, will not be changed. The rates and durations of benefits will still be largely based on wages earned, and old-age annuities also will be determined by past earnings.

Simplification of administrative techniques, however, is the new order of the day. The attempt to do workers exact justice through complicated determinations and redeterminations of full time weekly wages, weekly benefit amounts, and duration of benefits is rapidly giving way to the realization that greater good is done the worker by insuring him the prompt payment of his benefits when due, than by fixing his benefits meticulously on the basis of his exact past earnings. Merit rating, too, is faced with the conflict of clumsy procedures based solely on individual employers' experience, and demands simplified administration dealing quickly and accurately with employers' experience in the mass. 

Although a new book in the sense that it is independent of the former editions of the Foundation Guide for Payroll Taxes, this edition amplifies and makes specific applications, based on actual iii

INTRODUCTION 

rulings, of the principles of payroll tax legislation previously set forth. Employment, wages, state unemployment compensation laws and their exclusions, are all treated with a detail and comprehensiveness impossible two years ago. iv

CHAPTER I OLD-AGE INSURANCE Titles II and VIII of the Social Security Act provide for a com prehensive scheme of old-age insurance whereby benefits will be paid to individuals 65 years of age and over, after January 1, 1942.

Title II sets out the scheme for the payment of benefits, and Title 

VIII lays the two taxes used to finance the program. One tax is imposed on every employer in the United States, Alaska, and Hawaii who has employed one or more persons for wages for any interval of time, no matter how short, after December 31, 1936. 

This is an excise tax based on the privilege of hiring people. Its constitutionality was sustained by the Supreme Court in the case of Helvering v. Davis on May 24, 1937. As an excise tax it is deductible from the employer's federal and state income taxes annually. If the employer pays his employees' old-age insurance tax, such amounts may be deducted as a business expense in computing his taxable net income. 

The tax levied on employees by Title VIII is an income tax additional to that imposed by the federal internal revenue income tax statutes, and it cannot be deducted from an employee's gross income for federal income tax purposes. Although the employees' tax is measured by wages earned in employment, it is nevertheless a tax and not merely a transaction between employer and employee, and neither employer nor employee is entitled to a refund or credit on the discharge or resignation of the employee. Nor is the right to an old-age benefit dependent on whether a tax has been actually paid to the federal government by the employer. If the employee can prove that he was employed in employment covered by Title VIII, has earned sufficient wages, and has otherwise satisfied all the conditions of eligibility, he will be entitled to his old-age benefits or lump sum payment even though his employer has failed to report, or has incorrectly reported, his taxable wages. An alien on leaving the United States forever is not entitled to a refund of the taxes deducted from his wages, even though he will never draw out the benefits he might have had by remaining in this country. 

The employee's tax must be deducted from his wages at the time of payment, and the actual amount deducted must be paid over to the federal tax authorities. This amount may be different 1

than an amount computed at one per cent of all the taxable wages earned during a calendar quarter, which is the length of the reporting period.

Example: The Acme Cannery has employees who prepare beans for canning and who are paid 25 cents per basket, this payment being made immediately after inspection of the basket. During one day, one employee may prepare only one basket and receive only one wage payment of 25 cents, while another prepares four baskets and receives $1.00. The employees' tax must be computed on the basis of the total wages paid to the employee during such day. Accordingly, the Acme Cannery should deduct one cent from the employee who prepared four baskets and was paid $1.00, but nothing from the employee who earned only 25 cents, since the amount of employee's tax attaching to the 25 cents is less than one-half cent and is, therefore, disregarded. In computing the amount of the employer's tax under Title VIII, the total amount of wages, i.e., $1.25, should be included in the total amount of taxable wages reported on Form SS-1a.

The rate of the two old-age insurance taxes under Title VIII are the same for employer and employee. They are graduated at one-half of one per cent intervals over three-year periods beginning with one per cent in 1937 and rising to three per cent in 1949 and thereafter. The rates applicable for respective calendar years are as follows: 

Calendar Year 

Tax 

1937, 1938 and 1939.. 

1940, 1941, and 1942. 1 per cent 

12 per cent 

1943, 1944, and 1945.. 2 

1946, 1947, and 1948. per cent 

22 per cent 

1949 and thereafter.. 3 per cent

The employee's tax does not apply to any part of his wages for a calendar year from a single employer in excess of $3,000. 

The employer's tax does not apply to that part of his employees' wages in excess of $3,000 for a calendar year. The two taxes accrued on January 1, 1937, and apply only to wages for services performed on or after that date. If the services were performed before January 1, 1937, the remuneration therefor is not taxable even though it may have been paid after that date. 

Example: The A Company decided to award all of its employees who have been employed with them ten years or more on 

January 1, 1939, by giving them a cash bonus of $200 for each year of service. Only $400 of this bonus will be taxable to each employee since the tax applies only to wages, including bonuses, for services after January 1, 1937.

OLD-AGE INSURANCE 

3 The taxes are measured by the amount of wages actually or constructively paid or received on and after January 1, 1937, with respect to services in employment during the taxable year. This means that the taxes attach at the time that the wages are either actually or constructively received by the employee. The obvious case of actual receipt is one in which the employer hands the compensation to the employee. More difficult situations arise, however, in determining when constructive receipt has occurred. This concept is a legal fiction inasmuch as it does not involve any actual receipt at all. The employee is merely deemed by the law to have received his remuneration, and the taxes attach at that time. 

The usual circumstances surrounding constructive receipt are these: The wages are credited to the account of, or set apart for, an employee so that they may be drawn upon by him at any time. 

There must not be any substantial limitation or restriction as to the time or manner of payment, and the wages must be made available to him so that they may be drawn by him at any time and brought within his own control (Reg. 91, Arts. 203 and 303).

Therefore, it can be readily seen that there are three major elements involved: (1) that a credit be entered on the books of the company in the employee's favor, or the money be set aside for him, and (2) that this amount be available, and (3) subject to his unrestricted demand. If, however, by agreement between the employer and employee, the latter has contracted away his right to demand this sum, as, for example, where the employee is indebted to his employer and the employee agrees that the amount should be applied on the debt, a mere credit on the books of the company reducing the debt constitutes constructive receipt of the amount of the credit for purposes of the tax.

But where there is no agreement by the employee to surrender his right to demand payment, there is no constructive receipt of wages so long as the funds are not subject to the unrestricted right to demand by the employee, even though a credit has been entered upon the books of the company for his benefit and the money has been set apart for future disposal.

In the situation where a credit is entered upon the books of the company in favor of the employee, but his right to demand the money is restricted to the "convenience of the corporation," there is constructive receipt of the amount if the employee is in control of the company and if, further, the financial condition is such as to enable the company to pay. For all practical purposes, the "convenience of the corporation" under such circumstances really amounts to "his own convenience." Therefore, the employee actually has an unrestricted right to demand payment at any time and his entire salary is taxable at the time the entry is made. 

Officers of a corporation are, of course, employees under the Social 

Security Act. (Title XI, sec. 1101(a).)

Where the employee is in control of an enterprise which is in a financial position to pay him his salary, there is constructive receipt of the amount due him, at the time it is due him, even though no credit is entered on the books of the enterprise until some later date. Under these conditions, all that would be necessary to create a credit in his favor is the mechanical act of making an entry in his account.

But where the company is not in good financial condition, so that it is not able to pay the employee upon demand the amount owed him, there is no constructive receipt of the amount due until the company is able to pay him, because only at that time is the money available to the employee. 

It has already been pointed out that when the employee is in control of the company, it merely requires a mechanical act on his part to credit his account with the amount of compensation due him. The mere fact that the company is in bad financial condition and is therefore unable to pay the employee does not affect his unrestricted right to demand payment at any time. But the funds must also be available. Therefore, there is no constructive receipt until the company is able to pay the employee the amount due him. 

Care must be exercised in applying the correct rate of tax. 

The rate is determined by the calendar year during which the services were performed, and not by the calendar year in which the remuneration was actually or constructively received. Thus, there are two distinct questions which must be kept clear: (1)

The rate to be applied to the services in question, and (2) in the taxable payroll of what period must the remuneration for those services be included? The two do not always coincide, as the following illustration makes clear: 

Example: During 1939, Phillips is an employee of Rossiter. 

In the following year, 1940, Rossiter pays Phillips $1,000 as remuneration for the services which were performed by Phillips in the preceding year. The tax is payable at the one per cent rate in effect for the calendar year 1939 (the year of the employment), and not at the one and one-half per cent rate which is in effect for the calendar year 1940 (the year in which the wages are paid). 

Any individual or group of individuals, whether organized as an entity recognized in law or not, which hires other individuals to perform personal services for it or on its behalf, may be an employer. The term "employer" as used for old-age insurance tax purposes means one who employs one or more individuals for the performance of services (other than those which are exempted) within the United States. An employer may be a sole trader or individual proprietor, a partnership, corporation, trust or estate, a joint stock company, association, or a syndicate, group, pool, joint venture, or other unincorporated organization, group, or entity. An employer may be a person acting in a fiduciary capacity

OLD-AGE INSURANCE 

5 or on behalf of another, such as a guardian, trustee, committee, executor or administrator, trustee in bankruptcy, receiver, assignee for the benefit of creditors, or conservator. 

The relationship between the person for whom such services are performed and the individual performing them must constitute the legal relationship of employer and employee. Whether such relationship exists in any specific case is decided in the first instance by the federal Bureau of Internal Revenue, which is that section of the Treasury Department in charge of collections under the two taxing titles of the Social Security Act. The bureau decides such questions on the basis of well-established principles of law, the chief of which is that an employer has the right to control and direct the individual who performs the services, not only as to the results to be accomplished by the work, but also as to the details and means by which the desired result is accomplished. An employee is subject to the will and control of the employer not only as to what must be done but how it must be done. An independent contractor, that is, an individual who performs services for another outside of the employment, or master-servant relationship, cannot be controlled as to how his work is to be done, but must be permitted to do it as he sees fit so long as he accomplishes an agreed-upon result as per specifications given him. See Chap ters VII and XXI of the second edition of the Foundation Guide and Chapter VII of the present edition for a more complete discussion of who is an employee and what principles go toward establishing the existence of the employment relationship. 

The following exemptions to old-age insurance taxes relate to the type of services being performed or the character of the organization for whom they are performed: (1) agricultural labor; (2) domestic service in a private home; (3) casual labor not in the course of the employer's trade or business (according to Regulations 91 issued by the Bureau of Internal Revenue, a corporation cannot employ labor outside the course of its business); (4) service performed as an officer or member of the crew of a vessel documented under the laws of the United States or of any foreign country; (5) service performed in the employ of the United States government or of an instrumentality of the United States; (6) service performed in the employ of a state, a political subdivision thereof, or an instrumentality of one or more states or political subdivisions; (7) service performed in the employ of a corporation, community chest, fund or foundation, organized and operated exclusively for religious, charitable, scientific, literary, or educational purposes, or for the prevention of cruelty to children or animals, no part of the net earnings of which inures to the benefit of any private shareholder or individual; (8) service performed as an employee of a carrier, or for an organization of employees of carriers within the meaning of the Carriers' Taxing Act. These

6. 

exemptions apply to the services of an individual only at those periods when he is engaged in such activities. They do not inhere to the individual, and as soon as he performs services which are non-exempt, he and his employer are subject to the old-age insurance taxes. The following exemption, however, applies to and inheres in the individual, regardless of the nature of the services he is performing. This is the exemption from these taxes, both on employers and employees of individuals who have attained the age of 65. For a full discussion of exemptions, see Part III of the second edition of the Foundation Guide, and Chapter VI of the present edition. "Wages" is defined in the old-age insurance sections of the Social Security Act as all remuneration for employment, including the cash value of all remuneration paid in any medium other than cash; except that the term "wages" does not include that part of the remuneration which exceeds $3,000 paid to an individual by an employer with respect to employment during any calendar year. 

The limitation relates to remuneration for employment during any one calendar year and not to the amount of remuneration (irrespective of the year of employment) which is paid or received in any one calendar year. If the employee has more than one employer during the calendar year, the limitation of wages to the first $3,000 of remuneration received applies to remuneration received from each employer with respect to employment during that year.

As to what items constitute wages under the Social Security 

Act and state unemployment compensation acts, see Chapter XXVII of the second edition of the Foundation Guide and Chapter 

VII of the present work.

Benefits. Title II of the Social Security Act provides the statu tory framework for the system whereby the taxes collected under Title VIII go to pay lump sum benefits and annuities to qualified individuals. The states have no concern with the old-age insurance scheme either in the collection of taxes or the payment of benefits accruing to individual workers who are covered. Benefits will be paid in equal monthly installments to all men and women who meet the following requirements: 

1. They must be 65 years old. 

2. They must have received wages with respect to employment as defined in the Act on at least five days after December 31, 1936, and before attaining the age of 65, each day being in a different calendar year. 

3. They must have received total wages for employment subject to the Act, of at least $2,000 between December 31, 1936, and their sixty-fifth birthday.

OLD-AGE INSURANCE 

7 Under the plan as it is at present constituted, benefits will not be payable until January 1, 1942, since that is the earliest date on which an individual can have worked and earned wages for at least one day in five different calendar years after the plan went into effect. It is expected, however, that Congress may shortly amend this feature of the old-age insurance plan so as to provide for the earlier payment of annuities, perhaps in 1940. The President has already recommended this to Congress. 

Individuals who are qualified for benefits must leave all regular employment subject to the plan, after attaining the age of 65, or they will be penalized by the amount of their monthly benefit for each month in which they engage in such employment. 

The definitions of "wages" and "employment" for purposes of paying benefits are the same as those used in the taxing sections of the plan, i.e., wages are that part of the remuneration paid by an employer to an employee which does not exceed $3,000 in any calendar year, and employment is subject to the same exemptions. 

These exemptions are: (1) agricultural labor; (2) domestic service in a private home; (3) casual labor not in the course of the employer's trade or business; (4) service performed as an officer or member of the crew of a vessel documented under the laws of the United States or of any foreign country; (5) service performed in the employ of the United States government or of an instrumentality of the United States; (6) services performed in the employ of a state, a political subdivision thereof, or an instrumentality of one or more states or political subdivisions; (7) service performed in the employ of a corporation, community chest, fund or foundation, organized and operated exclusively for religious, charitable, scientific, literary, or educational purposes, or for the prevention of cruelty to children or animals, no part of the net earnings of which inures to the benefit of any private shareholder or individual; (8) service performed as an employee of a carrier or for an organization of employees of carriers within the meaning of the Carriers' Taxing Act; (9) individuals who have attained the age of 65.

The rate of the monthly benefit payment is to be determined on the basis of each individual's total "wages" received with respect to "employment" during the entire period from January 1, 1937, to his sixty-fifth birthday. In calculating total wages, all sums in excess of $3,000 from any one employer in one year are excluded. An individual who works for more than one employer can include in his total wages for each year all amounts earned up to $3,000 from each employer. If he earns $3,500 per year from four employers, his wages on which he could base his monthly benefit would be $12,000 per year.

The amount of each individual's monthly benefit will be equal to the sum of the following: (1) one-half of one per cent of the

first $3,000 of total wages; (2) one-twelfth of one per cent of the next $42,000 of total wages; (3) one twenty-fourth of one per cent of the remainder of the total wages, or all sums in excess of $45,000. Since an individual in order to qualify for benefits must have earned at least $2,000 between January 1, 1937, and his sixty-fifth birthday, the monthly minimum is $10. The maximum monthly benefit established by the Social Security Act is $85, representing taxable earnings of $129,000.

Illustration: An employee earns $1,500 in 1938, $2,500 for the next five years, and $4,500 thereafter, until he reaches 65 in 1975.

Any individual who has worked in included employment after 

December 31, 1936, but who cannot qualify for monthly benefits because he has earned less than $2,000 after that date, or because he has not worked in five different years between December 31, 1936, and his sixty-fifth birthday, will receive a lump-sum payment equal to 32 per cent of his total taxable wages on reaching 65. 

This payment exhausts his old-age benefits rights, but if he should die before receiving this sum it will go to his estate. These provisions of the Act ensure each employee that he or his heirs will get back more than they have paid in to the old-age benefit fund. 

Illustration: Higgins is 61 years of age on January 1, 1937, and has earned $3,000 or more a year until December 31, 1940, when he quits his employment. He receives no old-age annuity because he has not worked during five years before he attains age 65 and subsequent to December 31, 1936, but he does receive a lump sum payment of 32 per cent of his total wages to age 65.

9 After the payment of this lump sum benefit the individual is not entitled to receive any other old-age benefits under Title II. 

There are two types of death benefits payable in the old-age insurance plan, both, however, being based on the figure of 3½ per cent of an individual's taxable wages. Under the first type, an employee who dies before reaching age 65 will leave to his estate 

32 per cent of his taxable wages. If he has qualified for monthly benefits but dies before he has received at least 32 per cent of his wages in benefits, his estate will be entitled to the difference between what he actually received and 32 per cent of his wages. 

This is in the nature of a minimum guarantee, since any individual who lives beyond age 65 is not limited in his monthly benefits to 32 per cent of his wages, but is entitled to his benefit as long as he lives. 

Illustration 1. A man earns $1,500 in 1937, $2,000 in 1938 and 1939, and then dies:

32 per cent of earnings $5,500.00 192.50

This is the amount his estate would be entitled to. 

Illustration 2. A man earns $45,000 between January 1, 1937, and the time he becomes 65 in 1952. His monthly benefit amount is $50, computed as follows: one-half of one per cent of the first $3,000 is $15, and one-twelfth of one per cent of the next $42,000 is $35, or a total of $50 per month. He lives for five years in which time he receives 60 monthly payments at $50 each or a total of $3,000.

Illustration 3. The same individual as in illustration 2 dies after receiving one year of benefits, twelve monthly payments at $50 each, or $600. His estate will receive the difference between this amount and 32 per cent of his taxable wages:

The benefits outlined above are for employees only. Individuals who own their own businesses, follow their trades or professions as sole proprietors or in partnership, will receive no benefits under the Act as it stands at present. There is, however, a considerable movement on foot to include some, if not all, of these classes, among those entitled to participate in the old-age insurance program. The same types of employment as are exempted from payment of the old-age insurance tax are also excluded from the receipt of benefits from the fund. This is in line with the policy that the fund is to be as completely self-sustaining as possible and that only those who contribute thereto are entitled to share in its

benefits. (See page 7 for the types of employment exempted from the old-age insurance tax.) The collection of the old-age insurance tax is part of the work of the federal Bureau of Internal Revenue while determinations as to eligibility for old-age benefits is the work of the Social Security Board. These are independent agencies, and the decisions of one are not binding on the other. 

It thus happens, though infrequently, that the Bureau of Internal Revenue rules that an individual is an independent contractor and that therefore no tax is required on his account. If the individual on reaching age 65 applies for benefits, the Social Security Board may hold that he was an employee rather than an independent contractor, and therefore entitled to benefits based on his earnings, even though no tax was ever paid thereon. Or it may happen that the bureau will hold someone as an employee and exact the oldage insurance taxes from himself and his employers, and when he applies for benefits the board may rule that he is not entitled to anything on the ground that he was an independent contractor. 

Administration. Every employer of one or more persons after 

January 1, 1937, must keep records which will show his obligations under the old-age insurance tax. These obligations are of two sorts to keep records showing the amount of wages paid to each of his employees, and to collect and turn over to the federal government the amount of the employee's tax. 

Every employer liable for the old-age insurance taxes is required to keep accurate records of all remuneration paid to his employees after December 31, 1936, for services performed for him after that date. Such records must show with respect to each employee- (1) the name and address of the employee and his social security account number, (2) the occupation of the employee, (3) the total amount (including any sum withheld as tax or for any other reason) and date of each payment of wages and the period of services covered by such payment, (4) the amount of such payment which constitutes wages subject to tax, and (5) the amount of employees' tax withheld or collected with respect to such payment, and, if collected at a time other than the time such payment was made, the date collected.

If the total remuneration payment and the amount which is taxable are not the same, the reason for the difference must be made a matter of record. Accurate records of the details of every adjustment of employees' or employers' tax must also be kept, including the date and amount of each adjustment.

In many industries and localities it is a common practice for an employee, with either the express or implied consent of his em-

OLD-AGE INSURANCE 

11 ployer, to engage a substitute for himself and pay such substitute out of his own wages. In other instances a skilled worker will likewise hire and pay one or more helpers or assistants. It is not sufficient in such cases for the employer's records to show only the name and wages paid to the original employee. Substitutes or helpers are also employees, and the records must disclose all the information required in the case of other employees. The fact that the relationship between the substitute or helper and the employer has come into existence without direct contact between the parties does not relieve the employer from the duty of keeping the records mentioned previously. The employees' old-age insurance tax must be deducted from the wages of the substitute or helper, a statement of the tax so deducted must be furnished him, and the tax returns must properly reflect his taxable wages. 

There are no prescribed forms for the manner in which these records should be kept except that the employer should use forms which will enable the federal authorities to determine his tax liability upon inspection. Records must be kept for a period of at least four years after the date on which the tax becomes due, or the date the tax is paid, whichever is the later. 

Article 206 of Regulations 91, which deals with the old-age insurance tax, provides that an employer must furnish to his employees a written statement showing the amount of the employees' tax deducted from his wages. The employer can use any form or method suitable to the purpose which will provide the employee at the time of payment with written information as to the amount of the tax deductions. Such written statement must be given to the employee and retained by him for at least a period long enough for him to determine whether his tax has been correctly computed and deducted. A notation on a check issued to an employee as his wages showing the amount of the tax deduction is sufficient compliance. 

An entry on a clock card, pay envelope, or pay voucher are other methods by which this condition may be satisfied, provided that the employee has a reasonable opportunity to examine it. The fact that an employer may assume payment of the employees' tax without deducting it does not relieve him of the duty of furnishing a written statement as in other cases. Where all or any part of the wages are paid in cash, the statement must be furnished at the time of each wage payment. In cases where the wages are paid exclusively in a medium other than cash, for example, where the employer furnishes the employee with the use of a dwelling as his remuneration, the statement should be furnished at the end of each calendar month.

Returns. Employers who are now subject to the old-age insurance taxes have all been provided with identification numbers to be used on all their tax and information returns due under Title VIII of the Social Security Act. These were issued late in 1936

on completion of form SS-4 issued by the Social Security Board. 

All individuals or organizations becoming newly liable for the oldage insurance tax are required to procure form SS-4 at any field office of the Social Security Board for the purpose of having an identification number issued to them. These numbers are not the same as those issued by state unemployment compensation agencies for purposes of their laws, nor are the two numbers interchangeable. 

All persons who are in employment within the meaning of the old-age insurance tax are required to procure social security account numbers, applications for which are made on form SS-5. It is extremely important for employers to require their employees to have social security account numbers and to be informed of such number. If an employee has left the employer's service without having secured a number, it is the duty of the employer to fill out form SS-5 for the employee, on the basis of his best information and belief. Applications for social security account numbers can be had at any field office of the Social Security Board. 

For the year 1937 employers reported their tax remittances monthly on form SS-1, and their information returns on the taxable wages paid to each of their employees on a semi-annual basis. 

This information return was on form SS-2. Beginning on January 1, 1938, the tax and information returns were combined on the same form, SS-1a, which is filed quarterly within the month following the close of each calendar quarter on March 31, June 30, September 30, and December 31. Form SS-1a is to be filled out in duplicate, the original to be filed with the Collector of Internal Revenue for the district in which the employer is located, and the duplicate to be retained by the taxpayer. In case the prescribed tax return form is not available, a statement made by the employer disclosing, for the period for which a return was required, the amount of taxable wages and the amount of taxes due may be accepted as a tentative return and will relieve the employer from liability for the penalty imposed for delinquent filings, provided that the return is made on the prescribed form as soon as possible. 

Taxable wages are to be reported on form SS-la on a "wages paid" basis, i.e., they are to be reported in the tax return for the period in which they are actually or constructively paid. There is an important exception to this, however, which is available at the option of the taxpayer-taxable wages may be reported in a tax return for a prior quarter if the payroll period ended within that quarter, even though the wages were not actually paid in that quarter. This option applies only to tax-return periods prior to 

January 1, 1940.

Illustration: The X Company pays its employees on the nineteenth and fourth of every month for payroll periods ending on the fifteenth and end of each month. It can include on its tax return form SS-1a the taxable wages for the last pay period in each

OLD-AGE INSURANCE 

13 calendar quarter (tax-return period), even though the wages are not actually paid until the fourth of the following month which falls in the calendar quarter subsequent to the one for which the tax-return is being filed.

It is this possibility of reporting wages for that quarter in which the payroll period ends which made it feasible for some state unemployment compensation agencies to permit employers to make out the state contribution returns as duplicates of the federal old-age insurance tax return, form SS-1a. The great majority of state laws require contributions on a wages payable basis, differing from the old-age insurance tax which is on a wages paid basis. In effect, however, the permission granted by Regulations 

91 to report and pay taxes on wages earned in a quarter although they are paid in the first month of the subsequent quarter, means that the same basis of payment is used for both the state unemployment compensation contributions and the old-age insurance tax, when payment follows shortly after performance-that is, the tax payment is to be made at the time that the services for which remuneration will be paid were performed. For reasons which will be explained later, it is not always possible to fill out a state contribution report as a duplicate of form SS-1a.

The Bureau of Internal Revenue up to October 1, 1938, re quired all employers to fill out form SS-3 whenever an employee died or attained age 65. The period for which the return was filed was from the first day of the calendar quarter in which the employee died or became 65, to the date of his death or to the day preceding the date of his sixty-fifth anniversary of his birth. For example, if an employee's sixty-fifth birthday was on February 15, he attained age 65 at the first moment of February 14. The employer had to show on the return the total taxable wages actually or constructively paid within the period covered, for services performed by the employee, but the tax based on such wages had to be paid when the next quarterly return, form SS-1a, was filed. 

Wages for taxable employment performed by the employee prior to the date he attained age 65 or the time of his death had to be included in the return even though paid or payable to him or his estate after such event. Remuneration for services performed after the employee attained age 65 did not constitute taxable wages and did not have to be reported on this return. The return had to be in the hands of the local Collector of Internal Revenue on or before the fifteenth day after (1) the date of the employee's sixty-fifth anniversary of birth, or (2) the date of the employee's death, in case he died before attaining age 65.

If the employee had died, the employer did not have to file any proof of death with form SS-3. For employees who had attained age 65, affidavits had to be filed by both employer and employee. The employee's statement set out the date and place of

his birth to the best of his information and belief. The employer's statement set forth whether or not, to the best of his information and belief, the employee's statement was correct and what, if anything, his records showed as to the date and place of the employee's birth. After form SS-3 was filed, any inquiries relating to establishing the age of the employee and evidence in connection therewith were addressed to the Social Security Board and not to the Bureau of Internal Revenue. 

In connection with the employer's affidavit which was required to be attached to form SS-3, if the employer did not believe the employee's statement to be correct, or if the date of birth shown by the employee differed from that shown on the employer's records, it was up to the employer to establish to the satisfaction of the Commissioner of Internal Revenue that the employee had, in fact, attained age 65. While it is not possible to state in advance what evidence will be satisfactory in connection with all such cases, the four most acceptable types of evidence in order of acceptability are as follows: (1) the original of the employee's birth certificate or a photostatic or certified copy thereof, (2) an authenticated copy of the employee's infant baptismal certificate or similar church record, (3) a certified copy of family or business records showing the employee's age or date of birth, or (4) affidavits of individuals having knowledge of the employee's age or date of birth. 

Until January 1, 1942, wage earners who become 65 or who die before that age are entitled only to lump-sum benefits. Lumpsum benefits will be certified by the Social Security Board to the United States Treasury for payment only upon application of the wage earner if he is living and competent to act; if he is deceased or incompetent to act on his own behalf, the application for lumpsum benefits must be made by his guardian, conservator, or other legal representative who is entitled under the laws of the state in which the employee lived to receive payment. The application must be made on certain prescribed forms which are described below.

Form OAC (Old-Age Claim) 1002 is the application form prescribed for wage earners who have reached age 65. It calls for the wage earner's date of birth; the day preceding his sixty-fifth birthday; and a list of the employers he has worked for between 

January 1, 1937, and the day preceding his sixty-fifth birthday.

With this form the employee is also given form OAC 1001, one copy of which he must give to each of the employers he has worked for in the period shown on his application for lump-sum benefits. 

The Social Security Board makes the filing of form OAC 1001 

purely voluntary by employers but requests their cooperation in facilitating their employees' claims for benefits.

Form OAC 1001 is a certification by the employer that he has paid a certain amount as wages for employment, as defined by

OLD-AGE INSURANCE 

15 Title VIII, to a named employee in 1937 and 1938, not including any wages paid for employment on the day preceding the employee's sixty-fifth birthday. If an employer fills out this form and files it or hands it to the applicant, it did not relieve him prior to October 1, 1938, of the necessity of filing form SS-3 with the 

Collector of Internal Revenue. 

When a wage earner dies without leaving a will, the widow, widower, or close relative should file form OAC 1003, as an application for lump-sum benefits. Even if the deceased left a will, form OAC 1003 may still be filed by the surviving spouse if it is definitely certain that the will is not to be probated, and the laws of the state in which the wage earner was last domiciled do not require the probate of the will. In such cases, however, payment of the claim, if it does not exceed $500, is discretionary with the Social 

Security Board. 

Form OAC 1004 up to January 1, 1939, was to be used when there was no surviving spouse, no will or administration of the estate, and the wage earner was survived by a child or grandchild, or by a parent, in the order of preference for filing of the claim. 

These classes now use amended form OAC 1003. 

Form OAC 1005 is to be used by the executor or administrator of a wage earner's estate. Certified copies of the letters of administration or letters testamentary, or a short certificate thereof, must be attached. Form OAC 1006 is the application for lumpsum benefits to be used by a guardian or committee of a legally incompetent wage earner. It must be accompanied by a certified copy of the letters of appointment, or a short certificate thereof. 

In certain cases it may be necessary for the applicant, either the wage earner himself, or one of those permitted to initiate claims for lump-sum benefits to prove by corroborative evidence, the date of the wage earner's birth. Such further proof is necessary only when application is made on reaching age 65, the amount of the claim exceeds $100 (the claim will exceed $100 if the wage earner has earned more than $2,857 in covered employment), and the statement contained in the application as to the wage earner's date of birth is at variance with the date of birth as shown by the records of the Social Security Board, or there is some other reason for suspecting that the date of birth shown on the application may not be the true date. Corroborative evidence of the date of birth may be supplied by a certified copy of, or a statement as to the date of birth shown by a public record of the birth, or a certified copy of a church record of infant baptism; or, a sworn statement of two other persons having knowledge thereof. 

Where application is made for a claim based on the death of a wage earner, the applicant must furnish proof of the death. Such proof may be provided as follows:

(1) A statement signed by the undertaker or attending physician, or superintendent, physician or interne of the institution where deceased died, or by the custodian of the public record of deaths identifying the deceased and stating the time and place of death, or (2) A copy of the public record of the state or community where death occurred, or a copy of a coroner's report of death, or of the verdict of a coroner's jury, provided that the report or verdict properly identifies the deceased. (3) If the evidence specified in (1) and (2) above cannot be obtained, and satisfactory reason for failure to provide it is shown, the death of the individual may be established by affidavits of persons who have personal knowledge thereof and have viewed the body of the person whose death is in question, setting forth facts and circumstances concerning the death, including the place, date, time and cause thereof. (4) Where death occurs outside of the United States a report of the death by a United States Consul, or other agent of the State Department must be furnished. 

Applications for lump-sum benefits and supporting data must be filed with the Bureau of Old-Age Benefits of the Social Security Board in Washington, D. C., or with any field office of the board.

CHAPTER II  

Because the railroad industry as a whole is more unified and integrated than almost any other major American industry, and because of the magnitude and variety of its problems which are unduplicated in any other field, Congress felt that separate social security legislation was necessary for the railroads and their workers. The fact that railway labor was strongly organized and the railroads were under close and strict supervision by governmental agencies made it certain that the acute social security problem of the railroads would be tackled early.

The first Railroad Retirement Act, an old-age insurance measure for railway workers, was passed by Congress in 1934, whereas the Social Security Act was not passed until August, 1935. This first act was declared unconstitutional as a whole by the Supreme Court in 1935 because certain invalid provisions were declared inseparable from the rest of the act. Congress immediately passed two acts, The Carriers' Taxing Act of 1935 and the Railroad Re tirement Act of 1935. These acts were attacked in the courts by most of the major carriers, but the opposition lost much of its effect when the federal Social Security Act containing an old-age insurance scheme in Titles II and VIII was approved by the Supreme Court in May, 1937. In June, 1937, after an agreement between the carriers and the railway labor organizations, it was decided that the railroads should drop their pending appeal to have the 1935 acts declared unconstitutional. The two sides agreed on a new old-age insurance program for carrier employees, and on 

June 24, 1937, the Railroad Retirement Act of 1937 replaced the 1935 act, which still continues in force with respect to rights to annuities which had already accrued. Five days later, the Carriers' Taxing Act of 1937 was passed to replace the 1935 act, which was repealed.

Although the railroad old-age insurance plan preceded the Social Security Act, it was not until almost two years after the passage of the Social Security Act that Congress passed an unemployment insurance bill specifically designed for the railroads. 

Previous to June, 1938, when the Railroad Unemployment Insur- 17

ance Act was passed, the carriers were subject to state unemployment insurance laws to the same extent as all other employers. 

Indeed, until July 1, 1939, when the Railroad Unemployment Insurance Act goes into full effect, they are still subject to the state acts, principally through the necessity of paying unemployment contributions to the state funds.

An employer is defined in identical terms in the Carriers' Taxing Act of 1937, the Railroad Retirement Act of 1937, and the Railroad Unemployment Insurance Act. The definition is designed to include all organizations whose function in some way aids or promotes transportation by steam. First of all, an employer is any company or person principally engaged in the carrier business. 

This means any express company, sleeping car company, or carrier by railroad which is subject to Part I of the Interstate Commerce Act. If any company or person is principally engaged in some other than the carrier business, but as an incidental part of its business does engage in some carrier business, the determination of whether it is subject to the railroad security program will be made by the Railroad Retirement Board in the light of considerations such as the following: (a) the primary purpose of the company or person on and since the date it was established; (b) the dominance or subservience of its carrier business in relation to its non-carrier business; (c) the amount of its carrier business and its ratio to the entire business; (d) whether its carrier business is a separate and distinct enterprise. 

The three railroad acts also apply to any company which is directly or indirectly controlled or owned by one or more employers, as defined here (express company, sleeping car company or carrier by railroad, subject to Part I of the Interstate Commerce Act) or is under common control therewith. Such company to be subject must operate some equipment or facility or perform some service, with the exception of trucking or casual service, in connection with the transportation of passengers or property by railroad, or in connection with the receipt, delivery, elevation, transfer in transit, refrigeration or icing, storage, or handling of property transported by railroad. A. company may be controlled either directly or indirectly, through legally enforceable means or otherwise. The control may be by means of stock ownership, or by agreements, licenses, or any other devices which insure that the operation of the company is in the interests of one or more car riers.

The term "employer" under the railroad acts does not include any street, interurban, or suburban electric railway, unless

it is operated as a part of a general steam railroad system of transportation. It does not exclude, however, any part of a general steam-railroad system of transportation which on January 1, 1937, or thereafter is operated by any other motive power. If controversy arises between a carrier and the Bureau of Internal Revenue or the Railroad Retirement Board as to whether it is an electric line whose operation would make it an "employer," the Interstate Commerce Commission is authorized, after a hearing, to make such determination.

Any receiver, trustee, or other individual or body, judicial or otherwise, is an employer when in possession of the property or operating all or any part of the business of an employer as heretofore defined.

Certain other organizations which do not operate equipment or facilities aiding in transportation or its allied activities are also deemed to be employers. This includes railroad associations, traffic associations, tariff bureaus, demurrage bureaus, weighing and inspection bureaus, collection agencies, or any other type of organization which is wholly or principally maintained by two or more employers under the railroad acts, and engaged in the performance of services in connection with or incidental to railroad transportation. The final category of employers subject to the railroad acts are railway labor organizations, national in scope which are organized in accordance with the provisions of the Railway Labor Act, and their subsidiary organizations, such as their state or national legislative committees, general committees, insurance departments, and local lodges or divisions. 

An employer as defined in the railroad acts is subject to the liabilities of those acts irrespective of the number of individuals employed. However, since the taxes under the Carriers' Taxing

Act and the Railroad Unemployment Insurance Act are based on remuneration paid to employees, it becomes important to determine who are employees. In general, an employee is any individual who renders services to an employer for compensation, and who is subject to the continuing right of the employer to supervise and direct the manner in which the services are to be performed. An individual is in the service of an employer whether his service is rendered within or without the United States if he meets the requirements mentioned above, except that if his employer does not conduct the principal part of its business in the United States he is considered to be in employment only when he is rendering service to the employer in the United States. 

Since both the payment of the carriers' tax and the payment of retirement and unemployment benefits are based upon the existence of an employment relationship, the Railroad Retirement

Board has set up elaborate rules to aid in such determination. The board administers the payment of retirement and unemployment

benefits, and the collection of unemployment contributions or taxes. The collection of the taxes under the Carriers' Taxing Act is entrusted to the Bureau of Internal Revenue in the Treasury Department, which is the usual federal tax collecting agency. 

These collections go into the general treasury of the United States and are not earmarked for any special fund. The railroad unemployment insurance tax, however, goes into the treasury, but is invested as a special fund to be used only for the purpose of paying unemployment benefits to railroad workers.

It has thus devolved upon the Railroad Retirement Board to determine for its varied purposes, exactly who are in an employment relationship to the "employers" liable under the railroad acts. The Railroad Retirement Act in Section 1 (d) defined it as follows: 

"An individual is in the employment relation to an employer if he is on furlough, subject to call for service within or outside the United States and ready and willing to serve, or on leave of absence, or absent on account of sickness or disability; all in accordance with the established rules and practices in effect on the employer: Provided, however, That an individual shall not be deemed to have been on the enactment date in the employment relation to an employer not conducting the principal part of its business in the United States unless during the last payroll period in which he rendered service to it prior to the enactment date, he rendered service to it in the United States." 

Another section of the act includes as an "employee" (1) any individual in the service of one or more employers for compensation, (2) any individual who is in the employment relation to one or more employers, and (3) an employee representative of a railway labor organization. It also includes employees of a local lodge or division if such lodge or division is an "employer," and then only if the individual concerned was in the service of or in the employment relation to a carrier on or after the enactment date of the Railroad Retirement Act of 1935, August 29, 1935. Officers of employers are also deemed to be employees, as under the Social 

Security Act. 

The first question that arises under this definition of employment is under what circumstances services are being rendered for compensation? The Bureau of Internal Revenue under the Carriers'

Taxing Act and the Railroad Retirement Board under the Retire ment and Unemployment Insurance Acts will follow the familiar and well-established legal principles governing the existence of an employer-employee relationship, thereby distinguishing between employees and independent contractors. The latter, of course, do

not "perform services for compensation" within the meaning of these acts.

Of the cases in which question arises as to whether an employer-employee relationship exists, probably the most common is that in which an individual is engaged by a company which is admittedly an "employer," to perform certain work pursuant to a contract, and this contracting individual has persons working under him. Whether this individual and his assistants are within the scope of the acts as employees will ordinarily depend upon whether the nature of the arrangements between the contracting parties is such as to constitute the contractor an "employee" or an "independent contractor." If he is an "employee," then the persons working under him on the contract job will normally be "employees" of the employer company, while, conversely, a finding that the contractor is "independent" will normally mean that he and his employees are outside the scope of the social security railroad legislation. The principal test used to determine the status of the contractor and his employees is: does the alleged employer possess the right to control the contractor as to the means and methods of doing the work, as contrasted with control over results only?

The contract between the parties, whether it be oral or written, is examined first, and this may determine the question as to the right of control. But if, after study of the contract, the question of control remains, other factors in the situation may be material to a solution as to the status of the contractor. Some of the pertinent facts concerning the status of the contractor and the persons working under him are those as to the actual supervision and direction of the performance of the contract work; the manner in which the contractor and his employees are paid; who can terminate the contract, and upon what terms it can be terminated; who can hire, discharge, and discipline the workmen; what rights as employees of the alleged employer, such as that of free transportation, do the contractor and workmen possess; what is the nature of the contract work; what are the duties of the contractor and the workmen; are those duties performed on the premises of the alleged employer; does the contractor maintain an office or other place of business at his own expense; does the contractor offer his services to the general public; who furnishes the tools, equipment, and supplies used in the performance of the contract work; are there requirements as to the time the contractor and his workmen are to devote to the contract work; who is liable for damage to property or injuries to persons caused by the performance of the contract work; and does the contractor bear any risk of loss under the contract.

The factors to which reference has been made as important in determining whether a contractor and his workmen are "em-

ployees" of the employer company or an "independent contractor,” are also important in ascertaining the "employee" status of any individual, such as an attorney, though there may be no contract involved.

There should be mentioned in connection with the topic of employee coverage two types of situations, namely (1) "special" employees, and (2) "casual" employees. The first type of situation is one in which an individual who is in the general employ of one company or person is lent or otherwise transferred to another company or person in order to perform certain work. If in the performance of that particular work the individual is subject exclusively to the control of the company or person to whom he is transferred, it may be found that the individual is the employee of that company so far as the work in question is concerned. In other words, an individual may be in the general employ of one company or person, and yet be an employee of another company or person as to certain work. 

For example, a railroad may lend an employee to an industrial company to aid the latter company in its operation of a private plant railroad. If the individual became subject to the exclusive control of the industrial company as to the manner of performance of work for that company, he would be in fact the employee of that company as to that work, though he remained in the general employ of the railroad, being paid by it, on its seniority roster, and was subject to discharge by the railroad. In such a case, the individual involved would be in the service of the railroad or of the company to which he was lent only as to the work done for each of the respective companies. 

The other type of situation mentioned above is that of a "casual" employee, that is, one who is employed only at irregular intervals or for short periods of time. If such individuals are subject to the continuing authority of the employing company to supervise and direct them as to the manner of rendition of such service as they perform, they are "employees" within the meaning of the railroad acts.

The second category of employees covered by the railroad acts are individuals who are in the employment relationship to an employer, even though they may not at the time be performing any services. The importance of the inclusion of this category is appreciated when it is realized that the payment of the railroad retirement annuities is partly based on "years of service" with a subject employer, and that an employment relationship, entitling the employee to credit for years of service, may be established even though there may have been no performance of services for compensation. An individual is in the employment relationship to an employer if he is on furlough, subject to call for service within or outside the United States and ready and willing to serve, or on

leave of absence, or absent on account of sickness or disability. 

Such departures from actual service for compensation must all be in accordance with established rules and practices which the employer, as a matter of course or through contract, is obliged to follow. An employment relationship cannot exist unless it represents a continuous relationship following immediately upon actual compensated service. It is not brought into existence through an application for employment, or inclusion on a waiting list, or on a promise of employment preceding actual service or following an interruption of the relationship. There must also be some contemplation of a resumption of active service. Thus, where the terms of a furlough limit the time within which the employee may return to active service, and such limit has expired without the individual's returning to active service, the employment relationship will be considered as having ended.

The third and last category of employees covered by the railroad acts are "employee representatives" of certain railway labor organizations other than those which are defined as "employers" in the acts ("railway labor organizations, national in scope, which have been or may be organized in accordance with the provisions of the Railway Labor Act," and their subsidiary organizations). 

These "employee representatives" of railway labor organizations which are not employers under the acts establish their right to inclusion under the Retirement and Unemployment Insurance Acts by showing that they are officers or official representatives duly authorized and designated to represent employees in accordance with the Railway Labor Act, and by further showing that prior to being so authorized and designated, they were in the service of an "employer," within the meaning of the Retirement Act. 

Taxes Under Carriers' Taxing Act of 1937. Both employees and employers as defined in the Carriers' Taxing Act and the Railroad Retirement Act are taxed by the provisions of the former act. As in the old-age insurance taxes (see Chapter I), that imposed on employees is an income tax while that imposed on employers is denominated an excise tax. The rates are higher than under the old-age insurance taxes and are imposed on a different basis. Instead of being taxed on the first $3,000 earned from each employer in a calendar year, the railroad tax to provide annuities is based on the first $300 paid to the employee by all employers in any calendar month after December 31, 1936. Each employer may deduct the employee's share of the tax and pay his own tax based on that part of the $300 compensation which his total payment to the employee with respect to each calendar month bears to the aggregate compensation paid such employee by all employers with respect to that calendar month. For example, A performs services during September, 1938, for employers X and Y and is paid for the

month a salary of $200 by X and a salary of $300 by Y, or an aggregate compensation of $500 for the month. In such case X pays two-fifths of A's aggregate compensation, and therefore is liable for the employer's tax with respect to two-fifths of $300, or $120, and Y, having paid three-fifths of A's aggregate compensation, is liable for the employer's tax with respect to that portion of $300, or $180. Each must deduct from A's salary the employee's tax based on the same amount on which his tax is fixed. 

The employers' and the employees' taxes are as follows:

Calendar years 1940, 1941, 1942

Calendar year 1949 and subsequent years.……... 34 There is also a tax imposed on employees of railway labor organizations which are not themselves employers under the railroad acts. This tax, too, is based on so much of the compensation of the employee representative as is not in excess of $300 for any calendar month, earned by him after December 31, 1936. The rates are as follows:
