Calendar years 1946, 1947, 1948 62 

Calendar year 1949 and subsequent years...... 72 The taxes and annuities, as will be explained further on, are based on compensation earned in an employment relationship. 

It becomes important therefore, to expand on what is meant by the term "compensation." It means all remuneration in money, or in something which may be used in lieu of money (scrip or merchandise orders), which is earned by an individual for services performed as an employee to one or more employers, or as an employee representative. It is not confined to amounts earned or paid for active service, but includes amounts earned or paid for periods during which the employee or employee representative is absent from active service. The term does not include tips or the voluntary payment by an employer of the employee's tax, without the deduction of such tax from the remuneration of the employee. The name by which the remuneration is designated, the amount and the basis upon which it is paid, are immaterial.

Included as compensation are sick pay, vacation allowances, or back pay upon reinstatement after wrongful discharge; allowances for travelling or other expenses incurred in the business of the employer to the extent of the excess of such amount, if any, over the expenses actually incurred and accounted for by the employee; and certain types of premiums on life insurance covering the life of the employee, or payments made by the employer into a bonus or profit-sharing fund if such payments inure to the exclusive benefit of the employee. 

The employees' and employers' taxes for railroad annuities are based on compensation which has been paid. Compensation is earned when and as an employee performs services for which he is paid or for which there is a present or future obligation to pay, regardless of when the payment will actually be made. Remuneration paid for any period of absence from active service is deemed to have been earned in the month in which the absence occurred. 

The taxes are due on a wages paid basis, that is, for the quarter in which the compensation was actually or constructively paid. 

There is, however, one exception which is also found in the oldage insurance taxes, that is, compensation is deemed to have been paid within the period for which a return of tax is required to be made if the compensation was earned during that period and is payable during the calendar month following such period. Thus, if wages are earned in the last half of September but are not paid until the middle of October, they are included in the report for the period ending September 30, which report becomes due on 

October 31.

Accompanying the payment of tax is the tax return which is now filed quarterly in triplicate during the month succeeding each calendar quarter. The tax return is form CT-1 for employers and form CT-2 for employee representatives. Each employer and employee representative is required to file his own return. Parent and subsidiary corporations are not permitted to file consolidated returns. Returns on form CT-1 should be signed and verified under oath on affirmation by the individual, if the employer is an individual; by the president, vice president or other principal officer, if the employer is a corporation; or a responsible and duly authorized member or officer having knowledge of its affairs, if the employer is a partnership or other unincorporated organization. Returns on form CT-2 should be signed and certified under oath by the employee representative. The returns should be filed with the Collector of Internal Revenue for the district in which the employer's principal place of business is located, or in the case of employee representatives, with the collector for the district in which is located his legal residence or principal place of business.

Retirement Annuities. Unlike the old-age insurance annuities, there is no waiting period before railroad annuities can begin. An employee's annuity under the Railroad Retirement Act may be determined by what he has earned previous to the date the act went into effect, and not alone on what he earns after that date. 

To be eligible for an annuity an individual, in addition to other qualifications to be listed later, must have been an employee on the enactment date of the Railroad Retirement Act of 1935 

(August 29, 1935) or, if not an employee on that date, he must thereafter have become an employee and rendered services to an employer, or as an employee representative, after December 31, 1936. An individual who has satisfied the above conditions must furthermore fall in one of the following four classes: (1) he must have attained the age of sixty-five years or more, and he must have ceased to render service for compensation to any person or company, whether or not an employer under the act; or (2) he must be between sixty and sixty-five years of age; have completed at least thirty years of service; and ceased to render any services for compensation; or (3) he must be between sixty and sixty-five years of age; be totally and permanently disabled for regular employment or hire; and ceased to render any services for compensation; or (4) he must have completed at least thirty years of service; be totally and permanently disabled for regular employment or hire; and ceased to render any services for compensation.

For those individuals who are eligible for annuities because they fall in class 2 or 4, it becomes important to define a “year of service," since thirty of such "years" must have elapsed to establish their eligibility. Service which is counted for this purpose consists of time devoted to active service as an employee for compensation or time with respect to which remuneration is paid for time lost as an employee. Any calendar month in which an individual renders service as so defined is a "month of service," irrespective of the actual length of the service or of the amount of remuneration for lost time. Twelve such months, consecutive or otherwise, constitute a "year of service." In totalling the service of an employee, an ultimate fraction of six months or more is deemed to constitute a year of service, and an ultimate fraction of less than six months is taken at its actual value. Thus, in order to qualify for thirty years of service, an individual must have accumulated at least 354 "months of service."

Months of service to be creditable toward eligibility for an annuity must consist of service for a present employer, or to any person or company other than an employer if the service rendered before August 29, 1935, was for a present employer even though such person or company was not an employer when the services were rendered. Service rendered to any person or company which was at some time during its existence an express company, sleeping-car company, or carrier by railroad, and which was a predecessor of a company subject to the Railroad Retirement Act of 1935, also is creditable even though such person or company was not an employer at the time the service was rendered. 

Service is not creditable to employees who have attained age 

65 unless it was rendered prior to July 1, 1937. However, for services rendered prior to January 1, 1937, to be creditable the individual must have been an employee on August 29, 1935. If he was an employee on that date, the services he performed prior to January 1, 1937, are creditable to the extent necessary to bring the services rendered after that date up to the necessary thirty years. Once an annuity has begun, services rendered as an employee are not creditable except that if the annuity was granted on the basis of total and permanent disability and the annuity ceased because of recovery, then service between the cessation of the annuity and the month during which age 65 is attained may be credited toward any other annuity to which the individual may become entitled. 

All service claimed to be creditable must be verified to the satis faction of the Railroad Retirement Board. 

The computation of the annuity is dependent on two factors: the individual's "years of service" and his "monthly compensation." Compensation is the amount an individual earns for services as an employee, regardless of whether it is paid in money or in the form of some commodity, value, or service other than money. It does not include tips earned by the employee, but does include payments made by the employer for time lost, if an employment relationship existed between the parties. No amount can be credited as compensation unless it was received or earned for creditable service as previously defined. And in any event, compensation in excess of $300 cannot be credited for any one month of service.

The "monthly compensation" of an individual is computed by totalling the compensation (up to $300 in any one month) earned by him in his "years of service," and dividing that sum by the number of months in his "years of service," except that for service prior to January 1, 1937, included in his "years of service" the "monthly compensation" is the arithmetic mean of the monthly compensation earned in calendar months included in his "years of service" between 1924 and 1931. Where the "years of service" does not include any service in the period 1924-1931, or where such

service in the judgment of the board is insufficient to constitute a fair and equitable basis for determining the "monthly compensation" for service prior to January 1, 1937, the board is empowered to determine the "monthly compensation" for such service in such manner as is most just and equitable. 

The amount of the annuity is computed by multiplying an individual's "years of service" by the following percentages of his "monthly compensation": 2 per cent of the first $50; 12 per cent of the next $100; and 1 per cent of the next $150. The amount of the annuity cannot exceed $120 per month when it is based in whole or in part upon service rendered prior to January 1, 1937.

For service wholly rendered after that date there is no maximum limit on the amount of the annuity. For an individual who is an employee under the act at age 65 and who has at least 234 months of creditable service, the minimum annuity is as follows: (1) If the "monthly compensation" is $50 or more, the minimum annuity is $40; (2) if it is between $25 and $50, the minimum is 80 per cent of the "monthly compensation"; (3) if it is between $20 and $25, the minimum is $20; and (4) if it is less than $20, the annuity is the full amount of the "monthly compensation." But in no event may the amount of the annuity be less than the value of the additional old-age benefit that the individual would receive under Title II of the Social Security Act if his services as an employee after December 31, 1936, were included under that act. 

In three types of situation an annuity previously computed may be reduced. First, where an individual between age 60 and 65 is receiving an annuity because of 30 years of service or because of total and permanent disability, the amount of the annuity shall be reduced by one one-hundred and eightieth for each calendar month during all of which the individual is less than 65 years of age when the annuity begins to accrue. Second, where an individual who has been paid an annuity based on thirty years of service and total, permanent disability, and then is granted an annuity based on age 65 or age 60 to 65 and service, the amount of the latter annuity shall be reduced on an actuarial basis fixed by the board, in order to compensate for the disability annuity previously paid. Third, where an individual has been permitted to elect to receive a joint and survivor annuity, payable to himself for life and then to his spouse for life, the amounts of both annuities shall be such that their combined actuarial value as fixed by the board shall be the same as the actuarial value of the annuity if computed for the individual alone.

An individual may elect to receive a joint and survivor annuity for himself and spouse before January 1, 1938, if his annuity had not yet begun to accrue, or at least five years before the date of its accrual. He will then receive a reduced annuity to himself

for life and an annuity after his death to his spouse during life equal to 50 or 75 per cent of his reduced annuity.

It is the policy of both the old-age insurance provisions of the Social Security Act and of the Railroad Retirement Act to provide for death benefits when individuals die before their annuities have begun to accrue or when they have not drawn out in annuities a sum equivalent to a certain percentage of their taxable compensation. Under the old-age insurance provisions the percentage is 32 per cent, while under the Railroad Retirement Act it is 4 per cent. Although retirement annuities may be based on compensation earned in years prior to 1937, the death benefits are based only on the compensation earned after December 31, 1936. 

For individuals who die after they have begun to receive annuities, and who leave no spouse entitled to a survivor annuity, there will be paid to their estates the difference between what they had already received and 4 per cent of their taxable compensation earned after December 31, 1936. 

Unemployment Insurance. The Railroad Unemployment Insurance Act was passed in June, 1938, to create a national pooledfund system of unemployment insurance for railroad workers. Previous to this date, railroad workers were covered for this purpose by the state unemployment compensation acts, which was unsatisfactory inasmuch as the overlapping jurisdictions of the state agencies involved created major difficulties both in determining where contributions should be paid on such workers and where they should apply for benefits.

The Railroad Unemployment Insurance Act differs from the Railroad Retirement Act in that it provides not only a system for paying benefits but also for the collection of the tax necessary to finance this program. It thereby unifies the two related programs more efficiently than does the retirement program where the collection of the tax is done through the Carriers' Taxing Act administered by the Bureau of Internal Revenue and the payment of benefits is administered by the Railroad Retirement Board. The unemployment insurance program is entirely administered by the 

Railroad Retirement Board. 

The unemployment act withdraws the employers and employees it covers from the coverage of Title IX of the Social Security Act and the state unemployment compensation acts as of July 1, 1939.

Up to that date railroads who are liable employers under the state unemployment programs must continue to pay contributions thereunder. The coverage of employers and employees is for all practical purposes identical with that of the Railroad Retirement Act and the Carriers' Taxing Act. (See the preceding sections of this chapter as to the coverage provisions of these acts.)

The unemployment insurance system established by the act will be supported by a tax of 3 per cent of the compensation payable by employers to employees, excluding any amount in excess of $300 per month payable to any employee. This tax is borne by the employer alone, just as is the tax under Title IX of the Social 

Security Act and most state unemployment compensation acts. 

The limitation of the tax only to the first $300 per month payable to any employee is similar to the limitation in the Carriers' Taxing Act. Similarly, the items included in the tax base, such as pay for time lost, and the items excluded, such as tips, are the same as in the Carriers' Taxing Act. 

The Railroad Unemployment Insurance Act has adopted the device made common in the unemployment compensation provisions of the Social Security Act by providing that all taxes collected shall go into a special fund, 90 per cent of which will be used exclusively for the payment of benefits while the remainder is to be used for administration. After 1946, if the amounts credited to the administration fund exceed the amounts expended from the fund, such part of the excess as the Railroad Retirement Board deems proper may be transferred to the account from which benefits are payable.

An employee must meet two conditions to qualify for benefits. During his base year he must have earned at least $150 in covered employment, and within six months prior to the beginning of his benefit year he must have had a waiting period of a half month of total unemployment or two half-months which need not be consecutive, in each of which he had at least eight days of unemployment for which benefits were not paid. An individual's benefit year is the 52-week consecutive period after he first qualifies for benefits. His base year, the period during which he must have earned the minimum qualifying amount, is the last completed calendar year preceding a benefit year if the latter begins on or after 

July 1 of any year; and if the benefit year begins before July 1, the base year is the next to the last completed calendar year. 

There are a number of conditions which disqualify an employee from receiving benefits with respect to periods of varying duration. Included among these are leaving suitable work voluntarily without good cause; being discharged or suspended for misconduct related to his work; the right to receive pay in lieu of notice, annuities or pensions under the Railroad Retirement Act, or old-age benefits under Title II of the Social Security Act or any other act of Congress; receipt of unemployment benefits pursuant to a state or federal unemployment compensation system other than the one established by the Railroad Unemployment Insurance Act; and making any false or fraudulent claim for benefits. An employee whose compensation is determined on a mileage basis will also be disqualified from receiving benefits for any month during

which he performs 50 per cent of the maximum monthly employment allowable to him under his contract of employment, and during any half-month in which he performs 25 per cent of such maximum employment.

An additional disqualification is provided if the unemployment is due to a stoppage of work because of a strike at the employee's last employment, if such strike was commenced in violation of the Railway Labor Act or the rules of his labor organization, unless the employee is not participating in the strike and does not belong to a class of workers any of whom are participating in or are directly interested in the dispute. 

The amount of benefits that will be paid to an employee during any half-month is determined by multiplying rates varying from $1.75 to $3.00 by the number of days of unemployment in excess of seven during the half-month period. The rate between $1.75 and $3.00 to be applicable to an employee during his benefit year varies with the amount of his remuneration from covered employment (employment for an employer subject to the act) during his base year. The wage base used in determining such remuneration is the same as the base used for the measurement of the tax. 

The maximum amount of benefits payable to an individual during his base year is eighty times his daily benefit rate. 

The following is a table of the benefit rates and the maximum benefits during a benefit year applicable to the various compensation classes:

Total Compensation During Base Year Daily Benefit

The table of benefit rates and the provisions governing maximum benefits are not applicable to part-time workers. The benefit rate and maximum benefits to which such employees are entitled are to be prescribed by the Railroad Retirement Board in special regulations.

The board is also authorized to prescribe regulations concerning claims for benefits. Provision is made for appeals within the board from determinations with respect to claims, including provision for a number of three member district boards.

Unlike the state unemployment compensation acts, the Railroad Unemployment Act goes into operation with respect to the payment of benefits and the collection of contributions on the

same date, namely, July 1, 1939. The act expressly provides that unless specifically authorized, no state may legislate or attempt to cover the employers or employees who are in the federal railroad unemployment system. Certain changes necessarily result from the transfer of employers and employees to a new act. The federal act, for example, contains no provision for merit rating such as many of the state unemployment compensation acts do. To provide for the necessary funds to pay benefits immediately on the effective date of the act, Congress provided that the states should turn over to the Railroad Retirement Board that portion of the money in their unemployment trust funds which was raised by the taxation of employers now subject to the Railroad Unemployment Insurance Act. Further transitional provisions to meet any hardships that might otherwise arise from the simultaneous assumption of coverage by the board and exclusion from the state unemployment systems deal with employees' benefit rights. An employee who on 

July 1, 1939, is drawing benefits under a state unemployment compensation act will retain the same benefit year under the new act as he had under the old, and will not be required to serve a new waiting period before he can draw initial benefits under the federal system. Further, any covered employee who on July 1, 1939, is in the middle of a benefit year under a state act and who, solely by reason of the enactment of the federal act, becomes ineligible to receive benefits under a state law for unemployment after that date shall be deemed to have earned sufficient compensation to qualify him for benefits under the federal act, but the benefits payable during his benefit year may not exceed the maximum he could have received under the state act.

CHAPTER III  Title IX of the Social Security Act levies an excise tax on employers of eight or more individuals in the United States. Although commonly called the federal unemployment tax, it is not technically a tax for that purpose. Its proceeds are not used to pay unemployment benefits but go into the Treasury as internal revenue receipts to be used for all the ordinary purposes of government. It differs therein from the old-age insurance taxes levied by Title VIII of the Social Security Act, from the two railroad taxes, and from state unemployment compensation contributions, which are all raised for a specific purpose either to pay retirement annuities or unemployment benefits.

The tax under Title IX is part of a federal-state relationship whereby, through a tax credit device to be explained later, the states raise funds to pay unemployment compensation, and the federal government through the Social Security Board pays the costs of administering the states' programs. Although the tax under 

Title IX is a federal tax, administered by the Commissioner of Internal Revenue, it can be fully understood only when its relationship to the whole program of unemployment compensation is realized. This will be made clear in the present and succeeding chapter. 

The tax under Title IX is imposed as an excise tax in addition to all other federal taxes, on the privilege of hiring individuals. 

It is imposed only on employers of eight or more individuals, and no part of the tax can be raised through the assessment of employees. It is deductible for federal and state income tax purposes under the general provisions for deduction of certain items of business expense, and taxes of the same or other jurisdictions. The constitutionality of this tax, together with the other provisions of Title IX of the Social Security Act, was upheld by the United States 

Supreme Court in the case of Steward Machine Co. v. Davis (1937).

The rates of the federal unemployment compensation tax applicable to wages payable for employment in a respective calendar year are as follows: 33

Year Rate of Tax 

1936 1937 ..1% .2% 

1938 and any subsequent calendar year .3%

The rate is determined by the calendar year in which the services are performed for which wages are payable. The tax must be paid for the taxable year in which the wages are paid or payable, even though the rate of tax may be less than the rate for the current year, because the services were performed in a prior year. It is unusual, however, for wages to become payable in a year subsequent to the one in which the services were performed. Regulations 

90 under Title IX make this point clear, and the rulings of the bureau are all in accord that wages are payable whenever an obligation to pay for services arises, even though the time and actual amount of payment are unknown, and are to be determined in the future.

Article 201 of Regulations 90 provides as follows regarding the measure of the tax:

Wages are payable within the meaning of the act and these regulations (1) if there is an obligation at any time to pay wages with respect to employment during the calendar year, or (2) if, at any time, wages are actually paid with respect to employment during the calendar year. It is immaterial whether such wages are certain in amount at any time within the calendar year, and whether the right exists to enforce the payment of such wages at any time within the calendar year.

A distinction must be made between payments for services performed over a number of prior years and payments made for services performed in the taxable year limited, however, to individuals who have been in employment over a certain prior period of time. Thus, as an illustration of the first alternative, a cash bonus payable to employees for the past ten years' services is taxable only for the amount representing remuneration for those years from 1936 and thereon. However, a bonus based on the current taxable year's service but limited to employees of ten years standing is taxed at the rate in effect for the current taxable year, and the entire amount is taxable even though some of the ten years' service establishing the right to the bonus was performed in years prior to 1936.

Another problem arises from the fact that many employers pay their salesmen renewal or deferred commissions. Since the

federal unemployment tax is on a wages payable basis, there is no liability for tax where the services on which the commissions are currently being paid were performed prior to 1936. For example, an employer has an employee whose job it is to locate favorable sites for stores which the employer then rents for his retail operations. The employee's remuneration is a certain percentage of the gross sales in that store for a number of years, which remuneration is paid at the end of each year. After he has once located the site for the store which the employer uses, the employee has no further duties in connection with that store and is entitled to his wages for the agreed-upon number of years. The remuneration which is paid in 1936 and subsequently for stores located prior to 1936 is not taxable under either the old-age benefits or the unemployment tax. However, for the work done in 1936 and subsequently, there is an obligation to pay certain amounts to be determined in the future, that is, the wages are payable as of the date of the performance of the services. They are, therefore, under the federal regulations, taxable for that year. Since the amounts eventually to be paid are not accurately known, the employer must estimate what will be paid for those services in all subsequent years and must pay the unemployment tax thereon at the end of the taxable year in which the services were performed, at the rate of tax applicable for that year.

If such amounts are not estimated, the employer runs the danger of having to pay interest on such amounts which were payable in one taxable year but not actually paid until some subsequent time. The interest is an important factor, but there is another reason why estimates of future payments should be carefully made and the tax thereon paid in the correct year. Credit against the federal unemployment tax is allowed up to 90 per cent of such tax, for payments made to an approved state unemployment compensation fund. Such credit is allowed only for state contributions paid with respect to employment for the current year. Therefore, if amounts will subsequently be due for services performed in one year and an estimate of the future payments is not made until some subsequent time, the employer will not be entitled to federal credit for the contributions he pays to the state unemployment compensation fund because such payments were not made in due time under the federal requirements. Payments to a state fund to be entitled to federal credit must be made before the due date of filing the federal return, and if any amounts which are payable under the federal Act are not included in the basis for paying the state contributions, no credit will be allowed on such amounts when they are eventually paid. 

If the employer overestimates the amount of remuneration which is eventually paid, he is entitled to a refund or credit against his federal tax. In the event of an underestimate of such amounts,

the employer must file supplementary returns for the year in which the services were performed and pay the tax based on the amount of wages found to be correct. In the event of an underestimate the employer is likely to lose his federal credit in such sum since he will probably pay the state unemployment fund on the same basis that he has used for the federal tax. If an overestimate is made the employer has four years after the date of payment within which to file a claim for refund with the federal government, thereby giving him ample opportunity in which he may determine the true amount of wages payable for each taxable year. However, many state unemployment compensation acts provide a shorter period in which claims for refund or adjustment may be filed, thereby making it doubly important for the employer to attempt to estimate as closely as possible the amount of wages which he will have to pay in the future for services performed in the current year.

In the case of other types of salesmen who are paid renewal or deferred commissions, there is the same necessity to estimate as accurately as possible how much will be paid for services rendered in a given year. If under the contract of hire the salesman is obligated to render further services before he receives such renewal or deferred commissions, such payments can be construed as payments for the further services rendered instead of for the original work in procuring the sale. Thus, those insurance salesmen who are admitted to be employees may receive commissions, based on the procuring of a contract of insurance, for a number of years provided the premium is kept up. If the contract provides for nothing more, the employer is obligated, under the federal rulings, to estimate the total amount of such deferred commissions and report them for the year in which the contract of insurance was procured. But if the salesman is required to collect future premiums or service the contract of insurance in some way, the bureau has ruled that the deferred commissions will be treated as payment for such additional services, and will therefore be payable in the year in which the services were performed instead of in the year in which the contract was first procured. It is important to note that there is no requirement that these further services be at all commensurate in value with the commission paid. Evidently it is sufficient for the bureau that there be some services to which the deferred commission can be related. Where an employee is entitled to deferred commissions even after he is separated from his employment, there is no opportunity for the performance of such further services and the question arises as to when commissions of this sort are payable. Although this question has not been specifically answered by the bureau, it seems reasonably clear that the bureau will interpret all such deferred commissions as being payable in the year in which the original services were performed, since

there is no obligation on the employee to do any work in the future in order to be entitled to the receipt of such renewal commissions. 

A salesman who is paid a commission on a sale made in one year which is cancelled in a subsequent year is considered to have earned not the amount originally reported for the first year but only those commissions for sales which remain in effect. Even though the employer may deduct the commission on the cancelled sale in a year subsequent to that when the sale was made, he must report as the salesman's wages the full amount of all commissions on sales earned that year, and can take credit for the reduction in the salesman's salary only by filing a claim for refund of the tax he paid on the commission for the earlier year. 

Persons Liable for the Tax. The Act defines an employer as any person who has in employment at least eight persons on some twenty days, each day being in a different calendar week, during a calendar year. It is not necessary that the same persons be employed on each such day or that they be working at the same moment of time. The weeks need not be consecutive during the year nor need the day on which eight people are in employment be the same in each week. Since it is the employment relationship which determines what individuals are counted, it is not even necessary that the individual be performing services on a certain day in order to be counted. Thus, individuals who are on vacation or temporarily ill and away from work are considered to be in employment and must be counted for the purpose of determining whether there are eight persons employed on that day. This is true even though the employee is not paid for the days he is not present. Liability under the federal Act is determined on the basis of each calendar year, and an employer who is liable in one year may, by reducing the number of his employees, escape liability for the succeeding year. 

It should be remembered, however, that under most state unemployment compensation acts an employer who is liable for one year remains subject for the following year, and in many cases he remains subject until he has had an application for termination of coverage approved by the state agency.

Under the federal Act, once an employer becomes liable for the unemployment tax he must pay on all his employees for the entire year. All employees are covered even though individual employees have not worked twenty weeks or even twenty days. Thus, casual, part-time, and borrowed employees must be included and a tax paid on their wages.

Under the federal unemployment tax each legal entity which employs eight or more persons on some day in each of twenty weeks in a calendar year is subject to the tax. Problems frequently arise as to when a change has been effected in a legal entity and whether two or more separate establishments comprise

one or two legal entities. The fact that a business is being conducted at more than one site does not mean that the liability for the tax of each such establishment is based on its own experience. 

If the two establishments are being conducted by the same individual, partnership, or corporation, the employment experience of the two is combined in order to determine whether the unit is liable for the tax. Thus a sole proprietor may have a grocery store in one town and a filling station in another. Yet if the two combined have employed the required number of individuals for the required length of time the individual is a subject employer. If, however, the same individual owned a grocery store as a sole proprietor and the filling station as one of the members of a partnership, each establishment would be liable only if it employed the required number of persons.

Whenever an entity changes its status it ceases to be an employer and its successor under the federal Act must acquire its own employment experience before it becomes subject to the Act. This differs from most state unemployment compensation acts, which provide that the successor to a liable employer automatically becomes a liable employer itself. The usual types of changes in status are those where a partnership becomes a corporation or vice versa; where a sole proprietor enters into a partnership or establishes a corporation; and where a corporation is dissolved, the business meanwhile being conducted in some other form. 

A partnership is automatically dissolved whenever one of the partners dies or otherwise terminates his relationship with the partnership. If the former partnership was a liable employer, the death of the partner means that the liable employer no longer exists, and that a final return must be filed with the Commissioner of Internal Revenue and the proper notice given to him of the fact of such termination. In no case is the succeeding entity liable for the unpaid taxes of its predecessor if the necessary statutory requirements have been complied with. 

Many small corporations have succeeded in reducing their number of employees without any substantial change in their method of doing business by dissolving the corporation and forming a partnership between the former officers or stockholders. Officers of the corporation are considered employees while partners are not, so that if the corporation had nine employees, including its three officers, it would have only six employees if the officers became partners of the new partnership. It must be noted, however, that the designation of an individual as a partner is not sufficient to make him one if all the other indicia point to the fact that his relationship has not been changed. For example, if former employees are made partners it must be shown that they are partners in fact as well as name, that is, that they have con-

tributed something to the partnership assets, are treated as having an equal voice in the management of the establishment, and are liable personally for its debts.

In certain professions, such as the law and in stock brokerage firms, it is common to designate certain individuals as junior partners to distinguish them from the founders or principal interests engaged in the business. Usually these junior partners do not have the same rights as to distribution of profits that the senior partners have, but they are frequently permitted to act as agents for the firm. The Bureau of Internal Revenue has not as yet ruled directly as to whether junior partners of the type described are employees and therefore to be counted in determining whether the firm employs eight or more individuals, or whether they are employers. If they were actual partners, it would be necessary that the addition of a new member or the dropping out of an old should effect a change in the entity, thereby necessitating that the old firm file a final return and that the new firm make application for a new identification number on form SS-4. 

Even though there may be an outward change in the manner of doing business, there may be no change in the legal entity for purposes of unemployment taxes. An individual who is a sole proprietor may leave a business which on his death is continued by an administrator. This is not considered to be a change in the legal status of the establishment, and the administrator is considered to be acting on behalf of the estate. Whatever remuneration the administrator may draw for services connected with the estate is regarded as if it were the drawings of an individual proprietor, and would therefore not be taxable remuneration. If, however, the administrator or executor employed an individual and delegated to him his official duties on behalf of the estate, that individual would be an employee of the administrator and not of the estate.

Many types of changes are frequent in a corporation status. 

If the corporation is dissolved in one state and immediately incorporates in another state, it is regarded as a new employer, since the corporation is a creature of the state in which it was incorporated and has no existence elsewhere unless it satisfies the requirements of other states in which it seeks to do business. As a practical matter, there may be no change at all in the manner of operation of the corporation's business, and yet the new corporation will be a separate employer and must accumulate the necessary twenty weeks' experience before it is required to pay the federal unemployment tax. This has led to a frequent misunderstanding in regard to merit-rating provisions of state unemployment compensation acts where an employer may have the benefit of a lower rate if he succeeds in achieving a certain ratio between the contributions he has paid in, the benefits paid out, and his an-

nual payroll. Those states which have merit-rating plans keep separate accounts for each employer, and are not obligated to transfer the merit-rating accounts of one employer to another even though the successor may be merely a continuation of the predecessor. The question of the effect of changes in status on meritrating accounts will be more fully discussed in Chapter IV. 

A corporation may be dissolved voluntarily, or involuntarily for nonpayment of franchise taxes. Questions have arisen as to the status of a former corporation whose charter has been dissolved involuntarily but which has continued doing business in exactly the same way as before. The former officers of the corporation either continue to perform the same functions as before or else they may give themselves a new title as members of an association. Under the federal Act, if there is no outward change in the manner of doing business, it has been ruled that the former corporation continues as an association and that the former officers who were employees of the corporation are now members of the association. Under the federal Act, associations are included in the definition of corporations and, therefore, a finding that a certain business is being conducted as an association necessarily implies that the officers of the association are still employees in view of the definition of employee as including an officer of a corporation. Under the state unemployment compensation acts, however, none of which by their terms define a corporation as including associations, it is probable that the former officers or active stockholders may, after the dissolution of the corporation, be considered as partners instead of officers of the association. As partners, their remuneration is not taxable and they are considered as employers and not employees in determining whether the employing unit has sufficient workers to make it a liable employer under that state's act. There are few, if any, decisions or rulings directly on this point, and the question remains open in any state as to whether their ruling will follow that of the federal government in considering the former officers of the corporation as employees or as employers.

In the event of the bankruptcy of an employing unit, the trustee or receiver is considered to be the same legal entity as his predecessor and therefore no change in reporting is required except that information as to the change must be submitted to the Commissioner of Internal Revenue. Further, when a corporation is reorganized pursuant to section 77 (b) of the former Bankruptcy Act and is being operated by a trustee, the corporation continues as before for taxing and reporting purposes. Thus after reorganization the corporation does not have to acquire twenty weeks of experience, but is subject to the tax immediately on the discharge of the trustee.

An assignee of rent or a mortgagee in possession under an assignment of rent is a different legal entity than his predecessor, and becomes liable not on the basis of his predecessor's experience but on the basis of the employment experience he has himself acquired. If an individual takes over a building under an assignment of rentals, he must include as his employees all the employees on that building, and if in addition he has another business where he employs people, the employment experience of the two units will be combined in order to determine whether he has employed eight or more people on some day in each of twenty different weeks.

The problem of which employing unit is subject to the unemployment tax in the case of the merger or consolidation of two or more corporations depends on what, if any, new legal entities have been created. If the A Corporation, a liable employer, merges or consolidates with the B Corporation, the A Corporation will remain a liable employer if it has retained its corporate existence and merely absorbs the B Corporation even though the name of the A Corporation has been changed. But if the A and B 

Corporations both give up their corporate identity and merge into a third corporation, the latter will be the liable employer on the expiration of the necessary length of time even though its name may be that of one of the merged corporations. In other words, it is important to determine what had been accomplished by the dissolution and merger in order to fix the liability of all concerned. 

It is frequently difficult to determine which of two possible employers is responsible for the tax on a certain individual's wages. The worker may work for both employing units, in which case it may be difficult to determine whether certain services are being performed in the employment relationship or as an independent contractor or it may be difficult to determine for whom the services are being performed. Since the definition of employment in most state unemployment compensation acts differs from that under the federal Act, the following discussion is applicable only to the latter Act. The problem will again be taken up as far as the state acts are concerned in the chapter on the employment relationship. The federal Act and regulations adopt the commonlaw rules as to the existence of an employment relationship and depend on those rules to aid in the determination of which of two possible employers is the one liable for the tax on an individual's wages. The rule as most commonly stated is that he is the employer who has the right to control not only what work shall be done but the manner in which that work is to be performed, that is, he must have the right to control the means by which the worker is to perform his given task. This determination is a difficult one in many cases, when an employee is loaned from one employer to another or when it appears that the services being performed are

performed for both employers simultaneously. There is, of course, no doubt that an individual may be working for two employers at the same time. This is the common situation where a worker has two or more jobs. However, where a worker is performing services which benefit two employers simultaneously, the question often arises as to whether he is working for one or for both. Illustrations are common and a few will be given here. An employee working for two affiliated corporations is considered an employee of both if part of his remuneration is borne by both. It is not, however, necessary that he be paid by each corporation if it can, as a matter of fact, be determined that the services he performs are divisible as between the two employers. In such a case, he will be counted as an employee of both corporations even though his remuneration is paid by one alone. Demonstrators for products which are sold in department stores may be employees of the manufacturer or the department store, or of both, depending on the method of the remuneration and on who has the right to control the manner of the performance of the services. Where the manufacturer hires the demonstrator, pays his salary, and controls his activities, he is the employee only of the manufacturer, but where he is hired and controlled by the store but performs additional services for the manufacturer for which he is paid, he would be considered the employee of both. 

Department stores frequently lease out certain sections to lessees, who buy on their own account, are permitted to hire and fire employees, and are required to pay to the store either a flat rental or a percentage of their sales. If the lessee has eight or more employees, he will himself be an employer subject to the federal tax, and his employees will not be considered employees of the store even though they are required to conform to certain general rules and regulations which the store may impose on all people working therein.

An unusual situation involving the determination of which of two possible employers will be subject for the federal unemployment tax has developed with respect to an agency which obligates itself to furnish to a number of department stores in a large city sufficient sales girls to take care of their peak loads. It was formerly the practice of these stores for each to do its own hiring of sales girls and to pay all payroll taxes thereon. The new agency offers to hire and pay the girls itself, keep all the necessary payroll information, and pay all the employment taxes on these girls. 

Even though the understanding between the department store and the agency is that the latter is to be the employer of the girls, it is clear that these sales girls are performing an essential service for the store, in that they are doing sales work which otherwise would admittedly be done by store employees. Since the store must in effect retain the right to control the manner in which the girls'

services are to be performed, it is undoubtedly correct to consider the sales girls as employees of the store even though their remuneration is paid in the first instance by the agency supplying the girls. Although not important from the federal unemployment tax point of view, since that tax is paid by the agency which thereby satisfies the requirement of the bureau that the employees be covered in one way or another, it is important in connection with merit rating under state unemployment compensation laws. If these girls were to be considered as employees of the agency, it would be easier for the department store, by ridding itself of the unstable part of its employment record, to achieve a lower rate of contributions. Because of this consideration, it is probable that state unemployment compensation agencies will regard such schemes with doubt and will be inclined to consider the sales girls as employees of the department store, disregarding the fact that the agency supplying the girls does hire and fire them and actually pay their wages.

Radio programs have been a prolific source of controversy in regard to the question of who has the ultimate right to control the activities of performers on such programs. There are usually three parties involved, the sponsor of the program, the broadcasting company or network, and the advertising agency which has developed or produced the program. Each of these may, and as a matter of fact does, exercise some control or has the right to exercise some control over the manner in which the radio artist is to perform. When the show is being produced for a certain sponsor alone, the bureau has uniformly held that the sponsor is the employer of the radio talent used on his show and is primarily liable for the payment of the tax. Where, however, a show is built up by a radio station which then sells it as a unit, or, as is more commonly known, as a "package program," it is the radio station and not the ultimate purchaser of the show that is considered as the employer.

The last example of a situation in which it is important to determine which of two employing units is liable for the payment of payroll taxes is in the case of real estate agents who manage property for others. It is common for a real estate agent to be given a large amount of authority as regards the hiring and firing of employees and the actual control of their services. Where the agent turns over the income of the building, minus all expenses and its commission, to the owner of the building, the bureau considers the control that the real estate agent exercises over the building's employees as a control which is being exercised on behalf of the owner. The bureau has therefore ruled that in such cases the owner of the building is the employer and that reports must be made out in his name. Where, however, the real estate agent assumes complete control over the managment of the property, that is, he guarantees

the payment of a certain sum to the owner and then proceeds to pay all expenses of the building in whatever manner he sees fit, being merely obligated to pay the agreed upon sum to the owner, it is the agent who is considered to be the employer. It is perhaps not correct to speak of the employer as a real estate agent in such cases, since he is not acting as agent for the owner but is running the building on his own behalf and for his own profit. 

With the guidance of this principle, that he who has the ultimate right to control the manner in which an employee's services are to be performed is the employer liable for the payroll taxes, it is usually not a difficult matter to determine which of two possible employers should report the wages of certain workers and bear the taxes imposed thereon.

Taxable Services. The federal unemployment tax is based on the employment relationship as that concept is known at common law. As stated previously and as will be explained in greater detail in Chapter VII, the relationship of employer and employee exists between two parties when one has the right to control the other in the manner of the performance of his services. Generally the relationship exists when the person for whom the services are performed has the right to control and direct the individual who performs the services not only as to the main object or result to be accomplished by the work but also as to the details and means through which that result is to be accomplished. An employee is subject to the will and control of the employer not only as to what shall be done but how it shall be done. It is not necessary that the employer actually direct or control the employee's services. In many skilled trades, the employee may know more about the manner in which his work is to be done than the employer, but it is still the employer who has the right to say that the work shall or shall not be done in a certain manner even though the method the employer selects may not be the most efficient or helpful. The right to discharge is also an important factor, indicating that the person possessing that right is an employer. Other factors which may be characteristic of an employer-employee relationship are that the services are to be performed on the premises of the employer and that he is to furnish the tools or material with which the employee is to work.

An individual may perform services for another outside of the employment relationship. For lack of a better term and because of the usage of history, the phrase "independent contractor" has been more or less indiscriminately applied to many situations where the right to control the methods of an individual's work is not present. The courts originally tended to the view that to be an independent contractor an individual must have a trade, business, or profession of his own, which was not dependent for its

sole support on the services to be performed for one individual. 

This, however, has been broadened so as to include within the term "independent contractor" many individuals whose means of livelihood are dependent on the good will or grace of others, but who because of the very nature of their services cannot be subject to intimate direction or control over the manner in which their services are to be performed. Thus salesmen, even though they may work for one company alone, are generally considered to be independent contractors under the federal rulings, if their earnings are dependent solely upon their own efforts and if the "employer" does not have the right to direct them as to the means they are to use in effectuating their general purpose, that of making sales. Those who follow an independent trade, business, or profession in which they offer their services to the public, such as physicians, lawyers, dentists, contractors, and public stenographers, are considered to be independent contractors and not employees. The fact that an individual belongs to one of these professions or trades does not of itself make him an independent contractor. For example, a lawyer or doctor whose services are performed solely for one company is in employment by that company because of the fact that he is not free to follow his profession as he sees fit.

Remuneration paid to independent contractors is not taxable under the federal Act. Another type of remuneration which is not taxable even though it may be paid for services performed in the employment relationship is wages paid for services outside the territorial jurisdiction conferred by Congress on the taxing authorities. The Social Security Act covers only services performed in any of the states, the District of Columbia, and the Territories of Alaska and Hawaii. The other possessions of the United States do not constitute part of the taxable area. 

An individual who performs all his services outside of the above named jurisdictions is not considered an employee for any of the purposes of the federal unemployment tax. He is not counted in determining whether his employer is a liable employer nor are his wages subject to the tax. If, however, he performs some part of his services in the United States (as that is defined in the Social Security Act) he is counted as an employee on those days in which he performs services therein and that part of his wages which is allocable to services performed here are taxable. 

In general, the basis to be used for the allocation of taxable services to the United States is that of time spent in performing services within its borders. This, however, is not always an accurate guide, as for example in the case of salesmen who sell both in Canada and the United States. In such situations, the proper basis of allocation, if the salesman is on a strictly commission basis, is the amount of commissions earned on sales made in the

United States, even though a greater or less proportion would be allocable on the basis of time spent in the two jurisdictions. 

The services performed within the United States by aliens are taxable. Regardless of where the contract for services may have been entered into, or what the citizenship or residence of either employer or employee is, those services which are performed in the United States are taxable if the employer is a liable employer, that is, if on twenty days, each day being in a different calendar week, he has had eight or more persons in employment in the United States.

The federal unemployment tax exempts certain types of services from the definition of employment even though under the common law they are performed in the employment relationship, and within the United States. The reasons for these exemptions are of various sorts, usually for administrative reasons, or because under the Constitution or generally accepted tradition the services cannot be covered by a federal statute. The following are the services which are exempt under the federal unemployment tax: (1) agricultural labor; (2) domestic service in a private home; (3) services performed as an officer or member of the crew of a vessel on the navigable waters of the United States; (4) services performed by an individual in the employ of his son, daughter, or spouse, and services performed by a child under the age of 21 in the employ of his father or mother; (5) services performed in the employ of the United States or of any instrumentality of the United States; (6) services performed in the employ of a state, a political subdivision thereof, or an instrumentality of one or more states or political subdivisions; (7) services performed in the employ of a corporation, community chest, fund or foundation, organized and operated exclusively for religious, charitable, scientific, literary, or educational purposes, or for the prevention of cruelty to children or animals, no part of the net earnings of which inures to the benefit of any private shareholder or individual; (8) services performed in the employ of an employer as defined in the railroad unemployment insurance act and services performed as an employee representative, as defined in said Act. See Chapter VI for a complete discussion as to the reasons for these exemptions and the situations to which they have been held to apply. 

Wages. The term "wages" means all remuneration for employment, whether payable in money or something other than money. The name by which such remuneration is designated is immaterial. Salaries, commissions on sales, fees, bonuses, gifts, and prizes are wages if paid or payable by an employer to his employees as compensation for taxable services. The basis upon which the remuneration is payable, the amount of the remuneration, or the time of payment are immaterial. It may be payable on the

basis of hours worked, units of work done, percentage of gross receipts, or difference between buying and selling price. It may be payable yearly, weekly, monthly, or on the completion of specific tasks. It may be payable in cash or in anything which has a money value, such as goods, board and lodging, notes, or other tangible or intangible property. In Chapter VII, we will take up the problem of what specific items have been held to be wages under both the federal and state unemployment acts.

Federal Credit System. Although by its terms, Title IX of the 

Social Security Act imposes a 3 per cent tax on payrolls of employers of eight or more in 1938 and all subsequent years, it was not the primary intent of Congress that this tax be collected in its entirety. Through the tax offset provisions of Title IX an employer who is subject to the federal tax may credit against such tax the amounts he has paid in as contributions to approved state unemployment compensation funds up to 90 per cent of the federal tax. 

The purpose of this type of provision was clearly to induce states to pass unemployment compensation legislation of an approved type, since employers in a state which does not have such an act would be forced to pay the entire amount to the federal government without insuring to their employees the rights to unemployment benefits which employees in states with approved acts would have. 

The efficacy of the tax credit device can be appreciated when it is remembered that all 51 state jurisdictions passed approved state unemployment compensation acts in less than two years after pas sage of the Social Security Act. 

Title IX of the Social Security Act does not set up a system of unemployment compensation. Such systems are left to the states to work out for themselves within the broad limits permitted in the Social Security Act listing the conditions on which the Social Security Board would base its approval. These conditions which all present state unemployment compensation acts satisfy are as follows: (1) All compensation is to be paid through public employment offices in the state or such other agencies as the 

Board may approve; (2) No compensation shall be payable with respect to any day of unemployment occurring within two years after the first day of the first period with respect to which contributions are required; (3) All money received in the unemployment fund shall immediately upon such receipt be paid over to the Secretary of the Treasury to the credit of the Unemployment Trust

Fund established by Section 904;

1 (4) All money withdrawn from the Unemployment Trust

Fund by the state agency shall be used solely in the payment of compensation, exclusive of expenses of administration; (5) Compensation shall not be denied in such state to any otherwise eligible individual for refusing to accept new work under any of the following conditions: (a) If the position offered is vacant due directly to a strike, lockout, or other labor dispute; (b) if the wages, hours, or other conditions of the work offered are substantially less favorable to the individual than those prevailing for similar work in the locality; (c) if as a condition of being employed the individual would be required to join a company union or to resign from or refrain from joining any bona fide labor organization; (6) All the rights, privileges, or immunities conferred by such law or by acts done pursuant thereto shall exist subject to the power of the legislature to amend or repeal such law at any time.

Title IX provides that credit against the federal unemployment tax will be allowed to those employers in states having approved laws who actually pay into a state unemployment fund contributions which are required under the state law. Since the federal tax is on a wages payable basis, which means that the tax must be paid based on wages payable even though not actually paid for services performed in a given year, it follows that the state act, to entitle employers within its boundaries to take advantage of the full federal offset, should also be on a wages payable basis. 

Both the state and federal taxes must be levied for the same taxable year, or credit for payment of the state tax will not be allowed as against the federal tax. The contributions must also have been paid for services performed during the calendar year covered by the return, and for services covered by the federal act. 

Numerous problems have arisen as to the conditions under which a taxpayer is entitled to federal credit, because of the strict wording of the federal Act. In the first place, the payment to the state fund must be a required payment. This means that under the state act the employer had to be liable for the contributions imposed by that act. In the case of employers who operate largely across state lines, this has created a host of difficulties in that uncertainties arose as to which was the proper state in which to pay contributions for certain services. Construction firms especially are faced with this problem. Employees of such firms may work in a large number of states during each calendar year, and the employer is often hard put to it to determine to what state the contributions based on such an individual's wages should be paid. The

employer runs the risk of losing his federal credit if it is subsequently determined that he has paid his contributions to the wrong state. There is usually no difficulty in recovering such payments from the state where the contributions were not required, but if payment is then made to the proper state it will not be entitled to federal credit if it is made after the due date of filing the federal unemployment tax return. In the first few years of administering their acts, many states were in the habit of accepting all payments without examining too closely into whether they were correctly made or not. When it was subsequently determined that the payments were made to the wrong state, usually by an employee in some other state claiming benefits from the account of his employer, it was in many cases too late to secure the federal tax offset and the employer in such cases had to pay not only the state in which contributions were required but also the federal government for the amount on which he was not entitled to credit.

Many of the states permit employers who have less than the required number of employees in their state to elect voluntarily to come under the provisions of the state act to the same extent as all other employers and thereby extend the right to unemployment benefits to their employees. If the employer has eight or more people throughout the United States, but less than eight in some state requiring that number for an employer to be liable, it does not cost the employer any additional amount if he voluntarily elects to cover his employees in that state. The Bureau of Internal Revenue has ruled that payments made under such voluntary elections to a state fund are required payments and are therefore entitled to federal credit. There is, however, another type of voluntary payment which is not entitled to credit against the federal tax. A few states have provisions permitting an employer to make voluntary payments to his account whenever he wishes. The purpose of such voluntary payments is to increase an employer's credit in his meritrating account, thereby enabling him to achieve a lower rate of contributions or to prevent a higher rate from being assessed against him. The bureau has held that payments of this sort, since they are not obligatory upon the employer and do not subject him to all the other liabilities of other subject employers in the state, are not payments which are required under the state act and can not therefore be used as part of the payment to a state fund which is entitled to federal credit.

The contributions to a state fund must be for wages payable for services which are not exempt under the federal tax. If a state act covers a type of service such as agricultural labor or permits the employer voluntarily to elect to cover such services, any payments made thereon to the state could not be used as a federal credit against the liability of the employer for the federal tax on his employees who are covered under the Title IX tax.

The contributions must be paid for services performed within the United States and any payments which an employer makes to a state fund for incidental services performed outside of the United 

States will not be entitled to credit.

Besides being a required payment under an approved state unemployment compensation law, the payment to the state must actually be made before the due date of filing the federal return which is January 31 succeeding the close of each taxable year, unless the time for filing such return has been extended by the local Collector of Internal Revenue. Employers who are delinquent under a state act lose their right to a federal credit if their payments are not made before the end of January after the year in which they are delinquent. In a few instances where it has been impossible for an employer to pay his state contributions on time because the property is being operated by a receiver or trustee who has been unable to get permission of the court under whose authority he is operating, to make the payment to the state, it has been held by a number of lower federal courts that the attempt of the federal government to collect its full tax is in the nature of a penalty and therefore not allowable as a claim against the bankrupt's estate. Since this decision is made simply because of the provisions of the Bankruptcy Act, it is unlikely that the federal courts will extend it to other situations, giving relief to employers who are subjected to a double liability because they have failed to pay their state contributions before the due date of filing the federal return. 

Additional Federal Credit Allowances. There is, in addition to the credit allowed against the federal unemployment tax for payment into state funds, another federal credit allowance making possible savings which may be achieved by virtue of a merit-rating under a state act. It would be useless for a state to allow an employer to pay at a rate lower than other employers in the state pay, if the employer were allowed to deduct from his federal tax only the actual amount he is required to pay into the state fund. 

However, if such lowered rate is allowed by a state under conditions satisfying Section 910 of the Social Security Act, a real saving can be effected by employers who have succeeded in stabilizing their employment and thereby become entitled to a lowered state contribution rate.

The two federal credits cannot exceed 90 per cent of the federal tax. Every employer therefore must pay to the federal government at least 10 per cent of his federal unemployment tax after he has availed himself of all his credit allowances. The following are the requirements of Section 910 of the Social Security Act which a state law must fulfill before any reduced rate it may grant is allowable for further federal credit: 

Sec. 910. (a) A taxpayer shall be allowed the additional credit under section 909, with respect to his contribution

rate under a state law being lower, for any taxable year, than that of another employer subject to such law, only if the board finds that under such law- (1) Such lower rate, with respect to contributions to a pooled fund, is permitted on the basis of not less than three years of compensation experience; (2) Such lower rate, with respect to contributions to a guaranteed employment account, is permitted only when his guaranty of employment was fulfilled in the preceding calendar year, and such guaranteed employment account amounts to not less than 72 percentum of the total wages payable by him, in accordance with such guaranty, with respect to employment in such state in the preceding calendar year; (3) Such lower rate, with respect to contributions to a separate reserve account, is permitted only when (A) compensation has been payable from such account throughout the preceding calendar year, and (B) such account amounts to not less than five times the largest amount of compensation paid from such account within any one of the three preceding calendar years, and (C) such account amounts to not less than 72 per centum of the total wages payable by him (plus the total wages payable by any other employers who may be contributing to such account) with respect to employment in such state in the preceding calendar year. 

If an employer makes contributions at a reduced rate to more than one state in any one calendar year, the additional credit allowable with respect to each state law must be computed separately and the total additional credit allowable against the tax is the aggregate of the additional credits allowed with respect to each state law. The Bureau of Internal Revenue has summarized the allowance of the additional credit in the following language: (1) In no case is a taxpayer entitled to an additional credit unless under the state law he is permitted to pay contributions for a calendar year or portion thereof at a rate which is lower than the highest rate applicable under such state law for the corresponding period. If the state law does not make provision for such a lower rate, no additional credit is available to the taxpayer, and he can take only the credit allowable under section 902 for contributions actually paid. (2) No additional credit shall be allowed with respect to calendar years prior to the calendar year 1938. (3) The aggregate of such additional credit and the credit under section 902 (see article 211) shall not exceed 90 per cent of the tax against which credit is taken.

(4) No additional credit may be taken with respect to contributions under a state law for any calendar year unless the Social Security Board has found that a lower rate with respect to such contributions is permitted in conformance with the conditions specified in section 910 (a) of the Act. 

The employer must submit the following proof before he will be allowed the additional credit under Section 909 of the Social Security Act: A certificate of the proper officer of each state (with respect to the laws of which the additional credit is claimed) showing, for the taxpayer who pays contributions to such state at a rate which is lower than the highest rate applicable under the laws of such state-(a) the total remuneration, with respect to which contributions were required to be paid by the taxpayer under the state law, for each period during the calendar year for which contributions are payable; (b) the highest rate of contributions applicable under the law of the state for each period for which contributions are payable during the calendar year; and (c) the rate applied to the taxpayer for each period for which contributions are payable during the calendar year. These provisions, while of immediate importance only in Wisconsin where a lowered rate may be possible in 1938, will become increasingly important as the states continue to pay benefits and employers in those states where meritrating is allowed succeed in stabilizing their employment. It will be noted that under a pooled fund type of law an employer must have three years of compensation experience, that is, three calendar years throughout which compensation was payable with respect to any individual in his employ who became unemployed and was eligible for compensation. In those states where transfers of meritrating accounts are not allowed, and at present, this constitutes the great majority of states, it seems that this requirement of three years of compensation experience requires that the employer to be entitled to additional credit allowances must have been the same legal entity to which the original employer's account was assigned. 

It is doubtful, for example, whether a corporation which dissolves in one state and incorporates in another even though there may be no change in management, will be entitled to tack on the years of compensation experience of its predecessor, since it is a new legal entity and therefore a new and different employer than the predecessor company.

For a discussion on how stabilization of employment may be attempted, see Chapter V of the Foundation Guide, Second Edition. 

Chapter VI of that edition is a general resumé of the administration of the federal unemployment tax and should be consulted in connection with such questions.

CHAPTER IV  

Although for most employers in this country the federal Social Security Act was their first contact with a broad program of taxation designed for social ends, the major interest of all employers has now shifted, because of more immediate contact, to the state unemployment compensation laws. Although the federal old-age benefits tax covers every employer of one or more in the United States, with certain exceptions, and the state unemployment compensation laws usually require a larger number of employees for their coverage, it will be to the latter laws that employers will increasingly turn their attention. It is to the states that the biggest portion of taxes for social security purposes goes; in many states it is the one phase of the social security program that rewards efficient management by lowered rates of taxes; and it is the field where most of the future problems as to employer-employee relationship, the definition of wages, and accounting will come to the fore. 

One of the main reasons why unemployment compensation legislation was delayed in the United States far beyond European activities in that regard was the fact that the United States has forty-eight separate jurisdictions, each of which has almost complete sovereignty over employers and employees within its jurisdiction. The fear that large employers would tend to leave a state which imposed an additional tax burden on its inhabitants through passage of an unemployment compensation law was the main deterrent to such action by the states. The second decade of this century saw the introduction of numerous proposals to establish state-wide systems of unemployment compensation benefits, but none of them ever stood much chance of enactment.
