It was not until the federal government took the initiative in promulgating a policy of broad social action that the states seriously considered the matter of unemployment compensation. Manifestly, it was the tax credit offset of Title IX of the Social Security Act which in duced fifty jurisdictions each to pass an unemployment compensation law within less than two years after the passage of the federal Social Security Act. Only one state, Wisconsin, was able to pass such a law before the tax credit device was considered. Beginning 53

with the summer of 1935, when it became evident that the Social Security Act would be passed, the states made preparations for instituting what is perhaps the largest joint program of legislative action that the United States has ever seen. Since a state, which failed or refused to pass an unemployment compensation law which would be approved by the Social Security Board, could confer no greater benefits on its employers than a state which would pass such a law, and would in addition deprive its employees of substantial rights which employees in other states would have, it was a foregone conclusion that every state would fall in line. By July 1, 1937, every state, the Territories of Hawaii and Alaska, and the 

District of Columbia, had an unemployment compensation law which complied with the conditions set up in the Social Security Act.

These conditions do not attempt to set up in detail any unified plan of collecting contributions or paying benefits. Considering this fact it is therefore surprising that the state acts exhibit as much uniformity as they do. The rate of contributions is the same for practically all states, that is, 2.7 per cent of the wages paid for employment; the minima and maxima for benefit payments are very similar considering the fact that employment conditions vary so widely throughout the United States; and the exemptions from the definition of employment are also practically uniform. The reason for this is the speed and hastiness with which most state legislatures passed their laws, and, further, the desire of most states to impose no greater burdens than those which Title IX of the Social Security Act imposed. 

Aside from these intangible limiting factors on the provisions of state unemployment compensation laws, the Social Security Act provides two definite lists of conditions with which all state laws find it to their advantage to conform. Title IX of the Social Security Act provides that the state act must be approved by the Social Security Board in respect to certain provisions before the board can certify the state law as entitling employers in that state to take credit for payments made to the state fund against their federal unemployment tax. Failure to take such credit would, of course, subject those employers to two unemployment taxes, thereby doubling their liability and saddling them with an additional obligation which other employers would not have to bear. The provisions which a state law must contain to win the approval of the 

Social Security Board are these: 

(1) All compensation must be paid through public employment offices in the state or through such other agencies as the board may approve; (2) No unemployment benefits may be paid until two years have elapsed within which contributions to the state fund were required;

(3) All money which the state receives in unemployment contributions must immediately upon such receipt be paid over to the Secretary of the Treasury to the credit of the unemployment trust fund; (4) All money which the state withdraws from the unemployment trust fund must be used solely in the payment of compensation, exclusive of expenses of administration; (5) A state may not deny compensation to an otherwise eligible individual for refusing to accept new work under any of the following conditions: (a) If the position offered is vacant due directly to a strike, lock-out, or other labor dispute; (b) If the wages, hours, or other conditions of the work offered are substantially less favorable to the individual than those prevailing for similar work in the locality; (c) If as a condition of being employed the individual would be required to join a company union or to resign from or refrain from joining any bona fide labor organization; (6) All the rights, privileges or immunities conferred by the state law shall exist subject to the power of the legislature to amend or repeal such law at any time. 

Up to January 1, 1939, the Social Security Board had certified its approval for the year 1938 of all state acts except that of Oregon. The Oregon law was not specifically disapproved but decision on it was held in abeyance. The reason for such action with respect to the Oregon law was that a question had arisen as to whether provision 5 of those above listed was satisfied because of the law which Oregon voters had adopted by referendum defining very strictly what a strike or labor dispute was. If the Social Security 

Board should eventually decide that the Oregon employers are not entitled to deduct payments to their state fund against the federal unemployment tax, it will mean that those employers will be placed in an extremely unfavorable position, in comparison with all other employers in the United States.

The Social Security Board is empowered under Title III of the 

Social Security Act to certify to the Secretary of the Treasury for payment to each state which has an approved unemployment compensation law such amounts as the board deems to be necessary for the proper administration of the state law. The board will not pay the administrative expenses of any state law unless it finds that it includes provisions for (1) such methods of administration as are found by the board to be reasonably calculated to insure the full payment of unemployment compensation when due; (2) opportunity for a fair hearing before an impartial tribunal, for all

individuals whose claims for unemployment compensation are denied; (3) the making of such reports in such form and containing such information as the board may from time to time require, and compliance with the provisions the board may from time to time find necessary to assure the correctness and verification of such reports; (4) making available upon request to any agency of the 

United States charged with the administration of public works or assistance through public employment, the name and other information concerning recipients of unemployment compensation; and (5) making its records available to the Railroad Retirement Board, and affording reasonable cooperation with every agency of the United States charged with the administration of any unemployment insurance law.

The general nature of these conditions makes it certain that divergencies as to all matters not covered therein should exist in state laws. Such divergencies when based on peculiarities common only to one state or to a regional group of states, are to be expected, and are in fact desirable since they serve as testing grounds for new and possibly valuable theories. Certain differences between states are, however, a handicap. For example, the various definitions relating to geographical coverage serve only to confuse the employer and make it difficult for the employee who has worked in a number of states to claim his rightful benefits. These and all other matters relating to state unemployment compensation laws will be discussed in the present chapter, leaving to the tabular material in the next chapter the specific applications of such principles to the individual states.

Title IX, the federal unemployment tax, does not set up a system for paying unemployment compensation benefits. It is left to the state to collect the money, pay the benefits, and in general administer the entire program. Since the contributions paid by employers to state funds must be exclusively used for unemployment benefits, it becomes important to examine the various types of funds which the state accounts have established. 

The Wisconsin unemployment compensation law, the first state act passed in the United States, adopted what was then commonly termed the American plan of unemployment compensation, that is, a system whereby an inducement was given the individual employer to stabilize employment in the hope that unemployment would thereby be reduced. Whether stabilization is the most efficient method of achieving that desirable end is a problem for economists and practical administrators to decide, and will not be discussed in detail here. See, however, Chapter V of the Foundation Guide, second edition.

The Wisconsin plan is to limit employees to the funds paid in by their employer, and to permit employers whose employees draw no unemployment benefits to secure a reduced rate. The analogy

between such a system and the carrying of workmen's compensation insurance by private companies is evident. The Wisconsin plan of separate employers' reserves has not attained the popularity which it might seem to deserve. Only seven out of fifty-one jurisdictions have adopted it, and of those seven, four have modified it to a greater or less extent. Wisconsin, Nebraska, and Kentucky are the three states using the strict employers' reserve type of fund. A true employers' reserve account as applied to a state fund means that each employer, or employers who have joined together, has a separate account into which he pays his contributions and from which those individuals entitled to benefits by reason of employment with such employer are limited in the amount of benefits they may draw. Once an employers' reserve account becomes exhausted, those individuals still entitled to draw benefits from it are deprived of any further recourse except from a pooled fund consisting of earnings such as interest and fines which are not allocated to any employer's account. Although the employers' accounts remain separate, the funds comprising those accounts are, of course, mingled when they are turned over to the unemployment trust fund.

The remaining forty-four states use the pooled fund system, under which an employee is not limited in his right to benefits to the balance remaining in his employer's account, but is entitled to draw from the pooled moneys of all employers in the state until the state fund itself may run dry. Since even under the employers' reserve type of law the maintenance of separate employers' accounts is a bookkeeping device, it was found quite feasible to set up the same bookkeeping accounts for each employer even under a pooled fund, the only difference being that in the pooled fund type of law the employee had a larger fund to which he could look. 

Of the forty-four states with pooled funds, thirty-three provide for merit rating, that is, a system whereby an employer's rate may be reduced or increased depending on his employment experience, and eleven states, although without any present stipulation for merit rating, provide that the matter is to be studied by the state agency.

In connection with either the employers' reserve or the pooled fund type of law, a state may provide for a guaranteed employment plan, whereby an employer or group of employers enter into a compact with all the employees in a distinct plant or establishment, guaranteeing them in advance for the period of at least one year a specified number of weeks of work at a specified number of hours per week. In order to qualify for the additional federal credit allowed approved merit rating plans, a contract for guaranty of employment must guarantee thirty hours of wages in each of forty calendar weeks (with one weekly hour deducted for each added week guaranteed) in twelve months. It may provide that

an individual's guaranty is to be gained only after a probationary period of not more than twelve weeks. The guaranteeing employer must give security or assurance satisfactory to the state agency for the fulfillment of his guarantees. If the guaranty is not fulfilled or renewed, compensation must be payable with respect to the unemployment of the individuals whose employment was guaranteed and who would otherwise be eligible for compensation under the state law, from either the security given by the guaranteeing employer or from the state unemployment fund in which the employer has maintained a certain reserve. California, Idaho, Indiana, and Minnesota are the only four states making provision for this type of plan. The Idaho system, however, is inoperative because credit offset under its plan is not obtainable against the federal unemployment tax, thereby insuring that no employer will care to come within its scope.

California, Idaho, and Wisconsin have sections providing for the establishment of exempt plans, by which an employer will be exempted from the payment of contributions to the state fund if he guarantees a private unemployment benefit system under which his employees would secure at least equal rights as they would have under the state system. Wisconsin limits the exempt plan to governmental units and it is probably only in this state and with this type of employer that the plan will ever be used. Title 

IX does not provide that employers may take federal credit under the exempt plan system, and therefore no employer has any incentive to establish such a system, except if, as in Wisconsin, the state act covers employers such as governmental units who are not covered under the federal unemployment tax. 

Who Are Subject Employers. The term "subject" or "liable" employer refers to one who is subject to the payment of contributions under a state unemployment compensation law. Although the term "contributions" is usually used in the state laws it must be remembered that for all practical purposes of administration the contribution is a tax, the liability for which may be enforced through the ordinary tax enforcement provisions of the state. 

Although "tax" is synonymous with contributions in this respect the latter term is the preferred one in speaking of state unemployment compensation laws and is the one used by all state agencies when referring to the money payments required under their acts. 

The reason for using an unfamiliar term instead of the more familiar "tax" was (1) to raise the inference that these enforced money payments were for a special purpose and benefitting directly a limited class, and (2) to obviate the constitutional objections which might have arisen in many states through the usual state constitutional provision that all taxes collected shall be expended for a public purpose and shall be paid into the state treasury.

Since the contributions collected under the state unemployment compensation laws are required to be paid into the unemployment trust fund maintained by the Secretary of the Treasury, it was deemed advisable not to call the payment a tax.

The usual conception of an employer liable under a state unemployment compensation law is one who has had a certain number of individuals in employment on at least one day in each of a specified number of weeks. It is under this definition that most employing units in the state become liable, but it must be remembered that all state laws contain other provisions whereby an employing unit even though it has less than the required number of employees may be liable for contributions under the state act. A majority of the state laws base their numerical coverage on the standard of the federal unemployment compensation tax, namely, eight individuals in employment on some day in each of twenty different weeks, with the further proviso, not found in the federal unemployment tax, that this status imposes liability not only for the current year but also for the succeeding year. Under the federal unemployment tax an employer may terminate his liability for any year in which he does not employ the required number of employees for the necessary length of time; but the phrase "in the current or preceding year" which most of the state acts have, makes certain that an employer once liable can not terminate his liability for two full calendar years. 

Alabama, Kansas, Michigan, Nebraska, North Carolina, South

Carolina, and Washington, although they require eight or more individuals in employment do not specify that such individuals must be in employment on the same day. It is sufficient under these state acts that there be eight individuals performing services during a week, if there are twenty such weeks during the year. Thus in these states an employer would be liable if he had four individuals working in his establishment from Monday through Wednesday, and four different individuals working from Thursday through Saturday, even though he did not have eight working on any one day. This provision simplifies the state's administrative problem in checking an employer's records since it need not rely on daily records of employment which many employers do not keep. 

There is a uniform tendency among the states gradually to reduce the number of employees required to make an employer liable. Many states have increased their coverage thereby, including a vast number of employees who would not otherwise be entitled to unemployment benefits. In attempting to cover smaller employers, the states have assumed a difficult burden since the number of employers covered increases out of all proportion to the number of employees covered, the lower down in the scale one goes. Thus a state which has 15,000 employers who employ eight or more individuals may cover 1,200,000 employees. The inclusion

of all employers of one or more which would raise the number of liable employers well above 100,000 might increase the number of employees by less than half the number of those already covered. 

This is a problem, however, which must be met since there is no logical reason for paying benefits to an individual when he works for an employer who has eight employees but denying it to the same individual if he works for an employer who has only seven. 

Those employers who are already liable under a state law are usually in favor of including employers with less than the number of employees they have because it means that their competitor does not have the advantage of a tax differential because he hires less employees.

All the state acts require that an employer have a certain number of individuals "in employment." Each of the state acts defines employment as service under a contract of hire and many of them require in addition that it be for compensation. The addition of the phrase "for compensation" raises the inference that a contract of hire may exist even though no remuneration is paid for the services performed. This is probably correct and many states have gone on the basis that an employment relationship exists even though the individual performing services receives no compensation for his work. There is, however, the possibility that a court in construing the phrase "contract for hire" may find therein a necessity for consideration which, in turn, it may feel can be evidenced only through the payment of money or in kind. The problem, although it may seem an academic one, is important in considering the coverage of small corporations which may have unpaid officers. Under the federal unemployment tax an officer of a corporation is an employee by definition, regardless of whether or not he performs services or receives remuneration, unless his office is purely honorary. If a state act is construed to require the payment of consideration for a valid contract of hire it means that an unpaid officer should not be included as an employee in determining whether the corporation has the required number of employees to subject it to the state act. Some states have included unpaid officers of a corporation on the ground that they were required to perform certain charter or statutory duties and could therefore be called upon by the corporation to perform services for it. Illinois, for example, at one time maintained this position but later changed its interpretation of this section of its act by excluding from the definition of employment, officers of a corporation who neither performed services nor received remuneration. 

Other examples can be given of situations where it is important to determine what "service for compensation or under any contract of hire" means. If, for example, an employee takes a leave of absence for three months, is he to be considered in employment even though he performs no services and receives no

remuneration? The Bureau of Internal Revenue has construed the federal unemployment tax as covering an employee in this situation on the ground that Title IX is based on the employment relationship, which is not broken even when the individual is away from his job. Those states which have a statute requiring that all employees be allowed one day of rest in every seven, are faced with the problem of determining whether the individual who has a day off should be counted as in employment. Those establishments, such as restaurants, which are open every day in the week, often encounter this problem, if they have eight different individuals in employment but only seven working on any one day with the eighth relieving a different employee every day. Here, there are not eight individuals actually performing services on any one day although there are eight individuals in employment. States that have ruled on the problem have decided both ways, since either position is logically maintainable. 

The states requiring a certain number of individuals in employment "on some day within each of twenty weeks" have had to determine what a day is, and they have all decided that a day is the twenty-four hours from midnight to midnight. Here again the question has had to be decided because of its practical implications. Is an employee on a night shift to be counted as performing services in one day or in two days? Under the definition of the day it is clear that such an employee performs services in two different days if he works past midnight.

Although most of the state laws provide that the employer have some employment in each of twenty different weeks in a year, there are some requiring a lesser period. Iowa, for example, covers employers of eight or more within fifteen weeks, and New York covers employers of four or more within any fifteen days in the current calendar year. In Ohio only three employees are required at any one time in the current year for the employer to become liable as of the beginning of such year. The District of Columbia covers any one who employs one individual for any period of time, no matter how short. Oregon has a provision which is unique as far as coverage is concerned, in that it requires four or more individuals in any one day in any calendar quarter if the total payroll for such quarter is $500 or more.

Employers have questioned the suitability of the provision which most state acts include, that their liability for contributions is dependent on their employment experience in either the current or preceding year. An employer in a state with this provision will be subject to the law for the year in which he has less than the required number of employees if he was subject in the preceding year. There can be no valid constitutional objection to a definition of employer in terms such as these except where the definition is used to subject an employer to liability on the basis of his employ-

ment experience before the law went into effect. This frequently happened in many states during the first year the law was in effect, since by its terms it included employers who, in the previous year before the law was passed, had had the required number of employees but who in the current year had reduced their employment below that number. Although the point is arguable it has never been brought into court, mainly perhaps because of the small amounts involved.

Seven state laws at one time or another have had a provision requiring contributions from any employing unit within their jurisdiction which was subject to the federal unemployment tax. Such an employer would have eight employees throughout the United States, and possibly one or two in each of a number of states. He might not be covered on the ordinary basis of having enough employees in any one state and yet because he was subject to the federal act the full tax would have had to be paid on his employment experience. These states are Colorado, New Hampshire, Rhode 

Island, Maine, Massachusetts, Michigan, and Ohio for the year 1936 only. Vermont has a section in its law providing that if Title IX is interpreted or extended to include all employees of independent contractors, although they are not under his immediate direction and control or to include employers of less than eight persons, the governor by proclamation shall declare such employers and employees subject to the state law. Washington provides that if 

Title IX is extended to include employers not now covered the governor may by proclamation declare the state law automatically amended to include such employers. If the exemption of maritime employment is removed from the federal unemployment tax a new class of employers and employees will be included and Washington at least will not have to amend its act in the legislature to include this numerically large class of employees.

All but four state laws provide that an employer who becomes liable during the year must pay contributions on his employment from the first day of the year even though he had not fulfilled the conditions for liability as of that date. If an employer reaches the twentieth week of liable experience in a state requiring that length of time he must file contribution reports and pay contributions on the wages of all employees he has had since the beginning of the year. No interest is charged on the contributions for the period since the first of the year since the employer could not be delinquent for the payment of contributions which were not yet due.

The provision for termination of liability in the California act has been declared inoperative, so that at the present there seems to be no way in which the California employer who has once become liable may ever cease from paying contributions on the wages of his employees. There is no provision for termination of coverage

in the District of Columbia law and evidently it is assumed that an employer can cease payment of contributions only when he terminates business entirely. Liability in Ohio is continued only during the current calendar year, and since there is no provision for termination, it is probably up to the employer to decide when he has ceased to be an employer subject to the Ohio law. 

In Vermont an employer is liable for contributions through the end of the succeeding calendar year in which he first became liable but the contributions he has paid on wages in such succeeding year are subject to refund upon a determination by the agency that the conditions for liability were not fulfilled and no benefits were charged against the employing unit in such year. 

In Connecticut an employer is subject from the end of the period in which he fulfilled the conditions for being subject through the end of the quarter in which the agency accepts his termination of liability notice. The record of the preceding fifteen months is considered in order to determine whether the employer is entitled to terminate and the employing unit must notify its employees of the intention so to terminate. 

Beginning with January 1, 1939, a Massachusetts employer is subject as of the first day of the year in which he has fulfilled the conditions of liability and he may terminate only if the agency finds that he did not employ four or more individuals in employment on any day in the last year.

In New York and Oregon an employer is subject from the beginning of the period, either the month or quarter during which he fulfilled the conditions for being subject and he remains subject through the close of the succeeding calendar year. In all other states the employer remains subject for at least two calendar years including the year in which he first becomes liable for contributions.

If an employer is entitled to terminate his coverage on the basis that he has not had sufficient employment experience, he must still satisfy the agency in a number of particulars. He must file a written application, in most states before January 5 of the year for which he wishes to terminate his liability and he must prove to the state agency by whatever evidence or records they deem necessary that he has not had the employment necessary to constitute him an employer subject to the law. 

Because many employers who were entitled to file applications for termination of coverage did not do so within the short time given them by the state laws, many agencies considered it equitable and advisable to permit employers to terminate coverage even after the due date for filing applications had passed. As employers become more familiar with the provisions of their respective state laws, it is certain that this practice will not be indulged in further. It, therefore, behooves employers who may be

in some doubt as to their liability, to determine the closing date for filing such applications and conform thereto.

Voluntary Elections. An employer who has less than the requisite number of employees necessary to constitute him subject for contributions in a certain state may find it to his advantage to elect voluntarily to come under the provisions of the state act and pay contributions on the wages of his employees. Such an action may well increase employee morale since the employees will know that they have the same rights to unemployment benefits as employees of larger employers.

Unions, in many cases, are inserting in their labor contracts a provision that employers for whom their members work, who are not already subject to the state unemployment compensation act, shall elect to become so. If furthermore, the employer is already subject to the federal unemployment tax he is enabled to take credit for payments made to the state fund under a voluntary election, against his federal tax. Thus, no additional burden as far as money payments are concerned is placed on the employer, and the additional small burden of filing reports may well be outweighed by the fact that in those states which have merit rating provisions the employer may be enabled to build up his fund, secure a lowered rate and thereby effect a real saving on his unemployment taxes.

All states, but California and the District of Columbia, permit this type of election. In the case of the District of Columbia there is no need for election provisions since all employers are automatically liable no matter how few employees they have or for how short a period. The lack of such provision in the California law is unaccounted for, and is a serious objection from any point of view.

Forty of the fifty-one state jurisdictions permit an employing unit for whom excluded services are performed to elect to cover the employees performing such services. Thus, farmers, steamship companies, employers with a large number of domestic servants, and any other employer who has employees performing excluded services for him may, by filing an election, cover such individuals. 

Payments made to a state under this type of election are not entitled to federal credit if they are contributions on wages paid for employment not covered by the federal unemployment tax. When a state act exempts employment which is covered by the federal unemployment tax it may be advisable for the employer to elect to cover his employees since he must include them for purposes of his federal tax. For example, the Illinois act excludes services performed by casual labor in emergencies, such as snow shoveling or flood reconstruction work, if the entire job does not last longer than seven days in any one month. The federal unemployment

tax does not exclude this type of service, and an employer who would have to pay on such labor to the federal government might consider it advisable to elect to cover such of his employees who were engaged in this type of work, under the state law. 

Since states exclude certain types of service because of their policy that employees performing such activities are for one reason or another difficult to include in an unemployment compensation program, it may well be that the state agency will refuse to accept voluntary elections of coverage for these exempted types of service. Illinois, for example, makes it a policy not to accept elections of coverage on services performed by minors who are actually attending school. This type of service is specifically exempted from the state law and it is the feeling of the agency that the legislature did not intend to have minors actually attending school come within the scope of a program of unemployment compensation.

A third type of voluntary election allows an employing unit to accept liability in a given state for services performed entirely outside that state by a resident thereof, on whose wages no contributions are required and paid under any other unemployment compensation law. The situation covered by this provision is best exemplified in the case of a salesman who lives in one state but does no work in that state, confining his activities to the surrounding five or six states. Since his services are not localized in any of the states in which he does work and since he does not spend the greater part of his time in any one state, such an individual could not be covered in any state unless his employer were allowed to elect to cover him in the state of his residence. This type of election is not permitted in Connecticut, Hawaii, Indiana, New 

York, and South Carolina.

In most states permitting elections of these various types the election must be made for at least two calendar years, and may be terminated only on the basis of a written notice by the employer to the agency thirty days prior to January 1 of the third calendar year, indicating that the employer wishes to revoke his election. States which do not require an employer who is automatically liable to remain liable for at least two years, usually have the same provision for termination of coverage in voluntary elections as they do in cases of automatic coverage.

When the state laws first went into force there were a number of instances of employers who wished to elect to come under the state law under conditions of their own choosing. In all cases such conditional acceptances were considered to be an insufficient compliance with the state law and the elections were refused. Employers subject to the federal unemployment tax, but not to a certain state law, were willing to elect voluntarily to come under the state law if they could be assured that their moneys would

be refunded if either the federal unemployment tax or the state law were declared unconstitutional. However, since state and federal unemployment taxes have been sustained by the Supreme Court, such attempted conditional acceptances are unusual. In other cases, employing units have wished to accept voluntary coverage because they were not automatically liable at the present time but expected to become so toward the end of the year. In this way they expected to be able to space out more equally the contributions which would eventually become due. By attaching a condition that their election should terminate if it happened that they would not be automatically liable, the state agency was forced to refuse their election. Under the state laws, of course, a proper election involves the assumption of liability for at least two calendar years regardless of the fact that the original reason for the election may no longer be present.

In the few states which tax employees for unemployment compensation purposes an employer may accept voluntary coverage without first securing the consent of his employees. The employees in such cases must assume a burden not of their asking but in return they are granted the right to demand unemployment benefits when eligible. 

Employers who might otherwise willingly accept voluntary coverage have, in some instances, hesitated to do so because of a fear that such an election will be considered an admission that they are subject to other regulatory or taxing measures of the state. For example, employers who have small sales offices with less than the necessary number of employees to be automatically liable in a state have felt, in some instances, that a voluntary election might be construed as an admission on their part that they are doing business within the state and would, therefore, subject them to closer supervision and regulation over their activities than they would otherwise be subject to. Such states as have considered the problem have been uniformly of the opinion that a voluntary election of coverage is nothing more than an admission that there are individuals performing services for the employer in that state. 

No inference can be drawn from the fact that a company has employees in a state to the conclusion that that company is doing business therein. What constitutes "doing business" within a state is a difficult legal problem which cannot be dealt with here. It involves, however, many elements in addition to that of employment. A company may have services performed for it in a state by many individuals without being considered to be doing business within that state; and on the other hand it may very well be that it is doing business within the state within the purview of the corporation laws of that state, even though it may employ no one or has not elected to cover those employees which it may have therein.

Affiliation of Employing Units. On page 37 in the previous chapter the relationship between legal entities and "employers" was discussed. The distinction was made between a business establishment and a legal entity and it was pointed out that a legal entity or, as it is more commonly known in a state unemployment compensation legislation, an employing unit, can be situated in a number of different locations and can be engaged in a number of totally unrelated activities. For the purpose of determining how many individuals are in employment the state laws uniformly combine the employment experience of all establishments within the state comprising a single employing unit. Even without a specific provision to this effect in the state law (45 laws do contain this provision) it would be evident that all the employees of an employing unit, no matter where in the state they might be employed or in what activities, would be considered together in determining whether the employer has had sufficient employment experience to make him subject to the state law. 

Under the federal unemployment tax the employment experience of separate legal entities is never combined. The liability of each is based on its own record of the length of time and number of individuals in its employment, and the fact that separate legal entities may be owned or controlled by the same interests is unimportant. Even though a separation of one legal entity into two or more is entered into for the deliberate purpose of avoiding the federal unemployment tax no combination of their separate employment experiences will be treated as constituting each a "liable” employer. The situation is radically different under a majority of the state unemployment compensation acts. About thirty-five out of the fifty-one state acts contain a provision that if two or more employing units are owned or controlled by the same interests, and if their combined employment experience would be sufficient to constitute them an employer as defined in the act, then each will be considered as a "liable" employer and subject to the payment of contributions on the wages paid to its own employees. 

Doubts have been thrown on the constitutionality of a provision of this sort because it treats separate legal entities as one. 

There is a feeling current among laymen and lawyers alike that at least in the case of corporations the state can never look behind the corporate structure to the inner reality of the situation. Even though it is unusual for courts to go behind the corporate fiction, it is quite possible for a statute to provide that such shall be done, and where it is done the courts have no option but to follow the dictates of the legislature. When the entities to be combined are not corporations the constitutionality problem is worth even less consideration since a partnership is usually considered merely as an aggregate of the individuals composing it and not as an entity separate and apart from the individual partners or all its members.

What constitutes ownership or control of an employing unit is a fundamental question which each state agency interpreting its act must decide for itself on whatever bases it considers fair and equitable and administratively practicable. It is usually considered that an individual who owns more than a majority of the voting stock of a corporation is in control thereof, even though as a matter of fact he may have delegated his right to control elsewhere and has never sought to exercise such rights. It is possible for an individual, or group of individuals, actually to control the destinies of a corporation even when they own less than a majority of the stock but the state agencies are loath to consider such a situation as coming within the terms of the affiliation section of their act. No individual can be said to own a corporation, meaning thereby its assets, because such assets are owned by the corporation itself. Some one may control the activities of the corporation in so far as they own the stock which has been issued. A corporation may own or control the stock of another corporation and their employment experience will be combined. It is not necessary that either employing unit be "liable" on its own experience but only that when combined the two shall have sufficient employment experience to constitute them an employer within the meaning of the act. Therefore, if one corporation has more than eight employees and all its stock is owned by a corporation which has only three employees, the two will be combined so that the latter corporation will also be a subject employer. 

The interests which own or control an employing unit need not reside or be actually present in any way in the state if the employing units sought to be affiliated are both within the state. 

Thus, affiliation exists between two subsidiaries of a corporation if together they have the required number of employees, even though the holding corporation is not itself an employing unit in the state. But an employing unit which by itself has less than the necessary number of employees will not be affiliated with another company not an employing unit in the same state even though the two may be owned or controlled by interests residing in the state. The "interests" referred to in questions of the affiliation of employing units may consist of a combination of two or more individuals or groups. If A owns 40 per cent of the stock in the X corporation, B owns 30 per cent and C owns 20 per cent in the same corporation it will be affiliated with the Y corporation where B owns 40 per cent, A owns 30 per cent and D owns 20 per cent of the stock. The reason is that A and B together control both the X and Y corporations through the ownership of more than half the issued stock. Most states do not require any proof that A and B actually act in concert for the furtherance of their interest but presume that they do so. It is probable that such states would hold each corporation as a "liable" employer even

though it were actually shown that A and B did not constitute a community of interest. A few states, however, require a showing in all cases of possible affiliation that the interests sought to be combined actually constitute a single force in so far as the control of the two separate entities is concerned. 

In the example given above of the X and Y corporations, if A owned 60 per cent of the stock of the X corporation and B and C owned 20 per cent each; and in the Y corporation B owned 60 per cent and A and D owned 20 per cent each a more difficult question would be presented. In this situation A controls the X corporation without having to add B's stock to his; and B controls the Y corporation without A. Together A and B own 80 per cent of the two corporations but a good argument can be presented against the affiliation of these two on the ground that (1) A and B do not own the two corporations, and complete ownership is necessary for affiliation, and (2) there is no community of interests between A and B as far as control is concerned, since A does not need B's stock ownership to control the X corporation and B does not need A's stock to control the Y corporation. It is admitted, however, that many state agencies would consider A and B as combined for the purpose of affiliating the X and Y corporations. 

Whether separate entities should be combined because of the ownership or control of each by a husband and wife is a problem often faced by the state agencies. It is granted that husbands and wives may each own their own business separate and apart from the other, with each controlling his establishment absolutely. 

Where it can be shown that the wife has acquired her business independently of the husband, for example by inheritance or by purchase with her own funds, there is no reason why the separate organizations should be affiliated for the purpose of making each a "liable" employer. Where, however, a husband has transferred one of two businesses which he owned to his wife, for insufficient consideration, and where the husband still controls the activities of both businesses in the same manner as he did previously, it is the tendency of the state agencies to consider that the husband's control alone is sufficient to combine the employment experience of both units for the purpose of determining whether both together have sufficient employment experience. In those states where community property laws are in effect it is probable that the separate businesses of husband and wife will be combined even though it might be shown that each spouse conducts his separate establishment without the interference or control of the other. 

Each partner in a partnership has an equal voice in the management of the business unless the partnership agreement contains some provision to the contrary. Therefore, even though the interests of the partners may be unequal in so far as sharing of profits is concerned, it will be assumed that each partner has an equal

right with every other partner to manage the affairs of their organization. This has important consequences where affiliation of separate units is concerned. If A owns 75 per cent of the stock of the X corporation and B owns the remainder, and A and C are partners in a partnership providing that A shall receive 75 per cent of the partnership profits, and C shall be entitled to 25 per cent, it would appear that A controlled both the corporation and the partnership and that the two should, therefore, be affiliated. 

However, in the absence of a specific agreement between A and C that A shall have the right to control the partnership regardless of C's wishes, the usual rule is that neither A nor C alone control the partnership and that the corporation and partnership would not be affiliated since A does not own or control both units. 

Stockholders in a corporation may place their stock in trust to be voted either by fellow stockholders or by some other party. 

If the voting trustee is the same in the two separate corporations, the two will be affiliated because the trustee has the right to control both. If an individual is acting in two separate capacities, as for example, receiver of two separate businesses, such businesses would not be affiliated because of the fact that the same individual happened to be named as trustee of both. Thus, a bank which is trustee of property under a great number of separate trust agreements is not considered to have control of the various properties for purposes of affiliation, where the beneficial owners of the trusts are individuals who have no connection with each other. The same principle applies to managing agents for real estate who may, as a matter of fact, have a large degree of control over the properties they manage for different individuals but who are not considered as "the same interests" owning or controlling separate entities.

This is an instance of control being exercised through acquiescence of the true owners and the property of such owners cannot be affiliated with property which the managing agents may own or control in some other capacity, as for example, as receivers of property which has been foreclosed under a mortgage. 

If two separate entities have been affiliated and each has become a "liable" employer through ownership or control by the same interests, neither unit is entitled to terminate its liability immediately, when the affiliation is disrupted. If A and B each own 

50 per cent of the stock of two different corporations and A sells his stock in one corporation to C, thereby ending the mutuality of control of the two corporations, neither will be allowed to terminate until it can satisfy the provisions of the state act under which it is subject. As we have seen previously, this means, under most state acts, that neither corporation can end its liability until it can prove that in the previous year it did not have the required number of employees, considering for this purpose only, the two affiliated corporations as constituting a single employing unit.

S 

Thus, under the provisions of most state acts, entities which have been formerly affiliated must continue paying contributions for at least the remainder of the current year, and if the affiliation is broken after the required number of weeks has passed for liability, then for the succeeding year and until formal application for termination has been approved.

Acquisition of Liable Employer. Under the federal unemployment tax if one legal entity is succeeded by another which operates with the former's assets or in its place of business, the liability of the successor depends only on whether he himself has satisfied the conditions for becoming a liable employer, i.e., he has had eight or more individuals in employment on one day in each of twenty different weeks in the current calendar year. Most of the state acts extend their coverage of employers by making the successor entity automatically liable if he has acquired the organization, trade or business or substantially all the assets of one who was already an employer subject to the law. The term "acquisition" is general enough to cover many kinds of transfers between parties. The most common form, of course, is a voluntary sale by the predecessor to the successor. Acquisition, however, may take place by involuntary transfer through foreclosure of a mortgage, by a change in lessees, or by an owner leasing out equipment which he formerly used himself.

Little difficulty arises in determining whether the acquisition provision of a state act is applicable in the voluntary sale of an organization, trade, or business, unless the problem arises as to whether substantially all the assets of the predecessor have been sold. This point will be treated in further detail subsequently. 

Many state agencies have been in doubt as to how far they ought to extend this provision when dealing with transfers arising through other means than voluntary sales. For example, should the mortgagee who has foreclosed a chattel mortgage on the fixtures and assets of an establishment and who thereupon operates it as his own be considered as a successor to the organization, trade or business of the mortgagor? The decision of most state agencies in this situation would be that the mortgagee is the successor to the liability of the predecessor who has been foreclosed. 

But, if the mortgagee were to sell the assets, on which he had foreclosed, to a third party who then used them to open up a new business at some other locality, it is probable that this would not be considered an acquisition by the third party. Between these two extremes are found varying combinations of fact to which state agencies react in different ways. Some will require a continuity of interest between the predecessor and the successor, while others trace the assets and impose liability on the final owner regardless of the fact that he has not acquired directly from the predecessor who was liable.

Owners of equipment who lease it out may find that on the expiration of the lease a liability is attached to their equipment which will make the new lessee liable even though he has had no relationship with the former lessee. For example, an owner of a store suitable for a restaurant may acquire it with all the necessary fixtures and lease it as a unit to one who hires his own help and runs it as his own business. If a new party goes in on the expiration of the old lease he will, under the probable ruling of most state agencies, assume the liability of his predecessor on the grounds that he has succeeded to the latter's organization, trade and business.

Because of the vagueness implicit in the terms "organization, trade or business" some states have attempted to spell out what is meant by the use of these terms. The provision in the Kentucky law is worth quoting as an illustration of the factors deemed to be material in the determination of whether the successor has acquired his predecessor's trade, organization or business or substantially all of his assets. A subject employer means: "Any individual or employing unit which acquires any of the assets of a subject employer by any means whatever otherwise than in the ordinary course of trade" unless the Commission finds that all of the following conditions exist: (a) the transferee has not assumed a substantial amount of the transferor's obligations; (b) the transferee has not acquired a substantial amount of the transferor's goodwill; (c) the transferee has not continued or resumed a substantial part of the business of the transferor, either in the same establishment or elsewhere; and (d) the transferee has not employed substantially the same workers as those the transferor had employed in connection with the assets transferred. If, therefore, a transferee or successor has assumed the debts of his predecessor, or has acquired his goodwill, or has continued part of the business of the transferor in any locality or has employed the same individuals as his predecessor, it seems extremely probable that he will be held as a liable employer on the basis of his predecessor's employment experience. The successor's liability, of course, can be terminated only through the method prescribed in the state act which, as we noted above, usually requires a showing that he has not had the required number of individuals in his employ in either the current or preceding year.

It is to be noted that this provision does not impose on the successor any liability for the unpaid contributions of his predecessor. This is not to say, however, that he may not be so liable, depending on the enforcement provisions of the state act. Such liability for the unpaid contributions of his predecessor may be imposed in many states if the bulk sales law of that state has not been complied with. Or, depending on the necessary provisions in the state law, if a lien is attached to the assets of the predecessor

by virtue of his delinquency the successor will, of course, be subject to the same lien on those assets. This type of provision prevents firms from avoiding the payment of unemployment compensation contributions for some period of time by merely changing the legal form of the organization, for example, by dissolving a corporation and forming a partnership between the former officers or stockholders. Even though such a maneuver may reduce the number of employees below those required by the act, the successor may remain liable for contributions based on the wages paid all its employees in its new form.

Perhaps the most common situation (next to voluntary sales) requiring a decision on liability through acquisition from a subject employer, is that arising from the split-up of a subject employer into two or more separate units. This is especially frequent in cases of partnerships or corporations maintaining more than one retail establishment. What usually happens is that the corporation or partnership is dissolved and the principal stockholders, or former partners, each take over one of the retail establishments as a sole proprietor. The problem may be answered in two ways,-one, that each successor is a "liable" employer if the predecessor unit was, and the other, that the successor is not automatically "liable" on the grounds that he has not acquired the organization, trade or business or substantially all the assets of a "liable" employer. An opinion of the California unemployment compensation agency will serve as an example of the first alternative. Here, B was an employer operating two stores at different locations both selling the same line of merchandise. Eight or more employees were normally engaged in each store. B sold out one of the stores to C who prior to the time of such acquisition was not an employer subject to the act. In deciding that C became an employer subject to the act by acquiring one of the two establishments of B the California agency stated: "The section does not provide and was not intended to mean that the successor must acquire the entire enterprise of the predecessor, or substantially all the predecessor's entire assets. By 'organization, trade or business,' is meant any severable part of the predecessor's enterprise which may be operated as a self-contained unit and is customarily or frequently so operated by persons in business for gain. 

The word 'business' is to be construed in its common or colloquial sense. "A business may be separate because of the nature of the merchandise sold or the services rendered. For example, a general merchandise store may have a hardware department, a drygoods department, and a grocery department; the sale of any one of these departments or substantially

all the assets thereof would involve the sale of a separate business within the meaning of the section. Or to give another example, a radio store may be composed of a sales agency for a particular make of radio and a servicing department for the repair of radios. The two departments are separate businesses, unless the service department is wholly incidental to the carrying on of the sales department in the normal course of business."

The California opinion, however, qualifies the broad language quoted above by holding that the successor acquires a subject status of the predecessor only if the number of employees normally engaged by the predecessor in the separate business acquired would have been sufficient, without regard to the employment in his other "separate business" to give the predecessor the status of a subject employer. In other words, if the state act requires eight individuals in twenty weeks to constitute an employing unit an employer, that is the employment experience which the predecessor must have had in the separate business which was acquired, in order for the successor to be automatically "liable.”

This ruling seems a rather strange interpretation of "organization, trade or business" although some support is lent by the analogy of decisions under the bulk sales laws of many states which hold that a bulk sales notice is required to be given when one of several separable establishments is sold. The requirement of the 

California agency that the predecessor's separate establishment employ a sufficient number of persons to constitute the predecessor subject by itself would seem to be without any justification in the law. There is no reason to make a distinction as to the number of employees the separable establishments of the predecessor had in determining whether the successor should be automatically "liable" because of the acquisition.

A more logical stand was taken by the North Carolina agency in much the same set of facts as given above when it held that the successors to a liable predecessor each remained liable even though each separate establishment had less than the required number of employees.

A Utah decision, however, refused to consider a single employer maintaining two establishments as constituting two employers after a split-up. Here, two brothers owned all the stock of a corporation which operated two stores in different towns. The corporation was dissolved and each brother took one of the stores. Although the corporation was a "liable" employer, the Utah agency refused to hold that the two acquiring employing units acquired the organization, trade or business or substantially all the assets of an employer. The reason given was as follows:

"It is apparent that the two acquiring stores did not either of them acquire substantially all the assets of the corporation, nor can it be said that such units acquired the organization, trade or business of such corporation, the obvious intent of the language meant all or practically all of the organization, trade or business of the selling unit.” 

In the section on voluntary elections of employing units it was mentioned that such elections are for a period of at least two calendar years. What happens when an employing unit which has voluntarily elected to become subject to the state law is acquired by a successor? Two methods of approaching the problem are possible: (1) That the voluntary election of one employing unit cannot bind a successor organization since the election is the result of a voluntary act and should not be considered as imposing an involuntary liability; or (2) that since the employing unit which has voluntarily elected has done so with the understanding that it becomes liable to the same extent as any other employer it must be considered that one of the incidents of such liability is that of automatically transferring a liable status onto a successor. The latter line of reasoning has appeared more logical to those state agencies which have considered the problem. It is one of the things which a buyer must take into consideration these days since he may unknowingly subject himself to a liability to which he would not otherwise be subject.

Most of those states containing a provision for the automatic liability of a successor to a "liable" employer contain a corollary section in their act providing that if the employment experience of the predecessor before the acquisition and the employment experience of the successor after the acquisition, both within the same calendar year, would be sufficient to constitute one an "employer" that the successor becomes such. Thus, if A has had eight employees in each of ten different weeks in 1939 and then sells the business to B on April 1, who thereafter has eight individuals in employment in each of the same number of weeks, B would become subject as to his own employees as of the first of the year. It is to be noted that B's liability commences with the first of the year and not with the date of acquisition. B would not be liable for contributions on the wages of A's employees before the acquisition, but once B became a "liable" employer he would in his right be liable on all his employees whether in the newly acquired business or not, as of the beginning of the calendar year in which he became subject.

Contractors and Sub-contractors. About half of the state acts contain a provision usually known as the contractor and sub-contractor clause through which the coverage of employees is extended

to include individuals engaged by a sub-contractor to do work in the usual course of business of an employing unit. For the purposes of this section the employing unit must include these individuals hired by the sub-contractor as its own employees and, if through such inclusion the total number of employees is above the required number for liability in that state, it must pay contributions not only for its own employees but also for those of the sub-contractors. 

This provision is most common in those states requiring eight or more individuals in employment although it is not unusual for states with a coverage of less employees to have it also. Its purpose is clearly to discourage avoidance of payment of unemployment compensation contributions by contracting out or subletting work which was formerly done by employees of the employing unit. It has an evident justification for situations of this sort. But because of the lack of qualifications in the usual type of act containing this provision it has been quite uniformly implied to cover situations where there has been no attempt at evasion or avoidance. The effect of the provision is that an employing unit must include as his own employees those who are hired by an admitted independent contractor. Illinois and New York contain qualifications on this provision which better seem to accomplish its real purpose. These two state acts provide that the principal employer must include as his own employees those who are hired by the contractor or sub-contractor who is doing work in the usual course of the principal employer's business unless the sub-contractor is actually available to perform work for any one else at the same time that he is performing work for the principal employer, and unless the sub-contractor is engaged in an independent trade, profession or business. An illustration of the difference in effect of the Illinois and New York type of provision and that of the other states can be seen in the following situation: an individual owning a retail establishment requires some method whereby he may deliver parcels by truck. If he does not have enough business to keep one man on the truck busy all day he may contract with a trucking firm which agrees to supply him with a man and truck for as many hours as necessary at an agreed-upon rate. Under the usual type of sub-contractor provision the state agency would, if it were interpreting this provision literally, consider the individual driving the truck as in the employment of the retail establishment for the hours in which he was doing their delivery work. However, in Illinois and New York if the trucking firm was engaged in an independent business of its own and had a number of trucks so that its facilities were not exclusively devoted to one account, it would be held that the truck driver was in the employment of the trucking firm even for the time he was delivering parcels for the retail establishment. The Bureau of Internal Revenue in interpreting 

Title IX of the Social Security Act, which does not contain any

sub-contractor provisions, has ruled in a similar situation that the truck driver would not be an employee of the retail establishment even though the latter does exercise some control over the performance of his services.

In those states which have the unqualified type of sub-contractor provision the most common problem for decision has been that resulting from building operations, where it is the usual practice for a contractor to be given an entire contract, and then to subcontract it out to various specialized sub-contractors. Questions have been raised as to what part of the work which the general contractor lets out to sub-contractors is part of the general contractor's usual trade or business, so that the employees of the subcontractor are to be included as employees of the general contractor. 

The Tennessee agency has issued a ruling which illustrates this problem and suggests its solution. The ruling reads in part 

"We have consistently held, with reference to general contractors, that contractors or sub-contractors for painting, roofing, sheet-metal, glass and glazing, structural steel erection, masonry, and flooring are contracting for work which is part of the usual trade or business of the general contractor on construction work. Therefore, under the law, the general contractor is responsible for contribution on the wages of the workers of such contractors or sub-contractors while they are performing services under such contracts, unless such contractors or sub-contractors are themselves 'employers' under the Act. *** The theory upon which we hold such work to be part of the usual trade or business of the general contractor is that the general contractor makes a price for the completed job which job of necessity includes painting, roofing, etc. Thus, the business of the general contractor is to deliver to the party with whom he makes the contract a complete performance of the terms of the contract. Therefore, when the general contractor enters into these sub-contracts, they are for work which is part of the usual trade or business of the general contractor, and he thus becomes 'liable' in the manner aforementioned.” 

The theory upon which the Tennessee agency goes seems to prove too much. If the general contractor must deliver the completed building to the party with whom he contracts, why should any distinction be made as to the types of service performed by sub-contractors? If painting and roofing are part of the usual trade of the general contractor, why should not the work of building the foundation, of lathing, or of plumbing be considered as part of the usual trade of the contractor even when performed by a sub-contractor? The reason evidently is that the Tennessee agency feels

that that would be going too far. If carried to its logical extreme this doctrine of what the usual trade or business of an employer is, would include the most trivial and incidental tasks, such as having waste and garbage removed, or arranging with a detective service to watch the premises at night.

All the states containing the contractor provision, except Minnesota, provide that any payment of contributions that the principal employer is required to make on behalf of the sub-contractor's employees may be recovered by him from such sub-contractor. This, of course, represents some saving but it does not effect a complete freeing from liability, since once the contractor's employees have been included with his own for the purpose of determining liability he must pay contributions on his own employees.

The whole contractor and sub-contractor provision has been very difficult to interpret and apply, and it may be expected that future amendments to the state acts will eventually drop it since its purpose can better be accomplished by reducing the number of individuals required for liability purposes, thereby covering subcontractors in their own right.

Territorial Coverage of Services. Each state unemployment compensation law is limited in its jurisdiction to services performed within its territorial limits, except that they may provide that all the services of an individual, no matter where performed, must be covered within the state if certain other conditions are satisfied. 

Since all the states are coordinate sovereignties it is theoretically possible for the same individual's services to be covered either in whole or in part by more than one state, since each may have a sufficient basis for including the individual's services under its act. 

The tendency, however, is to allow or require an individual's remuneration to be covered in only one state regardless of how many states he may be working in. To this end, most of the states have adopted provisions on territorial coverage which are all very similar, on the theory that if the elements which are decisive of coverage are the same, no state could dispute the correctness of any other state's interpretation. As a whole, the adoption of this common principle has been of immense aid in helping employers determine in what state an individual, who performed services in many states, should be covered.

This basic provision, contained in the laws of forty-four states, provides in effect that service localized in the state, i.e., performed entirely within the state, or both within and without the state if the service without is incidental to that within; or if the service is not localized in any state but some of the service is performed in this state, and the base of operations or the place of direction or control is in this state, then the entire services are covered in this state; or if some of the service is performed in this state and the

base of operations or point of control is not in any state in which some services were performed, and the individual's residence is in this state, then the entire remuneration for all services should be covered in this state. This provision, through its series of alternatives, attempts to provide for every possible contingency, and in point of fact, has solved all but the most unusual situations. 

Under this type of provision the first thing to determine is where the individual's services are localized. If A performs all his services in Illinois he should be covered in that state regardless of where he lives and regardless of where his base of operations, or point of control is, and regardless of where his employer's place of business is. Of course, if his employer does not have any other employees in Illinois he will not be subject to the Illinois law for contributions on the remuneration paid to A since the Illinois law requires eight or more individuals for coverage. But even though the employer need not pay Illinois contributions for the remuneration paid to A he cannot pay it to any other state. Either he must elect to cover A voluntarily in Illinois or he must pay the full three per cent federal unemployment tax, based on A's wages, to the federal government.

If an individual works as a salesman in Illinois all of the time, but is occasionally sent into Indiana to take an isolated order, it would be considered that his services were localized in Illinois. If, however, his services in Indiana were not merely incidental to his Illinois employment but were in the regular course of his duties and occupied a considerable portion of time, his services would not be localized in either state. It would, therefore, become necessary to determine where his base of operations was located and if it were in either Indiana or Illinois he would be covered in that state. 

The phrase "base of operations" used in unemployment compensation legislation bears an analogy to the same phrase as used in military terminology. It means, in general, that it is the place from which the individual begins his operations and to which he returns on their conclusion. It may not be the main office of his employer, but merely a branch to which he refers for instructions and from which he receives his supplies, information, etc. If there is no base of operations, then the state from which the service is directed or controlled is the state to which contributions based on the entire remuneration of the individual should be paid, provided, however, that some service is performed in that state. It is to be noted that in these previous alternatives the individual's residence is not a factor. His residence becomes important only if he performs some service in the state in which he resides and his services do not fall within any of the previous conditions. Thus, a salesman for a New York concern whose base of operations is New York in the sense that he returns there after every trip for instructions, even though he does no selling in that state, should be covered in

the state of his residence if he does some selling in that state, regardless of how many other states he may travel in. Firms may have individuals working for them, usually salesmen or building construction men, who have no fixed residence but live in whatever state they may be working in at the time. It is important, both from the employer's and the employee's point of view, to pay the required contributions to one state alone. Such a situation can be solved by the use of the principles given above. Since the individual's services are not localized in any state, all of his services should be covered in the state in which his base of operations is located. This usually refers to the base of operations of the employee rather than the employer, but where the employee has no fixed base of operations or residence it is probable that the correct state to pay contributions in, would be the state in which the employer's base of operations is located, provided also that the individual performs some services therein.

Many employers are still under the impression that it is not necessary to make contribution payments to any other state than the one in which they are located, regardless of where their employees work. This is erroneous since the state in which the employee works has jurisdiction over the performance of his services and his employer may be subject to that state's unemployment compensation law for any one of a number of reasons: If he has the required number of employees in that state for the necessary length of time, or in some states, as was mentioned previously, if the employer is subject to the Title IX tax even though he has only one employee in that state.

In the next chapter, which gives a tabular presentation of the most significant features of the state laws it will be noted that in the item "Coverage of Employers," provision is made by many of the states that employers may elect to cover residents of that state if they are not covered by the law of any other state, even though they performed no services in the state in which election is sought. 

It may not be extremely difficult to determine where an individual should be covered if it is known beforehand where he will work during that year. In many types of industry where it is common for an employee to work in a large number of states during a year, it is impossible to forecast accurately where the employee may be at any given time or how long he may remain there. Since contribution payments must be made to a state either monthly or quarterly during the current year, it may be impossible to tell before that year is up, whether that individual's services will be localized in any state or whether he will perform any services in the state in which his base of operations is located, or in the state in which he resides. This is particularly true of supervisory employees for large construction companies or for companies specializing in a particular type of construction which may require them

to work in many different states during a year. In such instances it would seem best that the employer pay contributions to the state in which his general offices are located until it is determined at some time in the year that the individual's services should be covered elsewhere. He can thereupon secure a refund from the state to which he originally paid and pay the proper state before January 31 of the succeeding year so as to insure his paying all necessary state contributions before the due date of filing his federal unemployment tax return, on which, of course, he will take credit for all payments he has made to state unemployment funds.
