A provision found in some state acts to take care of residual types of situations provides that contributions shall be paid on any services performed within the state on which contributions are not required and paid under the unemployment compensation law of any other state or of the federal government. This is clearly in the nature of a catch-all provision designed to insure unemployment compensation coverage for an individual by some state act. The point, however, must be re-emphasized that in all the foregoing discussion no employer is subject to the unemployment compensation law of any state unless he has within that state the required number of individuals performing services covered by that law for the necessary number of days or weeks.

The laws of Connecticut, District of Columbia, New Hampshire, and New York still have what was formerly the popular provision with regard to territorial coverage that all of an individual's services should be covered in that state in which all or the greater part of his services are customarily performed. The conditions of determining proper coverage with this kind of provision is multiplied tenfold, since what one state may think is the greater part of the services may not be agreeable to any other state in which the individual performs some part of his services. 

For example, an employee of a New Jersey firm may spend onethird of his time working in New Jersey and two-thirds of his time in New York. His base of operations may be New Jersey and his residence may be there. Under the usual type of provision which 

New Jersey also has, contributions for his entire services, no matter where performed, would be paid to New Jersey, where his base of operations is and where he performs some part of his services. 

New York, however, would have a valid claim based on its own law, that the contributions for this individual's entire services be paid to New York since the greater part of his services, in point of time, are performed in that state. Another difficulty arising from this type of provision is that involved in determining beforehand where the greater part of an individual's services will be performed in any given year. It seems probable that the few remaining states which do not have the "localized service" type of provision will either amend their acts to include it or will, through interstate agree-

ments, work out some practical method whereby an employer will not have to pay contributions on the wages of the same individual to more than one state.

An exception to the general rule that all or none of an employee's services should be covered in one state during any one calendar year may arise when an employer transfers an employee permanently to another state. Contributions on such an employee's remuneration after the permanent transfer should be made to the new state. However, if the employee is transferred to another state with the idea that the transfer is not to be permanent, even though it is of indefinite duration, the payment of contributions should continue to be made to the original state since it is considered that the services performed in the new state are merely incidental to the employment of that individual in the original state. Since questions of this sort may largely be decided upon the intent or motive which the employer may have had in making the transfer, it is best to secure an opinion from both the state agencies involved so that the danger of conflicting opinions can be overcome before the end of the calendar year.

Service performed outside of the territorial jurisdiction of the 

United States is excluded for purposes of the Title IX tax. Services outside the country may, however, be considered by a state to be merely incidental to services performed within that state and, therefore, taxable under the usual provision that all of an employee's remuneration should be subject to contribution if his services are localized in that state. Thus, employees who go to Canada, Mexico, or even Europe for short trips must be covered, for the length of time they are out of the country, in the state in which they are normally covered. The contributions paid on the remuneration earned while on such trips cannot be used for credit against the federal unemployment tax since it was not made with respect to services covered by Title IX. 

The jurisdiction of a state to require contributions is extraterritorial only to the extent that other jurisdictions may be willing to concede it. Thus, the federal government, which has prior jurisdiction over interstate commerce, has provided in the Social Security Act that no employer can raise the defense that he is not subject to a state unemployment compensation law on the ground that he is engaged in interstate commerce. However, land lying within the territorial confines of a state which has been ceded to the national government is, under the ordinary type of cession, outside that state's territorial jurisdiction and services performed thereon are not subject to the state unemployment compensation law. They may, however, be subject to the federal payroll taxes since they are within the jurisdiction of the United States.

Coverage of Employment Relationship. Since both the federal unemployment tax and state unemployment compensation contributions are based on wages paid or payable for employment, it is clear that the coverage of the state laws was intended to apply only to those organizations and individuals between whom there existed a definite relationship, known in law as "employment." 

The origin of the law of employment is that of the master-servant relationship, whereby the servant performed services for wages and under a contract of hire. The complexities of modern industrial life have brought about a corresponding complexity in the old law of masters and servants, and the attempt to fit old phrases and concepts to cover existing situations not even contemplated as long ago as fifty years, meant that such old phrases had to be expanded far beyond their former meanings. 

The Social Security Act, however, has not attempted to include more than what the old common law definition of employment covered. If the individual performing services is, as a matter of fact, free from direction and control over the manner of the performance of his services, the Bureau of Internal Revenue and the Social Security Board exclude him from coverage. This situation has been unsatisfactory because such individuals are just as prone to the hazards of unemployment and old age as others whose conduct may better happen to conform to the accepted standards of the employment relationship. The Social Security Board, in its report to the President and to Congress on proposed changes in the Social Security Act, issued in January, 1939, had this to say of the problem: "Old Age Insurance Coverage is at present limited by the undefined terms 'employer' and 'employee.' The Board recommends that this provision be expanded to the extent feasible to cover more of the persons who furnish primarily personal service. The intention of such an amendment would be to cover persons who are for all practical purposes employees but whose present legal status may not be that of an employee. At present, for example, insurance, real estate and travelling salesmen are sometimes covered and sometimes not; the Board believes that all such individuals should be covered.”

Elsewhere, the board recommended that the chance to broaden and clarify these terms should also be incorporated in the federal provisions for unemployment compensation.

The great majority of the state laws have attempted to broaden the coverage to include all persons performing primarily personal services and who are, in the words of the board, "for all practical purposes employees, but whose present legal status may not be that of an employee."

In some states this has been substantially accomplished; in others the tendency has been to follow rulings of the Bureau of Internal Revenue in questions of employer-employee relationship even though the definition of employment in those laws may differ from that in the federal Act. 

The regulations to Titles VIII and IX of the Social Security 

Act adopt the attitude of the common law on the employment relationship: namely, that although other elements may be important, the determining element in fixing the employment relationship is the right of control. All the states are in accord on this proposition but in addition most state laws have included two other elements to serve as coordinates with the "right of control" in determining the existence of an employment relationship. The usual provision of coverage in state laws will, therefore, read in effect as follows: 

"Employment means all service for remuneration unless- (1) the individual performing the service is free from control over the manner and method of performance; (2) the service is either outside the usual course of the business for which such service is being performed or else it is performed outside of all the places of business; and (3) the individual performing the services is engaged in an independent trade, occupation, profession or business." 

At common law an individual who performed personal services and was not an employee was called an independent contractor. It is to be noted that none of the state laws mention the phrase "independent contractor" but speak only of "employment" as defined in the law. Chapter VII of this edition will treat the question of employment and its payment in detail.

Excluded Employments. The exclusions from the definition of employment adopted in the state unemployment compensation laws are usually the same as the exclusions to the federal unemployment tax. Since the state laws and the federal unemployment tax are so closely bound together, the reason for such concurrence is obvious: credit can be taken for contributions paid into the state fund against the federal tax only for services covered by Title IX, and if a state were to exclude certain employments covered by the federal law, tax-payers in that state would still have to pay the federal tax on such employment and the state would get nothing. 

Every state jurisdiction, except the District of Columbia, has excluded agricultural labor from its definition of employment. The administrative difficulties involved in collecting contributions from farmers who live far from concentrated population areas, and the difficulties in paying benefits to agricultural workers caused the states to follow the federal example. Under the interpretations

of agricultural labor which the states adopted, it happened that many other employers than those who are strictly farmers, were included in that definition. Nurseries, florists, and large farms run on an industrial basis all receive the advantage of the exclusion. 

Two states, New York and Wisconsin, have limited the exclusion to employment as a farm laborer. This narrows the exclusion considerably but shifts the problem of definition from that of defining what agricultural labor is to that of defining what a farm is; and since neither problem can be decided by objective standards, the administrative difficulty still remains.

Every jurisdiction excludes from its coverage the performance of domestic service in a private home. New York, however, excludes domestic service only if less than four persons are employed therein fifteen days in the year.

The exclusion of services on a vessel operating on the navigable waters of the United States is one that would be as effective even if it were not listed. Under the Constitution of the United States, Congress has exclusive admiralty and maritime jurisdiction, and the states are powerless to deal with such matters even though Congress may attempt to grant them such powers. A similar type of exclusion is that of the federal government and its instrumentalities since a state could not require contributions based on such employment even though its law specifically sought to include them. Under the decision of McCulloch v. Maryland, and the many decisions handed down since, based on its reasoning, a state may not tax the federal government, its instrumentalities or its employees unless specific permission is given the state by Congress. 

California, Georgia, Illinois, Louisiana, Massachusetts, and Rhode

Island all contain a provision that the exclusion does not apply to federal instrumentalities if and when Congress allows payment of contributions by such instrumentalities under a state law. 

The states, furthermore, do not require contributions on the wages paid to state employees, with a few exceptions such as Ohio, which excludes only services performed in governmental functions, and Wisconsin, which includes as employment subject to the Act, certain types of temporary work performed for the state and for any of its political subdivisions.

Service performed for a child or for a spouse, and services of a child under 21 for its parent is excluded both under the federal tax and under forty-eight state laws. The reason for the exclusion is partly the administrative difficulties attached and partly the desire to prevent close relatives from concocting fictitious employment relationships so as to allow such relatives to draw unemployment compensation benefits. The exclusion applies only to sole proprietorships, since services performed for a partnership or corporation of which the close relative is a member are being done for a different employer.

The last exclusion which is common to all the states and the federal government is that of services performed for non-profit, religious, charitable, scientific, literary, educational, or humane organizations. Indiana excludes such organizations from the definition of employer unless services of a commercial character commonly performed for profit are involved. New Hampshire adds fraternal organizations to the list of those excluded, and many states, even though not listing fraternal organizations, have in effect excluded them through administrative interpretation. The wisdom of excluding such organizations when the services performed for them are merely ritual services or perfunctory parttime work is admitted; but the present feeling of many state agencies is to limit the exclusion of non-profit organizations as far as possible because of the fact that employees working for them can not justifiably be refused the same protection as that given employees of a commercial enterprise.

Many of the states exclude services which are covered under a congressional unemployment compensation act. There is at the present time only one such act, that dealing with railroad unemployment insurance. (See Chapter II.) It is probable, however, that Congress will in the near future provide an unemployment compensation system for maritime workers. This will, of course, make no difference in the administration of the state laws since maritime workers are not now covered by anyone. 

There are a number of other exclusions adopted by one or two states usually in response to organized pressure in the state legislature or to the feeling that the administrative difficulties involved in attempting to cover such employment are not worth the small advantages to be derived. Thus, Illinois, Michigan, New Jersey, and Ohio exempt minor students who are part-time workers, while Rhode Island includes them for the purpose of employer contributions but will not pay benefits to such individuals. The District of Columbia, Wisconsin, and Ohio exempt teachers and officers in schools and colleges. Connecticut, District of Columbia, Indiana, and Oregon exclude casual labor which is not in the course of the employer's business. This exclusion is found in the old-age benefits section of the Social Security Act but not in the federal unemployment titles. Nebraska and Illinois exclude such casual employment when it is used in certain types of emergencies. Ohio requires contributions based on the wages paid to extra workers employed for one day a week but excludes such individuals in determining whether an employer is subject to the Act. Even though contributions are required based on their services they are excluded from receiving unemployment benefits when they lose such work. 

Many state agencies have excluded insurance agents from coverage on the ground that they satisfy the three conditions on which is based the determination whether services for remunera-

tion are deemed to be "in employment:" namely, that they are free from direction and control, perform all their services outside the premises of the insurance company, and are independently established. This ruling conforms to the general opinions on insurance agents which have been issued by the Bureau of Internal Revenue. 

Seven states, however, exclude from their law various types of insurance company solicitors or agents, and in these states it is not necessary to establish that the above three conditions have been satisfied. Alabama excludes all insurance company solicitors or agents, while Louisiana, South Carolina, and Tennessee exclude such agents or representatives of insurance companies who are engaged in field work. Kentucky limits the exclusion to persons soliciting insurance not in the ordinary pursuit of their livelihood, while Michigan and Texas broaden it considerably by excluding all insurance agents on a commission basis. 

Indiana and Ohio exclude all services for remuneration performed on a commission basis where the employee is the master of his own time and efforts. This means in effect that these two states have adopted the old common law definition of employment and have furthermore included an element not usually considered as having more than slight evidentiary value, i.e., that the employee is on a commission rather than a straight salary basis. Florida contains a provision excluding from coverage under its act all serv ice which is excluded under Title IX of the Social Security Act, and thereby nullifies the standards set up in its other provision which defines "employment" as all services for remuneration unless the three conditions of freedom from control, service outside the premises, and an independent establishment, have been satisfied. 

Wisconsin excludes golf caddies, newsboys, service for railroads engaged in interstate commerce and logging operations. All of these types of services are covered by the federal unemployment tax. Employers having such services performed for them are, therefore, subject to the federal tax based on the total remuneration paid such employees without being able to pay to the state fund, unless an application for voluntary election has been granted. 

Chapter VI of the present edition includes a fuller statement as to the scope of the major exclusions and to typical situations included therein under the state laws. 

Wage Exclusions. The federal unemployment tax contains no provision similar to that of the old-age benefits tax which exempts all remuneration for services above the first $3,000 paid by an employer to an employee. The limitation to the first $3,000 of wages may be adopted in the near future, but at present, because of its absence from the federal unemployment tax, there has been no compelling reason for the states to adopt a like exclusion. As a matter of fact only two states, Michigan and New York, exclude

that part of an individual's wages in excess of $3,000 per year per employer. An employee earning over $3,000 per year in these two states gets credit for benefit purposes only up to the amount paid in for contribution purposes, i.e., $3,000 per year from each employer.

Vermont has a provision excluding salaries of over $3,000 or the excess of such salary over $3,000, on the governor's proclamation that Title IX of the Social Security Act has been interpreted or restricted to exclude such salaries or excess. 

Kentucky, New Jersey, and Rhode Island exclude contributions from employees over $3,000 per year per employer for contribution purposes only. Massachusetts has suspended all employee contributions for the period from July 1, 1938 to June 30, 1939, so that the effect of excluding employees altogether from contributions may be studied. If the Massachusetts agency decides that employee contributions should be resumed after June 30, 1939, the present provision of the act will take effect, but wages over $2,500 per year will not be subject to employee contributions. In New 

Hampshire employers' contributions on that part of wages in excess of $3,000 in any calendar year are to be paid only so long as such contributions may be credited against the federal unemployment tax. Wages in excess of $3,000 per individual per year are not included in the employer's account for merit rating purposes. 

Only one state, Wisconsin, excludes for benefit purposes, employees whom it covers for contribution purposes. Individuals employed by a given employer on a fixed salary of $150 or over per month in ten out of twelve months preceding unemployment; or persons who have earned $35.00 or over per week for forty-five out of the fifty-two weeks preceding unemployment are not entitled to benefits even though contributions have been paid by their employer based on their remuneration. These provisions are in the nature of a guaranteed employment plan since the payment of the wages specified above had to be guaranteed, in the sense that the remuneration had to be paid whether the employer did or did not have sufficient work to justify the payment of such wages. 

Contributions. By far the overwhelming trend as to the rate of contributions to the states is that imposing a tax of not more than 90 per cent of the federal unemployment tax. Since credit can be taken for state contributions only up to 90 per cent of the federal tax, which for 1938 and thereafter is 3 per cent of the employer's taxable payroll, the states have limited their rate of contribution to 2.7 per cent, thereby imposing a total tax liability on their employers of 3 per cent. Michigan and the District of 

Columbia require employers to pay contributions at the rate of 3 per cent of their payroll, and thereby impose on their employers a total unemployment tax of 3.3 per cent since the payment must

still be made to the federal government after the maximum deduction of 90 per cent of the federal tax is made. The New York rate is, in effect, 2.7 per cent for those employers who are liable to the federal unemployment tax. New York covers employers of four or more within fifteen days, extending their coverage beyond that of the federal law, and for those employers subject to the New York but not to the federal unemployment tax, the rate of contributions is 3 per cent.

Ten states at one time or another have required the payment of contributions from employees covered by their laws. By January, 1939, seven states still imposed employees' contributions, while one of those seven, Massachusetts, had suspended employee contributions for one year. Those states which tax employees for unemployment compensation purposes are Alabama, California, Kentucky, Louisiana, New Jersey, and Rhode Island.

The District of Columbia is the only jurisdiction whose law provides that the state make payments to its own unemployment fund. The District of Columbia law authorized Congress to appropriate the sum of $175,000 for the year 1938. This appropriation, however, had not been made up to the time Congress adjourned in 1938. 

Merit Rating. About forty of the fifty-one state jurisdictions have fixed a date, either specific or by inference, on which reduced rates because of merit rating may go into effect. Wisconsin began effective merit rating in January, 1938, while Vermont began in January, 1939, for those employers who had elected the reserve type of fund. The remaining employers' reserve fund states, Indiana, Nebraska, Oregon, South Dakota, and Kentucky, may begin merit rating sometime in 1940. The pooled fund states cannot start before January, 1941, and eleven states provide that their merit rating plans may go into operation then. By January, 1943, with the beginning of merit rating in Florida and Illinois, all the states presently providing for reduction of rates through stabilized employment will be in full force.

Merit rating is a device peculiar to unemployment compensation in America. It seeks to reward the employer who has succeeded in stabilizing his employment, and thereby in some measure or degree lessening unemployment. For his success in doing so, the employer is permitted to pay a lesser rate of contributions on his payroll than other employers who have failed to so stabilize employment. Such a reduction would be worthless unless the federal unemployment tax allowed the amount of contributions saved to be used as a credit against the federal tax. This is provided for in 

Sections 909 and 910 of the Social Security Act. Subsection (a) of Section 909 reads as follows:

"Allowance of Additional Credit: 

"In addition to the credit allowed under Section 902, a taxpayer may, subject to the conditions imposed by Section 910, credit against the tax imposed by Section 901 for any taxable year after the taxable year 1937, an amount, with respect to each state law, equal to the amount, if any, by which the contributions, with respect to employment in such taxable year, annually paid by the taxpayer under such law before the date of filing his return for such taxable year, is exceeded by whichever of the following is the lesser- (1) The amount of contributions which he would have been required to pay under such law for such taxable year if he had been subject to the highest rate applicable from time to time throughout such year to any employer under such law; or (2) Two and seven-tenths per centum of the wages payable by him with respect to employment with respect to which contributions for such year were required under such law." 

To insure that the state laws would not grant reductions indiscriminately, or on insufficient experience, Section 910 of the Social Security Act provides the conditions on which such additional credit allowances will be made. If a state law does not conform with these conditions any savings through reductions in rates which the state may grant will be ineffective since the employer will be allowed to credit against his federal tax only the amount he has actually paid into the state fund. The Social Security Board must be satisfied that a state with a pooled fund type of law will allow reductions only on the basis of not less than three years of compensation experience. On page 52 in Chapter 

III the problem of what is a year of compensation experience was treated and the question raised as to whether a successor in interest will be entitled to make use of the "years of compensation experience" of his predecessor.

A state with a separate reserve type of law may not permit a reduction in rates unless: (a) Compensation has been payable from the employer's account throughout the preceding calendar year; (b) such employer's account amounts to not less than five times the largest amount of compensation paid from his account within any one of the three preceding calendar years; and (c) his account amounts to not less than 7.5 per cent of the total wages payable by him with respect to employment in the preceding calendar year in such state. 

Additional federal credit allowances will be granted under those state laws which provide for a guaranteed employment reserve ac-

count only if the lower rate is permitted on the fulfillment of the employer's guaranty of employment in the preceding calendar year, and only if the guaranteed employment account amounts to not less than 7.5 per cent of the total wages payable by him, in accordance with such guaranty with respect to employment in the preceding calendar year.

One of the requirements for the allowance of the normal federal credit is that unemployment compensation benefits cannot be paid until two years have elapsed from the accrual of contributions under such law. In a pooled fund type of law a year of compensation experience can begin only when benefits first become payable, and since three years of compensation experience are required, no merit rating is possible in the pooled fund type of law for at least five years after the accrual of contributions. It is possible for merit rating to begin in less than five years in the employer's reserve type of law since an employer's account may amount, in less than that period, to 7.5 per cent of the total wages payable by him in the preceding calendar year. To achieve such a ratio the state law must have been in effect at least four years since it would take that length of time, under the present rates of contribution, to pay in contributions amounting to 7.5 per cent of the annual payroll. Wisconsin was able to permit merit rating in less than four years after the Social Security Act was passed only because its unemployment compensation law became effective, and contributions were paid thereunder before the passage of the federal Act.

In most of the states providing for merit rating the rates for a given calendar year will be based on experience up to the beginning of such year. This will give the state agency no lag period within which it may gather the data and make the necessary computations to determine whether a given employer will be allowed a reduction in rates. Ohio, Connecticut, and Kentucky allow for a lag period in which the necessary data can be analyzed and the computations made. California provides that the rate shall fluctuate according to the employer's experience, except as the agency prescribes reasonable periods during which the rates are to remain unchanged. 

Whether an employer has succeeded in stabilizing his employment is answered by a determination of the agency that an employer has succeeded in maintaining a certain reserve in his account. This reserve must achieve a fixed ratio to his average annual payroll, so that it may be certain that a sudden spell of unemployment among his workers will not deplete his account too rapidly.. 

To determine what an employer's reserve ratio is, the state agency must keep records with respect to two items: (1) The contributions which the employer pays into the fund, and (2) the benefits which are properly chargeable to him because they have been paid out to former employees of his. This latter item indicates his suc-

cess or failure in stabilizing employment, since if he has been able to do so, fewer of his employees will be entitled to demand benefits. 

It is because of the necessity for these two items of information that all but one of the state laws provide that the agency shall maintain employers' accounts, to which shall be credited the contributions and against which there shall be charged the benefits payable to former workers. Pennsylvania is the only state that does not provide for employers' accounts. Even state laws which have no present provision for merit rating require employers' accounts to be maintained, so that if merit rating is adopted at some time in the future, the proper basis will have been laid for a determination of the rates.

The administrative difficulties involved in keeping the necessary records for employers' accounts may well prove insuperable. 

If any one thing means the success or failure of the usual plan for merit rating in the state laws, it is the proper maintenance of the employers' accounts, involving charging of employees' benefits against those accounts as they are paid. Very few of the states now paying benefits make any attempt to charge benefits paid to employees against their former employers' accounts, and if this condition keeps on for any length of time it will mean that merit rating, on the basis of individual employers' experience, will be doomed to failure. Employers and state administrators who are cognizant of this situation, and who are in favor of merit rating, have suggested various methods for overcoming the administrative difficulties involved in keeping track of individual employers' accounts. The novel element of such proposals is to rate employers not only on the basis of their individual experience but also on the experience of all employers, comparing any individual employer's success in stabilizing employment with the average success of all employers at the same task. A number of states are considering changing their merit rating plans to include a combination of individual and state-wide experience and thereby do away with the necessity of making detailed charges of all benefits paid to an employer's former workers. This plan is known as "experience rating" and will likely be widely adopted. 

The method of calculating an employer's reserve ratio is to divide the balance in an employer's account by his average annual payroll. The difference between the contributions an employer has paid in and the benefits which have been charged against his account constitute his balance, and this, when divided by his annual payroll, gives a percentage ratio which is the prime factor in determining whether that employer's rate shall be reduced. A state may credit an employer either with all the contributions he has paid in, or else with a certain portion of them. Only contributions in excess of 1 per cent of the annual payroll are credited to the employers' accounts in Arizona, Arkansas, New Mexico, North Dakota,

Tennessee, West Virginia, and Wyoming. In the year 1938, and thereafter, this means that only 1.7 per cent of an employer's payroll will be credited to his account for merit rating purposes, and his reserve ratio will thereby tend to be lower since the credit in his account will be less than if all his contributions were credited thereto.

In these states the balance in the employer's account is divided by his annual payroll averaged over the last three or five years, whichever is higher. The fact that the higher average annual payroll is used means that the employer's reserve ratio will be lowered because the higher the annual payroll to be used as a divisor, the lower will the quotient or reserve ratio be. In Connecticut only five-sixths of all past contributions are credited to the employers' accounts and the excess of this portion of the contributions over all past benefits is divided by the annual payroll averaged over the last three calendar years, to give the reserve ratio. Michigan uses four-fifths of all past contributions as the credit to the employer's account, and the balance remaining after all benefits are charged, is to be divided by one-third of the total wages earned during the last three calendar years. Except for Utah, the remaining states with pooled funds compute the reserve ratio by dividing the balance in the employer's account by the annual payroll averaged over the last three or five years, whichever is the higher. In New Hampshire wages over $3,000 payable to any individual in any calendar year are excluded from the annual payroll for the purposes of calculating the reserve ratio. California, Louisiana, New Hampshire, and New

Jersey do not include employee contributions for the purpose of determining the balance in their employers' accounts. 

In states with an employer's reserve fund or a combination pooled and employer's reserve fund, the formula for calculating the reserve ratio is to divide all, or some portion, of past contributions minus all past benefits, by the payroll for the preceding year. Kentucky and Wisconsin credit the employer's account with all his past contributions while Indiana and South Dakota credit him with five-sixths of the amounts he has paid in and Oregon credits his account with all contributions in excess of one-half of 

1 per cent. Nebraska and Vermont both provide that the reserve ratio is calculated by dividing the balance in the employer's account by his payroll for the preceding calendar year, but they have no further provisions on what reserve ratio is necessary for a reduction in rates.

The remaining states provide that the administrative agency is to determine the rates of contribution and fix the limits within which the rates may vary. Alabama, for example, provides for the standard rate of 2.7 per cent with a minimum of 1.5 per cent and a maximum of 4 per cent. There are no specifications as to what reserve ratio must be maintained in order to attain these rates.

The standards which the states have set up as to what reserve ratios will entitle an employer to reduced rates vary widely. Fourteen states provide that if the reserve is between 7.5 and 10 per cent, computed as described above, the rate of contributions shall be reduced to 1.8 per cent; if the reserve is 10 per cent or over, the rate is to be .9 per cent; and in Delaware, Illinois, and Utah if the reserve is 12.5 per cent or over, the rate is reduced to zero, while in Missouri the rate is reduced to zero only if the reserve ratio contains 15 per cent or over. The tabular material in the next chapter gives the reserve ratio necessary for reduction of rates in every state.

In the employer's reserve funds no rate can be decreased unless the reserve equals at least five times the largest amount of benefits charged in any one of the three preceding years. This requirement, of course, is to comply with the standard set for this type of fund in the Social Security Act's allowance for additional federal credit. In addition to the above requirement, each of the employers' reserve fund states sets its own standard as to the reserve ratio necessary for a reduction in rates. These also can be found listed under each state in the following chapter. 

Arizona, Arkansas, Colorado, Florida, New Hampshire, North

Dakota, and Wyoming provide that no rate shall be less than 2 per cent unless the assets in the fund are twice the benefits paid in the preceding year. Illinois has a much more liberal provision, so far as the employer is concerned, in that an employer's rate can be reduced to zero only if the total assets of the fund exceed the benefits paid in the preceding year.

Although it is not a necessary corollary to the reduction in rates for regularized employment, many states felt that employers who signally fail to regularize their employment should be penalized through imposition of a higher rate. Since such employers will prove to be a loss to the unemployment fund and will tend to drain it, increased rates were provided to counteract the loss of funds caused by the reduction in rates for successful stabilizers of employment and to provide an additional incentive for attempting to stabilize. In addition to an employer's individual experience, some of the states provide that no employer's rate can be reduced below the standard rate of 2.7 per cent unless the condition of the state fund as a whole is favorable in the sense that unexpected drains on the fund will not threaten its solvency. In the sixteen states containing a provision of this sort, eight provide that no employers' rate shall be less than 2.7 per cent unless the fund's entire assets exceed the benefits paid in the preceding year, and no rate shall be less than 1.8 per cent unless the assets are twice such benefits. 

Increases in rates are dependent only on the employer's own account. In no state does the condition of the fund, as a whole, have any bearing on whether an employer's rate shall be increased.

As was previously mentioned, certain proposed plans for merit rating provide that the condition of the state fund compared with the employer's own experience shall determine whether the employer's rate should be either increased or reduced. Twenty-seven states provide for an increase in rates and in seventeen of these states the maximum rate is to be 3.6 per cent. Alabama, Nevada, and Washington may increase rates at the discretion of the administrative agency but no maximum is set. It is, however, very unlikely that the rates set will ever rise to more than 4 per cent of the employer's payroll.

The calculation of an employer's reserve ratio depends to a large extent on how benefits are charged, that is, whether an employee's benefits are to be charged against his most recent employer completely or against more than one employer, or whether the full amount or only some portion of the benefits paid are to be charged against some employer's account. Most of the states specify that benefits shall be charged against the most recent employers in the inverse chronological order of employment without providing in what proportion such recent employers are each to be charged.

Michigan and Missouri are the only two states providing that all benefits to be charged are to be against the account of the last employer. California specifies that benefits are to be charged against the accounts of all employers during the first eight out of the last nine calendar quarters preceding an individual's benefit year, in proportion to the wages which the individual has earned from each employer. Those remaining states which treat the problem at all provide that the charges are to be made against the most recent and next most recent employer only. Any provision which states that any one but the most recent employer is to be charged tends to vitiate the essential element in the present system of merit rating-that it is the employer's own experience in hiring and firing employees which determines his rates. It is easy to see that if an employer is charged with benefits to former employees of his for whose leaving he was not at all responsible, his efforts to stabilize his own employment will not insure a reduced rate since employees who left of their own accord to take other positions from which they are then dismissed, may be entitled to benefits which will be charged against his account. It is the necessity of charging employees' benefits against others than his most recent employer that accounts for some of the difficulty in administering the merit rating provision. 

Benefits. The present American system of unemployment compensation benefits is based on the fundamental assumption that an individual's benefits should, in some way, be connected with his past earnings in insured employment. This is not necessarily the only method by which an employee's benefits may be determined,

and, as a matter of fact, most European systems of unemployment benefits are based both on previous earnings and on the inclusion in certain broad categories, such as sex, age, or number of dependents. For example, the rate of benefits payable in England is greater for men than for women, even though each may have earned the same amount. The rate of contributions is also dependent, to some extent, on sex although wage categories are also set up with a flat amount set for all wages earned within the category. The American attempt to maintain a close relationship between wages earned or length of time worked and the rate and duration of benefits has meant a considerable amount of record keeping which is not necessary under an unemployment system based on other more easily determinable factors. 

All state unemployment compensation acts attempt to relate an individual's rate of benefits to his full-time weekly wage, or the amount of wages earned for a week of full-time work in his normal occupation or trade. The determination of the full-time weekly wage may be based either on an individual's actual wage rate or on a formula designed to determine more simply what his average earnings are. The formula very commonly used is that the full-time weekly wage is to be one-thirteenth of the wages earned in that quarter in which such wages were highest in a certain defined period. This may be either the base period (that number of quarters in which wages earned therein are used to determine the duration of benefits), or it may be any other period that the Act may designate. A state may wish to use a period other than the base period by which to determine that quarter in which an individual's wages were highest, and it will usually be a certain number of quarters preceding the date on which an employee registers for benefits. For example, in Illinois, the full-time weekly wage is one-thirteenth of the earnings in the highest quarter among the first eight out of the last nine completed quarters immediately preceding whatever date the agency may prescribe. It is probable that the date so prescribed will be the date on which the employee registers as unemployed but it may, on the other hand, be a certain fixed date, such as April 1, in any year. If the latter method is used it means that the full-time weekly wage of all employees will be determined on the basis of the same period of employment, the first two out of the last two and one-quarter years. The use of the one-thirteenth formula of earnings in the high quarter means that the employee's rate of benefits is based on his most favorable period of employment and thus tends to raise his weekly benefit amount. However, if his weekly benefit amount is raised it will in general mean that it will be more difficult for him to qualify for benefits since he must usually earn a certain number of times his weekly benefit amount in order to be eligible. A high weekly benefit amount also tends to lessen an individual's duration of bene-

fits since that is dependent usually on his total earnings in his base period instead of on earnings in a high quarter.

The District of Columbia, Ohio, and South Carolina determine the full-time weekly wage as the product of his weekly hours and his hourly earnings. This involves extensive calculation and record keeping, thereby increasing the administrative work of the agency and slowing down the payment of benefits.

The weekly benefit amount is the rate of benefits which an unemployed individual is entitled to receive for one week of total unemployment. In all states but those listed below, the weekly benefit amount is figured as 50 per cent of the full-time weekly wage, and in all cases, with a definite maximum. The District of 

Columbia fixes the weekly benefit amount at 40 per cent of the fulltime weekly wage, plus 10 per cent for a dependent spouse and 5 per cent for each dependent relative, not to exceed, however, a 

65 per cent maximum. In Connecticut and Indiana the weekly benefit amount is 4 per cent of the total wages during the quarter of highest earnings among the first four out of the last five completed calendar quarters. The same rate is used in Kansas, Michigan, and 

Missouri, except that the period in which the highest quarter is to be found is the first eight of the last nine completed calendar quarters. This is approximately 50 per cent of the full-time weekly wage since it amounts to 1/25th instead of 1/26th of the earnings in the high quarter. In New Hampshire, Massachusetts, and Mississippi the weekly benefit amount is computed as 1/26th of the highest quarterly earnings in the period selected. Wyoming allows a rate of benefits at 60 per cent of the full-time weekly wage.

The maximum weekly benefit amount in most states is fixed at $15.00 for each week of total unemployment. Michigan allows a $16.00 per week maximum and Louisiana and Wyoming fix the maximum at $18.00 per week. In most states the minimum weekly benefit amount is $5.00 or three-fourths of the full-time weekly wage, whichever is the lesser. Three-fourths of the full-time weekly wage will be less than $5.00 only if the full-time weekly wage itself is less than $6.67. Virginia has a flat minimum weekly benefit amount of $3.00, Pennsylvania one of $7.50, New York and 

California $7.00, Massachusetts, New Jersey, Hawaii, and Delaware $5.00. In these states, if an employee is eligible at all he is entitled to this minimum amount per week regardless of how low his full-time weekly wage is.

The most common method of calculating the duration of benefits during unemployment is that of permitting the unemployed individual to receive as a maximum during his benefit year a given percentage of his earnings during his base period. The last term refers to a specified period during which the employee's wage credits are cumulated and on the basis of which the total amount of benefits he is entitled to receive is fixed. The amount of wages

credited to an individual in each quarter of his base period is always limited, so that an automatic maximum is achieved by fixing the duration as a percentage of his wage credits. A majority of the states allow an employee maximum wage credits of $390 per quarter, and then provide that for purposes of determining the duration of benefits only one-sixth of the wage credits may be used. An employee who earns more than $390 per quarter will, however, be limited to one-sixth of $390, or $65.00 per quarter, to be used as benefit credits. In states with an eight quarter base period an individual's maximum benefit credits will be sixty-five times eight, or $520, the amount he would be entitled to receive in benefits if there were no other limitations. There is, however, an alternative limitation of so many times his weekly benefit amount in most states. For example, in a state where the maximum weekly benefit amount is $15.00 and the maximum duration sixteen times $15.00, an eligible individual whose weekly benefit amount was $15.00, would be entitled to receive only $240 in benefits during his benefit year, even though he had accumulated wage credits in his base period of $390 per quarter, entitling him to benefit credits of $520.

Many states permit an employee to use wage credits earned during his benefit year, thus allowing him a flexible or extensible base period and permitting him to receive a greater duration of benefits if his previous wage credits have been exhausted before he has received the maximum duration permitted. The base period is most commonly the first eight of the last nine completed calendar quarters preceding the benefit year with the proviso mentioned above, in many states, of permitting the base period to be extended, so that the unemployed individual may use later wage credits to increase his duration of benefits. It is probable that the wage credits earned in a quarter falling in an individual's benefit year, which are used to increase the duration, will not be used to redetermine the original weekly benefit amount. Forty-six of the fifty-one state laws base the duration of benefits on earnings during a specified period, thereby making each individual's duration dependent on his own wage experience. Ohio is the only state at present which has a fixed duration, that is, each individual who becomes eligible for benefits is automatically entitled to receive benefits for a period of sixteen weeks, provided always, of course, that he remains unemployed and otherwise eligible during that period. Fixed duration of benefits is likely to become more popular in the near future because it is simpler to administer, in that the only determination that must be made is whether or not the individual has satisfied the eligibility requirements. Under the present system of basing duration on each individual's earnings, additional computations must be made as to his wage credits in each quarter of his base period and for the charging of benefits against those wage credits until he has reached the maximum allowed.

Four states fix duration not on the basis of earnings but on that of weeks of employment occurring within a specified number of preceding weeks. 

South Carolina permits one week of benefits for each four weeks of employment within the last one hundred and four weeks. 

Hawaii uses the same ratio but allows the worker an additional week of benefits to every twenty weeks of employment within two hundred and sixty weeks. The District of Columbia uses a period of one hundred and four weeks of employment but allows a greater duration since it gives one week of benefits for every three weeks of employment. Wisconsin's ratio is one to four within the past fifty-two weeks with respect to any employer's account. However, no employer's account is liable for benefits for unemployment occurring over twenty-six weeks after employee's last week of employment with such employer.

The maximum duration of benefits within an individual's benefit year varies widely from state to state. Alabama, California, Idaho, and Rhode Island allow an individual to draw up to twenty times his weekly benefit amount, provided, of course, that he has earned sufficient wage credits in his base period to entitle him to such a maximum. The most usual provision as to maximum duration is that of allowing sixteen times the weekly benefit amount within the benefit year, that period of fifty-two consecutive weeks following on an individual's first week of benefits. From this maximum, the state provisions range downward to Missouri and West Virginia, which provide for a maximum duration of only twelve times the weekly benefit amount. South Carolina has the same maximum duration but allows additional benefits on the basis of long periods of past employment. The other two states permitting the maximum duration to be extended because of high earnings or long periods of continuous past employment are the District of Columbia and Massachusetts.

The determination of when an individual is unemployed so that he may draw benefits, if otherwise eligible, is usually not a difficult one to make. In most states the definition of total unemployment given is that an individual is totally unemployed during any week in which he performs no services and with respect to which no remuneration is payable to him. A frequent question arising from this definition is whether dismissal wages constitute remuneration payable for weeks in which the individual performs no services. For contribution purposes a dismissal wage may be considered as remuneration for past periods of employment or as a bonus or special payment for the dismissal. Whether a dismissal wage should be treated exactly as wages in lieu of notice for purposes of disqualification is a problem on which the states disagree. 

Some regard it as being the same as wages in lieu of notice and, therefore, disqualify the unemployed individual for benefits in those

weeks in which he is receiving such payments; other states, however, refuse to regard it as anything more than a special payment for past employment whose receipt will not disqualify him for benefits.

If the unemployed individual receives earnings from odd jobs or subsidiary work he may still be considered as totally unemployed in many states if the earnings derived through such work is below a certain amount. Whether an individual loses the status of being totally unemployed if his earnings from odd jobs or subsidiary work exceeds the amount allowed by the state act is, as yet, a matter of disagreement between states. Some states regard any work which gives him more than the minimum amount, as services performed which will exclude the individual from the status of one who is totally unemployed, while other states will merely subtract from the individual's benefit amount for total unemployment that part of his wages which is in excess of the minimum amount allowed. There is a huge administrative difficulty in procuring this type of information from individuals coming in to register for benefits, especially when the individual knows that if he has earned more than $3.00 or so, he will not be entitled to benefits at all. The preferable way of handling this problem would be to deduct from the individual's weekly benefit amount any amount he earns from odd jobs and subsidiary work in excess of two or three dollars per week and thereby not penalize him for accepting small jobs. 

At the present time forty-four states provide benefits when an individual is partially unemployed. The states not providing for partial benefits are Massachusetts, Mississippi, Montana, Nebraska, New Jersey, New York, and Pennsylvania. The determination of when an individual is partially unemployed usually involves a determination of the relationship between his present earnings and his weekly benefit amount. If he earns less than his weekly benefit amount for total unemployment, he is considered partially unemployed in any week of less than full-time work in the following states: California, Connecticut, Georgia, Indiana, Kentucky, Louisiana, Virginia, and Wisconsin. In Alabama an incentive of $1.00 is given the partially unemployed individual, in that he will be entitled to partial benefits if his earnings are less than his weekly benefit amount, plus $1.00. A few other states allow him an incentive of $2.00. In most states, however, no flat amount is provided for as an incentive but an individual is deemed to be partially unemployed in any week in which he earns less than sixfifths of his weekly benefit amount for total unemployment.

Once it is determined that an individual is partially unemployed his rate for partial benefits must be fixed. In California, Indiana, and Wisconsin the partial benefit is the amount necessary to equal his weekly benefit amount for total unemployment when added to his actual earnings for that week. All the other states,

however, which provide for partial benefits allow the worker to draw more than his weekly benefit amount when he is partially unemployed by giving him a partial benefit which, when added to his actual earnings, will exceed his weekly benefit amount by a definite figure. In some states this amounts to $2.00 more than his weekly benefit amount, while most of the remaining states will allow him to earn, through actual wages and his partial benefit, an amount which is one-sixth larger than his actual earnings. Thus, if an individual is partially unemployed and is earning $6.00 a week, while his weekly benefit amount is $10.00 per week, he will receive a total of $11.00, that is, $4.00 representing the difference between his actual earnings and his weekly benefit amount, and his actual earnings of $6.00, plus one-sixth of such earnings, or $1.00. This formula is more easily expressed by saying that a partially unemployed individual is entitled to a partial benefit of the difference between his weekly benefit amount and five-sixths of his wages.

Besides qualifying as partially or totally unemployed an individual must satisfy certain other conditions before he will be eligible for benefits. He must, first of all, serve a waiting period of either two or three weeks within a certain specified number of weeks. Most states provide that he must serve a waiting period of two weeks in a period of thirteen consecutive weeks. Other states permit the waiting period to be spread over twenty-six or even fifty-two weeks. Those states requiring the serving of three waiting period weeks within thirteen usually provide, however, that not more than five waiting period weeks need be served in either a fiftytwo or sixty-five consecutive week period. This takes care of the situation of intermittent unemployment so that an individual need not serve the full waiting period every time he becomes unemployed in his benefit year. 

In Louisiana and Wisconsin a week of partial unemployment counts as a week of total unemployment for purposes of the waiting period. But in a great majority of the states it requires two weeks of partial unemployment to equal one waiting period week. Minnesota figures three weeks of partial unemployment as being equivalent to one week of total unemployment for this purpose. California, Michigan, and South Carolina require no waiting period for partial unemployment benefits, although Michigan will not pay such benefits until the end of the quarter in which the partial unemployment occurred.

In addition to serving a specified waiting period, forty-five states require that an individual to be eligible for benefits must have earned a certain minimum amount as wages for covered employment within a specified period immediately preceding the benefit year. Such eligibility requirements vary, from Connecticut where the unemployed individual must have earned twenty-four times his

weekly benefit amount in the first four out of the last five completed calendar quarters, to twelve times the weekly benefit amount required in West Virginia. Usually the amount required to be earned for eligibility purposes is exactly the same as the amount which the state will pay as a maximum in benefits. For example, if the state has a maximum duration in benefits of sixteen times the weekly benefit amount it will also require that the unemployed individual have earned the same amount in covered employment during the period specified which is, in most states, the first three out of the last four completed calendar quarters immediately preceding the week with respect to which the individual claims benefits. Michigan, Indiana, Kentucky, New Hampshire, Massachusetts, and California fix their earnings for eligibility purposes at a flat amount instead of as a percentage or ratio to past earnings. The requirement of a minimum flat amount of eligibility for benefits tends to deprive those who are not attached to the labor market of benefits since they will find it harder to qualify through this method than through the former method. 

In all states before an individual is eligible for benefits he must, in addition to the foregoing requirements, have registered for work at a public employment office or through such other method as the agency may prescribe and he must be available for work and able to work. An individual who has been forced to leave his employment because of ill health is, therefore, not en titled to benefits even though he has satisfied all the other conditions for eligibility. Such a requirement, while it appears to be stringent, is necessary since the essential purpose of unemployment compensation must be borne in mind: that these benefits are to be paid for enforced unemployment occurring through cyclical or seasonal causes over which the worker has no control. 

Disqualifications for Benefits. Even though an individual may be eligible for benefits in all respects, he may be disqualified either totally or for a variable period if it is found that the manner of his leaving his former employment was one that the state law attempts to penalize. These disqualifications are practically uniform in all states. For example, if an employee leaves his employment voluntarily without good cause he will be disqualified for the receipt of benefits. Such disqualification may be either for a flat number of weeks not to be varied by the agency, or for a varying number depending on the agency's attitude toward the turpitude involved. In Wisconsin an individual is barred from all benefit rights based on past employment with the employer he has left voluntarily, and is further ineligible for benefits from previous employers' accounts for the week in which he has left and the next four weeks. Pennsylvania disqualifies him for every week in which his unemployment is due because of his voluntary leaving, unless

the reason for his leaving was that his union rights were infringed upon or he was forced to accept wages, hours or working conditions not desired by the majority of the workers in the establishment or occupation where he was engaged. Ohio and Massachusetts penalize the employee who leaves work voluntarily by reducing his duration of benefits, in addition to requiring that he wait an additional period before he may receive such benefits. 

The greater number of states, however, do not impose a flat disqualification period but permit the agency to determine it within a minimum and maximum limit. Washington, Oregon, and Connecticut provide that the maximum period of disqualification for voluntary leaving shall be the week in which the leaving occurred and the next two weeks. In Hawaii, Michigan, and Kentucky there may be a maximum disqualification period of nine weeks. New 

York is the only state which has no disqualification for voluntary leaving.

What is a voluntary leaving which will subject an employee to disqualification for benefits is a question of fact to be decided by each state agency on whatever basis it deems most fair and equitable, bearing in mind the purposes of the act. It has been held by one appeals tribunal that an individual who is dismissed, prior to the expiration of a notice that he is quitting, does not leave his work voluntarily. If the employee leaves for trivial reasons to accept other work or fails to report for work he will be considered to have left voluntarily and will, therefore, be disqualified. If an employee leaves because of his health he will not be considered to have left voluntarily without good cause if the condition of his health was such that it would have been dangerous or inadvisable to continue at the work he was doing. 

Another disqualification for benefits is because of discharge for misconduct connected with the employer's work. All states but Pennsylvania, contain a provision providing for some disqualification for this reason. Here again, a few states provide for a fixed number of weeks of disqualification while others permit the agency to determine, within limits, the weeks for which he should be penalized because of his misconduct. Absence without leave will be considered misconduct if it was done for trivial reasons or is done habitually without giving the employer sufficient notice. Carelessness, negligence or mistakes connected with the work is not of itself misconduct unless it is habitually repeated or else is considered to be caused by a wilful disregard of the employer's interest. Inefficiency will not be deemed to be misconduct unless the employee's inefficiency is inexcusable, in the sense that he could do better but refuses to do so, or else that he makes no effort to rectify his errors. Dishonesty connected with the employment or misrepresentation will usually disqualify an employee on the grounds of misconduct. Drunkenness on the job is misconduct, but

if the discharge occurs because of drunkenness or unseemly conduct outside of business hours it will not serve to disqualify the individual, unless such bad conduct is considered to reflect on the employer's business or standing in the community. Habitual or unexplained tardiness and violation of company rules and regulations will be considered misconduct when prior warnings to correct such errors have been given by the employer. It must be recognized that any statement as to what is or is not misconduct is subject to many qualifications and shadings of fact, and what might be considered misconduct for one employee under one set of facts may very well be treated by another state as conduct which is reprehensible but not so serious as to warrant a disqualification for benefits. 

Since unemployment compensation benefits are paid primarily for enforced unemployment, it is uniformly considered by every state that an employee who refuses to accept work which has been offered to him, and which is deemed suitable by the state agency, should be disqualified for benefits. Missouri goes so far as to cancel all benefit rights based on past employment for such refusal, and Delaware, New York, Ohio, and Wisconsin impose a disqualification for the duration of the unemployment caused through such refusal. The problem presented for agencies facing this type of disqualification is whether the work offered is suitable employment, having due regard to the health, safety, and morals of the individual concerned. The factors usually gone into to determine whether the work offered is suitable are a comparison between what is offered and what the employee's experience and training might reasonably expect him to get; whether the wages offered for the new job are at least as good as the wages for similar work in the same locality; whether the employment offered is too far from the employee's residence; and whether the working conditions at the employment offered are such as to make it unbearable for the particular claimant. 

The most important of all the disqualifications is that imposing a penalty period on individuals who are unemployed due to stoppage of work because of a labor dispute in the plant or establishment where he is, or was last employed. New York imposes a disqualification of ten weeks for loss of employment due to an industrial controversy unless it was settled before the close of such period. Louisiana also imposes a disqualification of ten weeks, unless neither the individual claiming benefits nor any member of the grade or class to which he belongs is participating or directly interested in the dispute. Rhode Island limits the disqualification to eight weeks and Pennsylvania to three. All the other states provide that the disqualification shall last as long as the dispute is in progress. However, even though the dispute may still be in progress, if there is no substantial stoppage of work no disquali-

fication will ensue except for those who are still out on strike. 

The disqualification in any event does not apply to an individual thrown out of work because of a labor dispute if neither he nor any member of the grade or class to which he belongs is participating in, financing, or otherwise directly interested in the dispute. Some states provide for a disqualification in all cases of a labor dispute even though no substantial stoppage of work results. It is unlikely that the state agencies will extend such an interpretation since it will penalize workers who are laid off due to lack of work when a strike is in progress in their plant in which they have no interest at all. The uniformity of the disqualification for benefits in the event of labor disputes is explained by the desire of the agency to be impartial in such matters by leaving the parties to the dispute in exactly the same position they would be if there were no unemployment compensation law. To pay benefits in the case of unemployment due to a labor dispute would be, in effect, a subsidy of the state to help sustain strikers in their controversy with their employer.

The receipt of certain types of remuneration during periods of unemployment will serve to disqualify an individual in most states for the weeks involved. The types of remuneration generally listed are wages in lieu of notice, workmen's compensation for temporary partial disability, and old-age benefits under Title II 

of the Social Security Act, or for similar payments under any act of Congress. A smaller number of states also provide that the receipt of unemployment compensation benefits under the law of any other state or of any federal law will also serve to disqualify for the receipt of benefits from the state concerned. The distinction between dismissal wages and wages in lieu of notice for purposes of disqualification is vague and has been a source of difficulty to many state agencies. A dismissal wage is presumably payment for the fact of separation and may or may not be accompanied by the employer giving notice that the employment will soon be terminated. Technically, it should not serve to disqualify an individual since the laws speak only of wages in lieu of notice and these are considered to be only such amounts as are given an employee on immediate separation, in return for a failure to give some notice of an expected termination of employment. Employers, however, usually fail to see any real distinction between these two types of payments and consider that the receipt of any remuneration on separation from employment should disqualify the employee for the number of weeks after the separation to which the payment may be allocated. A good argument can be made, however, that receipt of such remuneration should not disqualify him from benefits but should merely serve to reduce the amount of benefits for those weeks.

Reciprocal Arrangements Between States. It has long been recognized that it is almost impossible to administer a state unemployment compensation law as if only one state were involved. 

The mobility of labor in America, and the fact that industry may easily operate without regard to state lines, have made it imperative for the states to enter into arrangements between themselves to better effectuate the general purposes of unemployment compensation. Such agreements between the states generally deal with one of two types of problems: (1) Arrangements whereby benefits accumulated under the laws of one state should not be lost when the employee moves to another state; and (2) arrangements whereby states agree that services performed in more than one state for a single employer may be covered by a single agency.

There are at the present time more than forty states which have entered into the interstate benefit payment plan proposed by the 

Interstate Conference of Unemployment Compensation Agencies.

This plan permits an unemployed individual to register at an employment office in a state in which he has not worked and claim benefits from another state in which he has accumulated potential benefit rights. The state in which he registers, called the "Agent 

State," does not pay him benefits from its own fund but merely serves as a forwarding agency, certifying to the state in which the individual has benefit rights that he has applied at an employment office and has registered as available for work. The eligibility of the individual for benefits and disqualifications, if any, are determined by the state in which the benefit rights have been accumulated. This system has, on the whole, proved practical and has served to pay benefits to individuals who are eligible even when they have left their home state to look for work elsewhere. 

All state laws, but the District of Columbia, authorize their agency to enter into arrangements with the federal government with regard to benefits payable under a federal unemployment compensation system. This will serve as a basis for arrangements between the states and the federal government providing for the equitable payment of benefits to individuals eligible under both a state law and the Railroad Unemployment Insurance Act, the only federal law now providing for unemployment compensation benefits. 

States which are geographically contiguous have found it advisable, in many cases, to enter into arrangements between themselves that services performed by a certain type of individual, or for a certain employer, shall be covered in one or the other state, where the facts are so complicated that it cannot be readily deter-

mined where the services are localized or where the base of operations exist. Arrangements of this type are becoming increasingly more popular.

The problem of states collecting contributions from employers who have had employment within the state but who have left is met by the provision authorizing the state agency to enter into agreements with other state agencies governing the collection and payment of contributions on such employment. At present there are only six states with an authorization of this sort: California, Indiana, Michigan, Oregon, Vermont, and Washington. It is likely that provisions of this sort will also become more popular as the states overcome the initial difficulties and settle down into day by day administration of their laws.

CHAPTER VI EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

Among the important provisions of the state unemployment compensation laws and the Federal Social Security Act are those excluding certain types of employment from the burden of taxes based on services rendered. Although the term "exclusion from payroll taxes" is used loosely to refer to any type of activity not covered by the acts, it should be strictly limited only to those services performed within the employment relationship which are not covered for some reason of policy. This chapter will treat only the exclusions definitely stated to be such in social security legislation. 

For most exclusions it is the services rendered that are exempt from the imposition of payroll taxes and not the individual who is personally performing such services, and the exclusion applies only for the period during which the individual is rendering services for an exempted employer. The only exclusion applying directly to the individual and not to the services is that in the old age benefit tax provision which excludes individuals over sixty-five years of age. 

The most common classes of excluded employment will be discussed in this chapter. 

Agricultural Labor. The two federal payroll taxes under the 

Social Security Act and every state unemployment compensation act, except that of the District of Columbia, exclude “agricultural labor" or "farm labor" from their coverage. This single exclusion accounts for a considerable percentage of those gainfully employed being removed from the operation of the unemployment benefit provisions of the states and from the old-age benefits of the federal government. It was considered expedient to exempt so large a class for administrative reasons, although questions of policy probably played a considerable role. It is a well known fact that most farmers do not keep adequate records, and to attempt to require it of them immediately would give rise to a multitude of enforcement problems; unemployment, furthermore, has never been so great with farm labor as it has been in urban centers. 

The exclusion of agricultural labor did not immediately lay at rest all questions concerning it. It was, and still is, necessary to determine the exact delimitations of the field-whether this occupation or that comes within the exclusion, and whether any admitted commercial pursuits should be considered within the exclu- 155

sion because of their close connection with agriculture. Although the definition is more precise here than in almost any other exclusion, that has only given opportunity for closer questions to arise. The difficulty of determining what is agricultural labor arises in two types of borderline cases-one, where the activity is closely related to agricultural activities but does not come within the specific terms of the rules or regulations defining the exclusion, and two, where the activities are connected with the preparation of agricultural products for the market.

The regulations to Titles VIII and IX of the Social Security 

Act (the taxing provisions) both define the exemption accorded to agricultural labor as including the following, among other things: "The term 'agricultural labor' includes all services performed—(a) By an employee, on a farm, in connection with the cultivation of the soil, the harvesting of crops, or the raising, feeding or management of livestock, bees, and poultry; or (b)

By an employee in connection with the processing of articles from materials which were produced on a farm; also the packing, packaging, transportation, or marketing of those materials or articles. 

Such services do not constitute 'agricultural labor,' unless they are performed by an employee of the owner or tenant of the farm on which the materials in their raw or natural state were produced, and unless such processing, packing, packaging, transportation, or marketing is carried on as an incident to ordinary farming operations as distinguished from manufacturing or commercial operations. As used herein the term 'farm' embraces the farm in the ordinarily accepted sense and includes stock, dairy, poultry, fruit and truck farms, plantations, ranches, ranges and orchards."
