The services contemplated in subdivision (a) above, include those which are performed on a farm by an employee of the tenant or the owner of such farm (whether or not he is in possession), directly in connection with the cultivation of the soil, raising and harvesting of crops, etc. These services are what are generally considered to be farm labor in the most common sense of that term. 

Certain services, although they are not performed directly in connection with the activities mentioned above are nevertheless performed as an incidental and necessary adjunct to such activities. 

They are necessary in the sense that they are essential for the proper carrying on of such activities and are incidental in the sense that both their duration and character are such as to constitute their performance a subordinate and minor part of such activities. Examples of such services are labor involved in hedging, ditching, and repairing of fences on a farm. 

Not all service performed on a farm by an employee of the owner or tenant thereof is "agricultural labor." Where the nature of the service is such that it might properly be said that the individual performing it is pursuing a special trade not closely con-

EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

157 nected with agriculture, such individual will not be excluded. Services performed by bookkeepers, stenographers, mechanics, or engineers are, therefore, not excluded when the individual is engaged exclusively in such work, even though it is done on a farm. 

The fact that an individual is engaged in handling farm products does not of itself make the services performed by him agricultural. Services are often performed by employees in connection with the processing, packing, or other handling of farm products, which are not a part of ordinary farm operations, but a part of manufacturing or commercial operations. Likewise, services are often performed in connection with the processing, packing, or other handling of farm products which do not constitute agricultural labor because they are rendered in the pursuit of a special trade or calling. 

The question whether the processing, packing, packaging, transportation, or marketing of farm products is carried on as an incident to ordinary farming operations as distinguished from manufacturing or commercial operations is necessarily dependent upon the facts in the particular case. The nature of the question does not permit of the adoption of a general rule which is controlling under all circumstances, but the following factors, while not all-inclusive, have been designated by the Bureau of Internal Revenue as those which will be taken into consideration as indicating that the services in question constitute agricultural labor: (1) The same employees who perform admittedly agricultural services also perform services in connection with the handling of the farm products. (2) The equipment used and the methods employed in handling the farm products are dissimilar to the equipment and methods used in like operations by persons admittedly engaged in commercial or manufacturing operations. (3) The employer handles products produced on his own farm exclusively. (4) The place where the handling is carried on is located on the farm. (5) The product handled is sold exclusively at wholesale. (6) It is the general custom and practice in the particular type of farming with respect to which the question arises to perform the handling operations in question. (7) The capital invested in the equipment used in handling the products does not constitute the greater part of the investment in the enterprise as a whole.

If an employee during certain periods renders services which are agricultural labor and at other times renders services in an employment subject to tax, the two types of services must be segregated, if possible, and the taxes paid on the remuneration for the amount of time spent in the covered employment. Where the serv-

ices cannot be so segregated, and the agricultural labor is only incidental to the covered employment, a tax must be paid on the entire remuneration earned. The contrary also applies, that is, where the covered employment is merely incidental to the agricultural services, and the two cannot be segregated, no tax need be paid at all.

One of the requirements of the regulations is that labor to be excluded as agricultural must be performed in connection with a farm. Although a farm is usually understood to be a plot of ground of considerable area, mainly devoted to the cultivation of the soil for the purpose of growing products useful to man, it is recognized both by the bureau and all state agencies that a farm may be used for other purposes. Thus, the word "farm" includes places where livestock, poultry, or bees are raised. Whether an establishment out in the open country which is devoted to other pursuits may be called a farm has been passed on in a great number of cases. Animal farms of one sort or another are in general not considered to be farms in the sense that the regulations speak thereof, and the raising of fur-bearing animals, horses for racing purposes, rabbits, and dogs, have been ruled to be nonagricultural pursuits. The caring for wild fur-bearing animals is not considered to be agricultural labor, but activities in connection with the raising of livestock, such as cattle, sheep, goats, mules, and hogs, would be. The exclusion of such labor would, however, be dependent on the nature of the services performed, that is, they would have to be directly connected with the feeding, herding, or otherwise ministering to the needs of such animals. An establishment engaged in the business of hatching chickens cannot be said to be engaged in agricultural activities when the hatchery is not located on the farm where the eggs are produced, but is engaged in receiving eggs from farmers, hatching the chickens, and selling the chickens to farmers and poultry raisers.

By far the most important determinations of what is a farm is made in the case of greenhouses and nurseries. The original ruling of the Bureau of Internal Revenue was that greenhouses and nurseries, which are, of course, primarily engaged in what are known as commercial operations as distinguished from purely agricultural operations, could not be considered as excluded. However, in later rulings the bureau recognized that horticulture is properly a branch of agriculture and that even though the greenhouse or nursery may be engaged in commercial pursuits, those services of the employees who were specifically engaged in planting, transplanting, and cultivation of the soil should be considered as agricultural labor if their services were performed on a farm. 

This raised the question of whether a greenhouse or nursery was located on a farm, and the bureau has rather consistently ruled that such establishments are located on farms. The bureau, however,

EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

159 requires that a showing be made as to certain elements which it feels will aid in that determination, such as: whether the establishment is within the limits of an incorporated municipality, and if so, how far it is from the central business district; the amount of acreage, showing the amount used for indoor cultivation in greenhouses or hothouses and the amount devoted to other uses. 

If the bureau determines that the greenhouse or nursery is not located on a farm, then the services of its employees are not excluded as agricultural labor, even though the nature of their services may be exactly similar to services of employees in greenhouses or nurseries which are located on a farm. The state agencies in many cases have refused to follow the ruling of the bureau in this regard, and preferred to maintain that services for a greenhouse or nursery are not agricultural labor. A Colorado Supreme Court decision has already decided that mushroom growing is not agricultural labor where the cultivation requires special conditions, such as caves and very dark places not duplicated in ordinary farm activities.

Problems have arisen as to whether certain activities come within the coverage of "cultivation of the soil and the raising and harvesting of crops." Even though the products grown are not directly useful to man, the services necessary to grow them may be agricultural labor. Thus, a company which is engaged in growing tobacco and grass primarily for seed may exclude those of its employees who are engaged in the cultivation of the soil and the growing and harvesting of the tobacco and grass. When the same employees are also engaged in the cleaning, packing, and shipping of the seed, that work also may be considered to be agricultural labor on the ground that it is incidental to their admittedly agricultural services. The cultivation of forest products may or may not be agricultural labor depending on whether the bureau or state agency considers such activity as directly relating to the cultivation of the soil. The bureau has ruled that services performed by employees on farms, of which their employer is owner or tenant, in connection with the planting, cultivation, or growing of tung trees and harvesting of tung nuts, constitutes agricultural labor, but that the production of maple sirup and maple sugar does not involve the cultivation of trees in the ordinarily accepted sense, and that the processing of maple sap into sugar is a manufacturing activity rather than an incident to ordinary farm operations. Such services, therefore, are not excluded. Services performed in the growing, harvesting, and processing of gum naval stores (turpentine and rosin) are not agricultural labor. Gum naval stores are produced principally in the state of Alabama, which has amended its act to provide that services performed in connection with such products are excluded. The Alabama exclusion does not, of course, affect the federal ruling, and employers of employees engaged in the

production of gum naval stores must report the full amount of their wages for purposes of the Title VIII and Title IX taxes. 

Services of an individual engaged in supervising the activities of employees who actually cultivate the soil are also deemed to be agricultural labor. However, services performed by an officer of a corporation operating a farm are not agricultural labor when such officer is exclusively engaged in the executive management of the corporation and does not devote himself to the direct supervision of services which are admittedly excluded. 

Certain operations which cannot be considered as agricultural in themselves are excluded because they are incidental to and directly connected with "agricultural labor." The bureau has ruled that pasteurizing, bottling, delivery, and sale of milk are not incidental to ordinary farming operations and cannot be excluded. 

However, preparing lettuce for packing; cleaning seeds; drying, fermenting, grading, or baling of tobacco; or preparing timber land for use as an orchard which involves clearing the timber, cutting, sawing, and transporting the logs to market, are all incidental to ordinary farming operations and are, therefore, excluded. 

The ruling of the bureau and of the state agencies as to whether packing, processing, or transportation of agricultural products should also be excluded as agricultural labor will depend on whether the services are performed by an employee of the owner or tenant of the farm on his own premises and whether the operations are commercial or incidental to ordinary farming activities. 

The processing of sugar cane, the milling of rice, and cotton ginning have been ruled to be commercial operations and, therefore, not excluded. If the services are performed for a producers' co-operative or association, it is uniformly held to be nonagricultural labor since the activities are not being performed on the premises of the owner or tenant of the farm on which the products in their raw state were produced. Thus, a co-operative of fruit growers which does grading, packing, and transporting for its members must include all its employees even though they are engaged in the performance of services which, if done by each member for himself, would be considered as "agricultural labor."

Domestic Services. The two federal payroll taxes and every state unemployment compensation law exclude from their coverage domestic service performed in a private home. Both elements must be present for the exclusion to operate-that is, the service must be of a sort usually regarded as involving work of a household nature, and the place in which such services are performed must be in a private home or residence.

The services of cooks, maids, butlers, valets, laundresses, furnace-men, and chauffeurs of automobiles for family use are the usual types of domestic service. A "handy-man" on an estate or

EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

161 for a residence will also be regarded as performing services of a domestic nature. However, when the services are not of a kind which are ordinarily and customarily performed as an integral part of household or home duties, they will not be considered as excluded. Such services as are performed by social secretaries, librarians, or trained nurses will not be excluded even though they are performed in the individual's place of residence. 

Even though the services are of a domestic nature they must still be performed in or about the private home of the employer. 

The term "private home" means the fixed abode of an individual or family, and the exclusion does not apply to services performed for hotels, rooming and boarding houses, or fraternities. An employee who performs both domestic services in a private home and nonexcluded services for the same employer will be considered to be in that category in which the substantial part of his services are performed if the two types are not segregable. Thus, a woman who is employed by a doctor, whose office is located in his private home, to clean up both his office and his home, will be considered to be excluded when her services are devoted mainly to cleaning up in the home. If the services are segregable, that is, the amount of time spent on the excluded and nonexcluded services can be accurately determined, the employee will be counted in determining the employer's liability, and his wages will be subject to taxes and contributions for that period of time in which he is engaged in performing nonexcluded services. For example, a girl who is hired to clean up in an employer's home in the morning and to wait on trade in his store in the afternoon will be counted as an employee and a tax must be paid on that amount of her remuneration which can be allocated to her services in the store.

Government Employment. Whether or not a state act contains a provision excluding employment for the federal government or its instrumentalities, such employment cannot be taxed under certain precedents of the Supreme Court. Congress, however, may grant permission to the states to tax any instrumentalities of the United States if they so desire, and the acts of California, Louisiana, Illinois, Massachusetts, and Rhode Island all contain a provision that instrumentalities of the federal government are to be subject as are all other employers in that state when Congress allows payments under a state unemployment compensation law by such instrumentalities.

The term "instrumentality of the United States" has been construed by the Bureau of Internal Revenue as meaning only such agencies as are made use of by the government as an instrument through which it directly exercises some governmental power, and as not comprehending private persons, who by contract with the government have undertaken to perform some services therefor.

Individuals who enter into contracts with the United States Post Office Department for the delivery of mail in isolated areas and who hire such persons as are needed to carry out the contract are neither employees of the United States nor of an instrumentality and, therefore, the individuals employed are not performing services in an excluded employment. Contractors who undertake

P.W.A. jobs on which they are obligated to hire a certain percentage from the relief rolls are not for that reason instrumentalities of the United States, even though their work may be for the government. Employees hired by a partnership engaged in court reporting in federal courts are not in excluded employment since their services are not performed for the United States but for a private firm which has a contract with the government. 

Referees in bankruptcy and all their employees are considered by the federal government to be in excluded employment since the referees are officers of a court and, therefore, part of an instrumentality of the government. This is true even though the remuneration of the referees and other employees is not derived from funds of the government but from assets of the insolvent estates whose affairs they are supervising. The trustee of an insolvent corporation who is operating the business under the general bankruptcy laws of the United States may be an officer of the court in the sense that his appointment is made through the court and his activities are supervised by it, but the services he performs do not constitute him an instrumentality of the United States, and he is subject to the payroll taxes imposed by the Social Security Act and to the contributions imposed by the state laws on the wages of the business' employees. A trust which has been organized to take over a part of the assets of an insolvent national bank for the benefit of the creditors of the bank is not an instrumentality of the United States even though the bank itself was. The trust was created by and acted for and on behalf of the creditors of the national bank and not on behalf of the bank. A federal statute, the Act of March 1, 1879, prohibits the assessment or collection of a tax by the federal government from any bank which has ceased to do business by reason of insolvency or bankruptcy where the assets thereof are insufficient to pay all depositors in full, but Section 818 of the 1938 Revenue Act provides that this shall not affect the payment of social security taxes. 

The Bureau of Internal Revenue has ruled that national banks are instrumentalities of the United States on the basis of many Supreme Court precedents. Most of the states have followed the bureau's ruling and excluded services performed for national banks from the coverage of their state unemployment compensation laws. 

A few states, however, cover national banks on the theory that they are not the type of instrumentality of the United States that is meant to be excluded since they are operated for private profit,

EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

163 and the aid they render the United States government is not of a governmental nature. It is quite probable that this controversy will be solved by Congress passing a law permitting the states to require contributions from all types of banks which are not wholly owned by the government and which have heretofore been considered as instrumentalities of the federal government. 

All member banks of the federal reserve system, whether they are national or state, are considered by the federal government to be instrumentalities, and the services performed for them to be exempted. It does not matter whether the services of any given individual who is employed by a bank deemed to be an instrumentality are actually of a nature designed to aid the government; it is sufficient that the services are being performed for an exempted institution. The service to be excluded, however, must be performed for the institution which is a federal instrumentality itself. Thus, a national bank owning a subsidiary trust company or safety deposit company can exclude only those individuals working for the bank, while those who work for the wholly owned subsidiary are to be counted as in employment because they are working for a different legal entity than the one to which the exemption applies. If the activities of the trust company were performed by a trust department of the bank which was not separately incorporated, these same activities would be considered as in excluded employment. Employees of a building which an exempt bank owns directly are not considered to be covered by the federal payroll taxes, but if the building is separately incorporated, even though all its stock is owned by the exempt bank, its employees must be considered as in employment. An organization of banks, all of whom may be exempted, to act as a clearing house association is not an instrumentality of the United States, and its employees are covered both by the state and federal taxes. Employees of a private trust are not excluded, even though an exempt bank is the trustee. 

State banks which are not members of the federal reserve system and which do not belong to any other system which would make them instrumentalities are not excluded from either the state or federal taxes. The only state bank which the Bureau of Internal Revenue has considered to be an instrumentality of a state, and which therefore the federal government cannot tax, is the Bank of North Dakota which, by the statutes of that state, is directly used to further the purposes of the state government. A few states have refused to follow the decision of the Bureau of Internal Revenue, which excludes state banks that are members of the federal reserve system for the reason that such banks are rarely used as fiscal agents of the government and are, therefore, not arms of the federal government, the taxation of which would in any manner impede the conduct of the business of the national government. Here also the conflict of opinion between the states and the federal gov-

ernment may be solved practically through permission given the states by Congress to require contributions for unemployment compensation from such banks.

Membership in the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation does not of itself make a bank an instrumentality of the United States. Such a bank would be excluded, of course, from the federal payroll taxes if it were a national bank or a member of the federal reserve system. However, organizations, such as building and loan associations, savings and loan associations, co-operative banks, homestead associations, insurance companies, and savings banks, chartered by the various states, which are members of the Federal Home Loan Bank System, are considered to be instrumentalities of the United States, and services performed for them are excluded from the definition of employment. But the fact that such organizations may be insured with the Federal Savings and Loan Insurance Corporation does not of itself confer on it the benefits of being an instrumentality of the United States. The Bureau of Internal Revenue has ruled that in view of the provisions of the act under which the following corporations were created, services performed by individuals in the employ of Federal Land Banks, National Farm Loan Associations, Joint Stock Land Banks, The Federal Farm Mortgage Corporation, Federal Intermediate Credit 

Banks and other like institutions, are services performed in the employ of an instrumentality of the United States. Wholly owned corporations of the United States, in addition to those mentioned above, such as Federal Home Loan Banks, the Home Owners Loan 

Corporation, and the Federal Savings and Loan Insurance Corpo ration are, of course, instrumentalities. The Social Security Board in its recommendations to Congress for amendment of the Social Security Act included the following paragraph in its report: "The 

Board recommends the inclusion of services performed in the employ of the United States or its instrumentalities. The Board anticipates no administrative difficulties in such inclusion. However, in extending Old Age Insurance to all employees of the federal government, it would be necessary to give consideration to the effect on other retirement systems for federal employees, with a view either to excluding employees already covered by these systems or to adapting these systems. 

In any event, the Board recommends an amendment to bring under coverage employees of instrumentalities of the United States, except those which either are wholly owned by the United States or are exempt from the taxes levied under the Social Security Act by virtue of some other act of Congress. The probable 'federal instrumentalities' which would thus be brought into Old Age Insurance are national banks and state banks which are members of the federal reserve system, and building and loan associations which are members of the Federal 

Home Loan Bank System."

EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

165 The expansion of the activities of state governments has accounted for a considerable growth in the number of commissions, boards, and other agencies of the states designed to take care of the new activities. Both the states and the federal government have been called upon to decide which of these many agencies are instrumentalities of the state government which cannot be taxed under the provision of the Social Security Act and the state acts. 

Liquor control commission, boards to supervise activities at race tracks, and motor vehicle agents who are designated by a commissioner of motor vehicles to issue registration certificates and license drivers, have all been considered to be instrumentalities of a state government. The states themselves have not always been as liberal as the federal government in excluding certain agencies of their own. Ohio, for example, excludes from its definition of employment only those services which are performed in governmental functions, while Wisconsin includes the state and its political subdivisions as employers. Wisconsin, however, does not require contributions from the state or its political subdivisions on all services performed for them but excludes employment in the following capacities for a governmental unit: as a public officer, employees on an annual salary basis, on work relief projects designated by the agency, and for certain other emergency or casual types of service.

The Bureau of Internal Revenue and most states do not attempt to make any distinction between governmental and proprietary functions of the states. Services performed even for a proprietary function of the state are considered to be performed for an instrumentality, and are thereby excluded from employment.

Many proprietary functions are exercised by such political subdivisions of the state as cities, counties, and irrigation, reclamation, and power districts. To determine whether services performed in such functions are excluded, it is necessary to examine the statute under which the duties of the institution seeking to be excluded are performed. If the statute in effect provides that the services are to be performed by and on behalf of the state or by and on behalf of one of its political subdivisions, it will be considered to be an instrumentality; but if the institution is privately maintained, it will not be excluded even though it is under state supervision and control.

As in the case of contracts entered into with the federal government, services performed for contractors with the states or their political subdivisions are not excluded from employment. Road building, construction of public buildings, and other similar types of activities, although they may be considered to further the work of the state, are not excluded as government employment when they are performed for private contractors.

Municipally owned utilities which are directly operated by the municipality are not employers within the meaning of the Social

Security Act nor of those state acts which make no distinction between governmental and proprietary functions. If, however, the utility is privately operated under a lease or agreement with the municipality or other political subdivision of the state owning it, services performed therefor will not be excluded as governmental employment. 

Maritime Employment. The federal unemployment tax and the laws of a great majority of the states specifically exclude services which are performed as an officer or member of the crew of a vessel on the navigable waters of the United States. The four states not specifically excluding such employment are Colorado, the District of Columbia, New York, and Wisconsin, but in these states also, maritime employment is not covered by the state agency because of the constitutional provision that the maritime and admiralty jurisdiction of the United States is exclusively in Congress. 

Congress cannot even delegate to the states any part of this jurisdiction as it may in the case of instrumentalities of the United States, or with respect to interstate commerce. It is likely, therefore, that since the states are powerless ever to deal with maritime employment, it will be necessary to set up a federal system of unemployment compensation just as was done for the railroads. The 

Old-Age Benefits provision of the Social Security Act can be amended to include therein maritime employment, and it is probable that this will be done instead of setting up a separate system of annuities as for the railroad industry (see Chapter II).

At present Title VIII of the Social Security Act, the Old-Age 

Benefits tax, excludes services performed as an officer or member of the crew of a vessel documented under the laws of the United States or of any foreign country. This differs from the wording of the exclusion in Title IX, which requires only that the service be performed by an officer or member of the crew of a vessel on the navigable waters of the United States. The reason for the difference in the wording is obscure, and as a practical matter it makes little or no difference in coverage. It has been held that the term "documented under the laws of the United States" means registered, enrolled, or licensed under the laws of the United States. Thus, vessels which are not so registered, etc., and which are on the navigable waters of the United States will be the only vessels excluded under the Federal Unemployment tax but not under the Old-Age Benefits tax. The number of these is probably very small.

A vessel as defined in Regulations 90 and 91 includes every description of water craft or other contrivance used as a means of transportation on water. It does not include any type of air craft. 

The term, therefore, applies to dredges, barges, pile-drivers, floating grain elevators, and show-boats. It is not necessary that the craft be self-propelling. For Title IX and for the state unemployment

EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

167 compensation laws, such vessels must be used on the navigable waters of the United States in order that services performed thereon are to be excluded. The term "navigable waters of the United 

States" has been construed to mean, through a long series of Supreme Court decisions, such waters as are navigable in fact and which by themselves or their connection with other waters form a continuous channel for commerce with foreign countries or among the states.

It is only the services of those individuals who are deemed to be officers or members of a crew which are excluded from the definition of employment under all the payroll taxes. This includes the master or officer in charge of the vessel, however designated, and every individual subject to his authority serving on board and contributing in any way to the operation and welfare of the vessel. 

The federal regulations state that "the exception extends, for example, to services rendered by the master, mates, pilots, pursers, surgeons, stewards, engineers, firemen, cooks, clerks, carpenters, dockmen, porters and chambermaids, and by seal hunters and fishermen on sealing and fishing vessels." It is necessary that these individuals perform some services to aid in the operation and welfare of the vessel. A carpenter who is employed to make repairs on the vessel while it is in navigation would be excluded, but carpenters who work from a floating pile-driver, barge, or dredge in connection with the construction of docks and piers on the waterfront would not be excluded. These individuals are not navigators or seamen, and perform their services on a vessel simply because it is more convenient for them to do their work thus. While the term "crew" is not limited to those persons on board who are physically engaged in causing the vessel to move through the water, it is ordinarily limited to those who contribute in some way to the operation of the vessel as a vessel, and not to those who use it only as a handy method of achieving their main purpose. A regular member of the crew, such as a purser, who must travel on land between ship voyages or who must attend certain hearings on shore, does not lose his exemption for that reason, since even his duties on shore contribute in some way to the welfare of his vessel. It still remains true, however, that employees of a company engaged in operating vessels are not excluded even though their services contribute to the welfare of the vessels, since they are not officers or members of a crew. 

Vendors of candy, soft drinks, and cigarettes on a ferry boat are considered to be members of a crew, and their services are excluded. If the vendor is not under the supervision of the master of the crew but works under the supervision and for the benefit and welfare of one who operates a concession to sell candy, cigarettes, etc., on board a vessel, his services are not excluded from the definition of employment.

The Bureau of Internal Revenue has ruled that fishing, and catching oysters, shrimps, and lobsters are excluded services when the individuals performing them are subject to the supervision of the master of the crew. But individuals, such as superintendents, clerks, machinists, and others who are employed in the operation of a cannery and in the canning of fish aboard a vessel, are not excluded even though they are subject to the authority of the master of the ship. These individuals are not required to sign the ship's articles, and it is clear that the services performed by them in canning fish do not contribute to the operation and welfare of the vessel, nor may such services be said to be incidental to regular duty on shipboard. Individuals engaged in catching oysters who begin their sorting and grading on board vessel and finish it on shore, where they help to pack the oysters, are excluded on the ground that the services performed on shore are incidental to their work on board the vessel. Georgia has ruled that although members of the crew who are occupied in commercial fishing are exempted, any other person on board a fishing vessel who is engaged in the business or trade of commercial fishing is an employee of the boat owner and must be counted in determining the number of workers under the 

Georgia Unemployment Compensation Act. New Jersey also has ruled that pound fishermen are not officers or members of a crew of a vessel which is employed in transporting them to and from the area of fishing operations. Connecticut excludes the services of employees of oyster growers which is performed as seamen on the company's boats, but considers them as in employment when they are engaged in other activities than that of navigation. 

Stevedores have been uniformly held not to be engaged in maritime employment even though a good part of their time is spent on board a vessel. They may be subject to the authority of the ship's master while on board, but since they are not required to sign the ship's articles and their services are not essential for the operation or welfare of the vessel while in navigation, they are considered to be in employment. The Supreme Court of the United 

States considers a stevedore's contract to be a maritime contract, and the argument has been raised that regardless of what the state unemployment compensation act may say, a state cannot make any provisions dealing with maritime contracts, which are exclusively within the admiralty and maritime jurisdiction of Congress. 

A New York appeals tribunal refused to consider this argument as valid because it considered unemployment compensation as granting rights unknown at common law and that, therefore, the granting of such new rights should not be considered as an encroachment upon the exclusive jurisdiction of the federal government. Not only stevedores but all workers, such as dry dock employees and those who work on pile-drivers or caissons, are affected by this argument, which would deprive them of rights to unemployment com-

EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

169 pensation on the ground that the state's action in attempting to cover them is unconstitutional. 

Services performed by individuals as watchmen on vessels permanently withdrawn from navigation are not excluded from the definition of employment, but if the vessel is only temporarily withdrawn, such services will be excluded. Ship keepers who live on board a vessel during the winter lay-up in northern ports, after the crew is disbanded, are employees of the ship owner, and their services are not excluded from employment. The distinction made between this and the previous example of a temporary lay-up is that in the latter case the crew remains constant while in the case of a winter lay-up the crew is dismissed and the ship keeper, although he may be a former member of the crew, is no longer one during the winter months. A watchman on board a vessel which is in port to exchange cargoes, who is employed by a contracting company, does not become a member of the crew when on board the vessel, and his services are, therefore, not excluded. 

Non-Profit Organizations. The term "non-profit organization" refers to organizations devoted to religious, charitable, scientific, literary, educational, or humane purposes, no part of whose profit from its operations inures to the benefit of any private shareholder or individual. Every state unemployment compensation act and the two taxing titles of the Social Security Act contain this exclusion in substantially the same form. New Hampshire adds fraternal organizations among those excluded; New Jersey adds hospitals, benevolent and philosophical organizations; while New 

York does not exclude non-profit organizations devoted exclusively to the prevention of cruelty to children and animals. Two conditions must be satisfied before an organization seeking exemption under these terms may consider services performed for it as not in employment. The status of the organization must be one whereby it is organized and operated exclusively for one of the purposes stated above, and secondly any profit accruing to it must be devoted to purposes other than the distribution to share-holders or any other individuals. The fact that an organization may be exempt from the payment of federal income taxes as a non-profit organization does not serve to exclude it from the unemployment compensation taxes unless its income tax exemption is based on that section in the income tax laws which is exactly similar to the exemption clause in social security legislation. Section 101 (6) of the Income Tax Act of 1936 and the analogous sections of prior acts contain the same provisions as the "non-profit" exemption of the state and federal laws dealing with payroll taxes. Institutions which have received exemption under Section 101(6) are entitled to a like exemption from the federal government, although of course the state agencies may still make their own independent determinations on

these matters. Certain states have refused to follow the federal rulings in this regard, and have considered institutions which have been exempted as "non-profit organizations" from the coverage of the Social Security Act, as employers subject to the payment of contributions for the wages paid their employees. A few states have held the Y.M.C.A. as not exempt, even though the Bureau of Internal Revenue has exempted it from the coverage of the federal Act.

These states, and a few others, seem to make a distinction between operations which are predominantly of a commercial nature and those which are predominantly charitable or religious and only incidentally commercial. They hold institutions and organizations which compete with businesses conducted for profit as not being exclusively engaged in charitable purposes. Although their reasoning may be faulty, inasmuch as the determining test should be the nature of the purposes on which the profits are spent instead of the methods used to secure those profits, it is most probable that the whole trend of social security legislation is away from the exclusion of this type of institution. The Social Security Board has recommended that individuals employed by "non-profit organizations" should be included in the scope of the Old Age Benefits provisions of the federal Social Security Act and the unemployment compensation system. Although the reason for the exemption of the income of these institutions from taxation still remains valid, such reason is not valid when used to argue that employees should be deprived of social security benefits simply because of the nature of the institution for whom they are working. 

Fraternal benefit societies and labor unions are not excluded under this definition unless the state act specifically exempts them. 

The ruling of the Bureau of Internal Revenue and of those state agencies which have considered the problem is that such societies are not exclusively organized for charitable or religious purposes but have an important element of catering to the entertainment and welfare of its members only. Under the rulings of the Bureau of Internal Revenue, however, slight incidental entertainment features will not deprive a non-profit organization of its exemption when its primary purpose is that of education. 

A charitable organization within the meaning of the exemption refers in the first instance to one which is devoted to the relief of the poor. It may, however, include organizations whose purposes are to give relief to sickness or to any of the other miseries to which humanity is subject. Hospitals, for example, may establish their exclusion only if they can show that they are engaged in charitable activities, such as furnishing free beds, clinics, etc. A hospital which is privately owned is not exempt even though as a matter of fact it shows no profit and offers extensive charity, since the fundamental test is not whether the institution actually makes

EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

171 1 any profit but whether any profit which might be made would inure to a private individual. Hospital Service plans have been exempt as charitable organizations in one state. The purpose of such plans is to provide a form of insurance whereby individuals pay a monthly sum in return for free medical care in any hospital co-operating with the plan. It is doubtful whether the Bureau of 

Internal Revenue would come to the same conclusion since the plan is more in the nature of a health insurance system than a charity in the legal sense of the term.

Religious organizations employing individuals are excluded from all payroll taxes. If the religious organization or group maintains commercial enterprises, all the profits of which are devoted to the stated purposes, it is the general concensus of opinions of the state agencies that such employment is also excluded. The problem has arisen quite frequently in the case of newspapers and other publications of religious groups, which are published as an adjunct to religious activities. Here, too, some states have ruled that the predominantly commercial nature of the publishing houses devoted to these publications, even though all its profits go to the religious group, deprives them of the exclusion. When the publishing house is separately incorporated or organized, there is more reason for stating that it is not organized exclusively for religious purposes and, therefore, for denying exclusion on that ground. 

There is at present no unanimity in the various opinions of the states and the federal government on whether a separate corporation devoted exclusively to publishing religious material for one sect or creed should be exempted, and its employees not covered by the various social security regulations.

The same problem has arisen in the case of cemeteries which are owned either directly or through complete stock ownership of a separately organized corporation. Many Catholic cemeteries are owned by the Bishop of the diocese as the representative of the Church, and employees of such cemeteries will not be covered on the ground that their employer is a non-profit organization devoted exclusively to religious purposes. If the cemetery is owned cooperatively by a number of different sects, at least one state has ruled that employment for the cemetery will not be exempted. 

Where the cemetery is not directly affiliated with any religious organizations, it cannot be exempted even though by its charter it is a non-profit organization. This is the ordinary accepted ruling of all state agencies, even when the general statutes on cemetery associations exempt them from all general taxes. This latter exemption is held to apply only to taxes raised for general revenue by the state, and does not apply to contributions which do not go into the state treasury but are raised for a specific purpose, such as unemployment compensation contributions are.

Other organizations with a semi-religious tinge, such as charities for the unemployed or organizations promoting self-help, are

usually considered as being organized and operated exclusively for religious or charitable purposes and, therefore, excluded. A corporation that is organized to improve the distribution of public and private charity is exempt even though it may not itself make any direct donations to individuals needing charity.

The regulations under the federal payroll taxes in dealing with exclusion for educational organizations state: "An educational organization within the meaning of the Act is one designed directly for the improvement or development of the capabilities of the individual but, under exceptional circumstances, may include an association whose sole purpose is the instruction of the public, or an association whose primary purpose is to give lectures on subjects useful to the individual and beneficial to the community, even though an association of either class has incidental amusement features. An organization formed, or availed of, to disseminate controversial or partisan propaganda or which by any substantial part of its activities attempts to influence legislation is not an educational organization within the meaning of section 811(b) (8) of the Act." Certain foundations which do not come within the expressed terms of the statement set out above have been excluded by various state agencies on the ground that the functions performed are of true educational value in spite of the fact that no formal instruction is given. The District of Columbia, for example, has excluded as a non-profit organization a foundation which was organized for promoting peace among nations by investigation and study, by assisting in developing international laws, by diffusion of information, and by promotion of agencies of peace. A wider definition of "education" was given by a federal court in the following language: "It includes not only the narrow conception of instruction, to which it was formerly limited, but embraces all forms of human experience, owing to the recognition of the fact that every stimulus with its corresponding reaction has a definite effect on character. It may be either mainly esthetic, ethical, intellectual, physical, or technical, but to be most satisfactory it must involve and develop all these sides of human capacity." 

Associations of professional people, such as lawyers, doctors, nurses, and accountants, are not exempt as non-profit organizations, even though one of their stated purposes for associating is to further their own education and that of the general public. They are not granted exclusion because it is felt that their primary purpose is limited to the betterment of their own group either economically or intellectually and that whatever general betterment is gained is incidental to the betterment of the association.

Orchestra associations have been excluded by many state agencies on the ground that their amusement features are incidental to their educational activities in furthering the musical appreciation of the community. This is a broader extension of the term

EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

173 "education" than appears to be tenable, but the inclusion was probably granted because of the general feeling that orchestras contribute something to their community which is valuable and that a reward should be given them through lowered taxation. Conservatories for the study of music are excluded on what is a more obvious ground, that of educating its students to become proficient musicians. An art gallery with which is maintained an art school was also held to be excluded on the ground that it is devoted exclusively to educational purposes. 

Schools which are not by their charters and by-laws organized on a non-profit basis will not be excluded even though their purpose is clearly educational. However, once their method of organization is changed, they may be excluded even though the former owner of the private school has become the present administrative head, drawing a salary for such services. This method has been used with success by trade schools, business colleges, and semiprofessional institutions of learning. The salaries of the administrative officials of such non-profit organizations must, however, be a fair remuneration for services and not merely a device by which an excess of income over expense is drawn off. Once the institution has been organized on a non-profit basis, such excess of income over expense must be devoted exclusively to the betterment of the institution, and an effort to dispose of the profits in the form of interest on loans or salary will serve to deprive the institution of its exempt status.

Family Employment. Title IX of the Social Security Act, and forty-eight of the fifty-one state unemployment compensation acts, exempt from their definition of employment services which are performed for a child or spouse, and by a child under the age of twentyone for a parent. The exemption is not found in the old age benefit provisions. The Social Security Board has recommended that a like exemption be put in the Old Age Benefit provisions so as to prevent collusion between members of families whereby rights to benefits might accrue through a fictitious employment relationship.

For the exemption to apply under Title IX and under the state acts, the services in question must be performed for an individual who comes within one of the stated classes. Services performed by a child for a partnership, one of the members of which is his parent, is not exempt since it is considered that the child is working for the partnership, an entity composed of all the partners. It is probable, however, that a child working for a partnership composed of his father and mother would not be considered as in employment. 

If the service sought to be excluded is performed for a corporation, the exemption does not apply even though a family relationship exists between the individual performing the service and one or all of the officers or stockholders of the corporation. Such family mem-

bers must be counted as in employment by the partnership or organization even though they are working without pay. This is important, since the inclusion of a family member, even though he is working without pay, may subject the employing unit to liability for taxes and contributions on the ground that sufficient individuals are in employment.

The Bureau of Internal Revenue has ruled that services of foster or stepchildren under twenty-one who are working for their parents are exempt, and that foster or step-parents working for their children also come within the exclusion. A foster parent is one who has not formally adopted a child as his own, but the argument of the bureau was that the exclusion is based on employment on a family basis rather than on a business or commercial basis, and that the same reason for exclusion is found in the case of foster parents as in that of natural parents.

California has ruled that the services of fathers-in-law and mothers-in-law do not come within the exemption. Although there is no ruling on grandparents, it is probable that their services would be exempted.

Casual Labor. Title VIII of the Social Security Act, dealing with the old age benefits provisions, excludes casual labor not in the usual course of the employer's business. Eleven states also exclude casual labor under one definition or another, even though the exemption is not found in Title IX. Therefore, the casual labor which is exempted by the state will be covered by the federal government and the full three per cent tax will be paid thereon. Connecticut, the District of Columbia, Indiana, and Oregon exclude casual labor which is not in the course of the employer's business. 

Michigan does not cover services in professional capacities which are not in the nature of permanent employment. Massachusetts and Ohio permit the exemption when the services do not last for more than four weeks, but only under rules to be established by the agency. Nebraska and Illinois exclude certain types of casual labor performed in emergencies if such services do not last more than ten days a month in the former state, and seven days a month in the latter. Ohio further excludes extra workers who are not hired for more than one day a week for the purpose of determining the coverage of the employment, but such individuals' wages are included for contribution purposes if the employer is otherwise liable. These extra workers, however, are not entitled to benefits based on the contributions paid in by their employer. 

Under Title VIII the services will be exempt as casual labor only if its performance is occasional, incidental, or irregular, and does not promote or advance the employer's trade or business. 

The most common examples of cases in which services may be exempt as casual labor are found in those instances in which an

EXCLUDED EMPLOYMENTS 

175 individual employer hires workers to build, remodel, or repair his home, summer cottage, garage, or other similar structures for his own occupancy and use, and not to promote or advance his trade or business. The feeling of the bureau is that a corporation cannot expend money for purposes which do not indirectly further its trade or business and, therefore, no matter how occasional, incidental, or irregular the services may be which are performed for a corporation, they will not be excluded as casual labor. The Bureau of Internal Revenue has promulgated two rules as to presumptions raised by the performance of services which may be considered to be casual. If the presumption raised by either of the rules is that the service to which it applies is considered "employment," then, in accordance with the presumption, the services are not excepted as casual labor unless and until the employer is advised in writing by a federal taxing agency that on the basis of evidence submitted it has been determined that the presumption has been rebutted. The presumption caused by either of the rules may be rebutted by any employer or employee affected by the rule or by the Bureau of Internal Revenue. If the performance of the services falls within the presumption, no evidence will be required except in special cases, since the bureau feels it administratively desirable that the question be set at rest in that limited field in which the rules are intended to apply. The rules follow: "Rule A. The term 'casual labor means all service performed by an employee for his employer during a period of 1 calendar month or any 2 consecutive calendar months if such service is performed on not more than 10 calendar days, whether or not such days are consecutive. Such service does not constitute 'employment,' and is not taxable under Title VIII, provided it does not promote or advance the employer's trade or business. If the employee performs service for the employer on 11 or more calendar days during such period (irrespective of the duration of performance on any such day, and whether or not such days are consecutive), the term 'casual labor' does not include any of such service; and, whether or not it promotes or advances the employer's trade or business, all of such service of the employee, including that performed on the first 10 days, constitutes 'employment,' and is taxable under Title VIII. "Rule B. If the total number of hours of services performed by all employees of the employer on the particular job or project is more than 200, the term 'casual labor' does not include the service (irrespective of its duration) of any such employee. Whether or not it promotes or advances the employer's trade or business, all of such service, including that performed during the first 200 hours, constitutes 'employment,' and is taxable under Title VIII."

The temporary services of a nurse employed by a patient in his private home have been held to be casual labor. If the nurse is employed by a sanitarium, hospital, or doctor, her services are not considered to be excluded. Chauffeurs, pallbearers, and singers, who perform service of a temporary character for funeral directors are not excluded as casual labor, since the performance of such services directly furthers the trade or business of the employer. 

Individuals who distribute handbills, make speeches, or otherwise aid a candidate for election to public office are not considered as in employment, even when the candidate is seeking re-election and has no other trade or business. If, however, the candidate forms a corporation which takes over all activities connected with his campaign, any services performed for such corporation will be considered to be in covered employment. 

Minor students who are part-time workers are excluded for services performed while they are in regular attendance at a public or private school by the acts of Illinois, Michigan, New Jersey, and Ohio. Rhode Island excludes minor students who are either regularly attending school or are in their customary vacation period, for benefit purposes, but includes them for contribution purposes. 

The employer must count them in determining whether he is liable under the state act, and must pay contributions on their services, but the wages earned by them may not be used for the establishment of potential benefit rights.

CHAPTER VII  

The definitions of employment and employees to be found in the Social Security Act and the state unemployment compensation laws are of so indefinite a nature that they will be of little use in determining in a given case whether a relationship exists between two parties which will be covered by those respective acts. Such a decision, however, is probably the most important one that has to be made in determining the coverage under the Social Security laws. The exclusions from the definition of employment which were treated in the previous chapter are at least specific, even though their application may not be clear in any given case. 

But given a definition of employment such as that in Title IX of the Social Security Act, it takes more than merely a knowledge of what is contained in the four corners of the Act to determine whether the performance of certain services will be subject to the Act. 29 

Section 907 (c) of the Social Security Act defines employment as follows: "The term 'employment' means any service, of whatever nature, performed within the United States by an employee for his employer, .

From this definition it can be seen that the Social Security Act imposes its taxes only on the employment relationship as that is known at common law. Article 205 of Regulations 91 makes this even clearer by the use of the following language: "An individual is in the employ of another within the meaning of the Act if he performs services in an employment as defined in Section 907 (c). However, the relationship between the individual who performs such services and the person for whom such services are rendered must, as to those services, be the legal relationship of employer and employee. The Act makes no distinction between classes or grades of employees. Thus, superintendents, managers and other superior employees are employees within the meaning of the Act. "Generally the relationship (that of employer and employee) exists when the person for whom services are performed has the right to control and direct the individual who performs the services, not only as to the result to be 177

66 accomplished by the work but also as to details and means by which that result is accomplished. That is, an employee is subject to the will and control of the employer not only as to what shall be done but how it shall be done." State unemployment compensation laws usually define services in a more limited manner than does the Federal Social Security Act. A typical definition is that of Illinois, which is as follows: 'employment' means services, including services in interstate commerce, performed for wages or under any contract of hire, written or oral, expressed or implied. ." The state definition, then, requires a contract of hire or the performance of services for wages before the relationship can be construed to be one which will be covered by the unemployment compensation laws. The problem of what is a contract for hire frequently arises in instances where services are being performed without remuneration. This may happen quite frequently in cases of small corporations when some of the corporation officers may either be totally inactive, or if they do perform services, may do so without any payment in salary. The question is then raised whether such corporation officers should be included for the purpose of determining whether there are sufficient individuals in employment to subject the corporation either to the state unemployment compensation law or to Title IX of the Social Security Act. 

Before taking up this problem we must first determine what a corporation is. Under the state unemployment compensation laws, a corporation is not further defined but is evidently intended to mean those fictional legal entities which exist by virtue of a charter issued by the state, enjoying limited liability, and with an existence separate and apart from that of its officers, incorporators, or stockholders. The Social Security Act, however, defines a corporation as including associations and joint stock companies. The term "association" is not further defined in either the Social Security 

Act or in Regulations 90 and 91. Certain rulings of the Bureau of

Internal Revenue, however, help to define an "association." In one ruling, the question was whether nine members of a partnership association, formed under a Pennsylvania statute, were employers or employees of the association. If they were partners in a bona fide partnership, they would be considered as employers under the federal regulations, which are the same in this particular as those of the state unemployment compensation laws. The bureau ruled in this case, however, that it was not a bona fide partnership, even though it was recognized as such by the Pennsylvania law, but that it was an association and, therefore, taxable as a corporation. Its nine members were thus considered as employees, since they performed services for the association, an entity distinct from its component members. Aid is given in helping to determine what is an

"association" for purposes of the federal Act by consideration of the regulations to the Internal Revenue Act of 1936. Article 1001-2 

of Regulations 94, issued under this Act, says: "The term 'association' is not used in the Act in any narrow or technical sense. 

It includes any organization created for the transaction of designated affairs, or the attainment of some object, which, like a corporation, continues notwithstanding that its members or participants change, and the affairs of which, like corporate affairs, are conducted by a single individual, a committee, a board, or some other group representing in a representative capacity it in- cludes a voluntary association, a joint stock association or company, a 'business,' trust, a Massachusetts trust, a common law trust, an investment trust (whether of the fixed or management type) a partnership association, and any other type of organization (by whatever name known) which is not, within the meaning of the Act, a trust or an estate or a partnership.' 19 If the rulings of the Bureau of Internal Revenue under the Social Security Act follow the lead of these regulations, it will mean that officers or managers of the types of organizations listed above will be treated as officers of an "association," who will, therefore, be taxable as employees of a "corporation." It is difficult to make any definite statement as to the attitude of the state agencies on whether officers or members of associations which are treated as corporations under the federal Act will be considered as employees of a separate legal entity or as the employers on whose remuneration no contributions will be due.

The circumstances under which officers of a bona fide corporation should be counted as in employment for purpose of the payroll taxes have led to a great deal of controversy between the state and federal agencies, and even to frequent changes of opinion. 

Up until the early part of 1939 it was the opinion of the Bureau of Internal Revenue that the definition of an employee in the Social Security Act, as including officers of a corporation, meant that all the officers must be counted as employees irrespective of the fact that they performed no services, received no pay, and were in fact purely honorary. Since corporations include associations under the federal definitions, this interpretation of the Bureau of Internal Revenue meant that many fraternal organizations, such as the Masons, Elks, Moose, etc., and many non-profit organizations, such as trade associations and chambers of commerce, were considered as employers for purposes of Title IX even though they may not have had more than one or two individuals in their employ who performed services in what is usually considered to be an employment relationship. Many rulings were issued on this point, all of them consistent in that they considered an individual to be in employment if he occupied an office provided for in the charter or by-laws of his organization, if the office could not be considered to

be analogous with the position of director on a board of directors in a business organization (directors are not considered to be in employment). The bureau, probably for administrative reasons, came to the realization that a change in interpretation was necessary. It was effected by the following language: "It has been concluded after further consideration of the matter that an honorary officer of a corporation may not, in any real sense, be regarded as an officer within the contemplation of Section 1101 (a) (6) of the Act. In determining whether this conclusion applies in any particular instance, however, it is necessary first to decide whether the individual involved is in fact an 'honorary' officer. While this decision must in turn rest upon a consideration of the facts in each specific case with respect to which the question arises, in general it will be considered that an officer of a corporation is an 'honorary' officer within the meaning of the foregoing rule if (1) he is specifically designated an honorary officer; (2) such designation is solely to do him honor or to have him included in the organization because of his name, prominence, or standing in the community; (3) it would not ordinarily be necessary to fill his office should he die or resign; (4) as such officer, he does not actually perform any service and is not required or expected to perform any; and (5) as such officer, he does not receive, and is not entitled to receive, remuneration. If it is determined, upon a consideration of these factors, that an individual's relationship to a corporation is in fact solely that of an honorary officer, he is not an employee of the corporation for purposes of Title IX of the Social Security Act and should not be included in determining whether the corporation is an employer of eight or more so as to be subject to the tax imposed under that title." It is impossible to say at present whether the new interpretation will be held to apply to corporations as they are commonly known. The five factors which are listed as aids in determining whether an officer is "honorary" or not seem to be much more applicable to associations or non-profit corporations than they are to the usual small corporation which may have one or more inactive and unpaid officers.

At the same time the above ruling was made, the Bureau of Internal Revenue also changed its opinion concerning officers of fraternal organizations whose duties are exclusively ritualistic. The bureau decided that officers whose duties and activities are exclusively ritualistic are not, in the performance of such duties and activities, to be considered as in employment for the purposes of

the taxing provisions of the Social Security Act. In determining whether or not services are exclusively ritualistic within the meaning of this rule, incidental non-compensated administrative services may be ignored. This means, in effect, that the taxes imposed under Titles VIII and IX attach only with respect to compensation of those officers whose services are not exclusively ritualistic, and only such officers are to be counted for the purpose of determining whether the organization has eight or more individuals in employment so as to subject it to the tax imposed under Title IX. 

In general, the state unemployment compensation interpretations adopted one of two alternatives: Either they followed the former ruling of the Bureau of Internal Revenue and held all officers of corporations as in employment regardless of the fact that they neither performed services nor received remuneration, or else they attempted to conform strictly to the usual state law definition of employment, given above, and required either the performance of services for wages or under a contract of hire. This latter approach meant, for most state agencies, a requirement that an officer of a corporation would not be counted unless he performed services, whether or not such services were remunerated, or else that he received wages, whether or not such wages were in payment for the actual performance of services. In other words, the state agencies that followed the latter alternative inferred the existence of a contract of hire when money was paid to an officer or when he performed services even without pay. The states that held to the first opinion given above, that an individual was in employment by a corporation if he was a duly elected officer even though he might perform no services and receive no remuneration, usually justified their position on the ground that a contract of hire existed between the officer and the corporation in the sense that the latter could require the former to perform certain charter or statutory duties, and that the right to demand the performance of such services was sufficient to place the officer in an employment relationship with the corporation. Many states which first adopted this attitude later changed their rulings so that they included as being in employment only those officers of a corporation with whom a valid contract of hire, as that would be interpreted at common law, existed. 

Some states refused to consider officers of a corporation as in employment unless they received remuneration for the performance of their services, on the ground that a valid contract of hire required a consideration, in the form of money payments, for the performance of services for the corporation. 

Whether an officer of a domestic corporation, who is not a resident of the state in which his corporation is an employing unit, should be considered as an employee has been considered by a number of agencies. The opinion of most of them has been that if the officer is not performing services within the state he

should not be counted as in employment within that state. There is, however, a possibility that a state which considers all officers as being in employment irrespective of whether they perform services or receive remuneration will count a nonresident officer as being in the same position as one who is a resident, in so far as the former may be under a duty to perform certain tasks imposed on his office by the corporate charter or the corporation laws of the state. If, however, the corporation was incorporated in one state and is licensed to do business in another, a nonresident officer of the latter state will not be considered to be in employment in that state if he performs no services there. 

A state agency will follow its usual rule as to what officers of a corporation are in employment, even though the officers may be minors or aliens.

The status of officers of a former corporation whose charter has expired or which has been formally dissolved by the state was considered in Chapter III. If the agency follows the federal rul ings that an association should be treated for payroll tax purposes as a corporation, it will mean that the officers of the former corporation will still be considered as employees. If, however, the agency feels that a corporation no longer exists and that the legal entity surviving is in the nature of a partnership, it will treat the former owners of the corporation's stock (who may or may not be the same as the officers) as being members of a partnership or a joint stock association.

In determining which individuals performing services for trusts are to be considered as in employment, the Bureau of Internal Revenue makes a distinction between strict trusts and business trusts. The trust or estate is in any event considered to be the employer, but whether the trustee or other fiduciary engaged in administering the trust or the estate is to be counted as the representative of the employer or as an employee will depend upon whether he performs any services outside of his official administrative duties. Thus, where trustees and members of the advisory board of a Massachusetts trust perform services other than attendance at board meetings, they are considered to be employees of the trust itself and their remuneration is subject to the payroll taxes. A trustee who hires another individual to perform certain of his official duties in the administration of the trust may himself be an employer of such individual, and may be required to pay the necessary taxes on his remuneration. The state agencies, as a general rule, follow this reasoning of the bureau, and hold the trustee as an employer only when he performs no duties except those required from him in his official capacity. The performance of any services that he may do which could be done by individuals not serving in the same official capacity are performed as an employee.

For the purposes of the great majority of state unemployment compensation laws, there is a presumption that all services performed for remuneration or under a contract of hire are performed in the employment relationship. As a practical matter, the state agencies do not examine too closely into what constitutes a contract of hire. They are satisfied to presume the existence of an employment relationship from the existence of a status between the parties which is usually evidenced by the performance of personal services or the receipt of remuneration. The status, however, may exist even in the absence of these two indicia, as was pointed out above in the case of officers of corporations, who may be considered as in employment even in the absence of these two conditions. The status may also exist even in the absence of remuneration or services where the intent of the parties, as evidenced by their conduct, is that a former relationship, admittedly one of employment, should continue. Thus, an individual who has taken a leave of absence or who is on vacation may be considered to be in employment, even though he receives no remuneration and performs no services during the time of his absence. 

Since unemployment compensation contributions are in the nature of an excise tax on the status of employment, the performance of any services raises the initial presumption that they will be subject to the unemployment compensation law of the state in which they are performed, subject always to the fulfillment of these conditions that the employer has the minimum number of employees for the required length of time set out in the act; that the services are such as are not specifically excluded from the operation of the act; and that the services conform to the definition of the employment relationship in the state law. The fact that the coverage of the Social Security Act's taxing provisions is based on an employment relationship known at common law as master and servant was mentioned previously. Under this definition independent contractors are not considered to be in employment, and the services they perform are excluded both for determining whether the employer has sufficient individuals in employment to be subject, and for the computation of the tax. 

An opinion still commonly held by many business men and their advisers is that all individuals who were formerly known as independent contractors are exempt from the operation of the state unemployment compensation laws. That opinion, however, is not correct unless certain other important qualifications are met. 

An independent contractor's most important characteristic is that he is free to determine for himself the methods he will use in attaining the objectives for which he has contracted. As usually stated by courts and legal text books, the employer has the right to determine what an employee is to do and how he shall do it, while an independent contractor, although he is told what has to

be done, carries out that work without the necessity of following any further orders from the party with whom he has contracted. 

Most of the state unemployment compensation laws, however, do not exclude from the definition of employment all services performed by one who was an independent contractor at common law or under the federal rulings. These state laws supply their own definitions as to what services shall be exempted, and in most cases these definitions do not coincide with the one given above. 

Instead of satisfying the control phase alone, the services performed must now come up to other standards before they may be excluded from the coverage of the state acts. These standards have been expressed in identical language in most of the state laws. 

It should be noted that the three provisions must be in conjunction and not in the alternative. All three, therefore, must be present before the state agency can rule that the services are not to be covered. The Illinois section on this point is typical of all the other state laws and may well be quoted in its entirety: 

"Section 2(f).
