"(5) Services performed by an individual for wages shall be deemed to be employment subject to this Act unless and until it is shown to the satisfaction of the Director that- "(A) Such individual has been and will continue to be free from control or direction over the performance of such services, both under his contract of service and in fact; and "(B) Such service is either outside the usual course of the business for which such service is performed or that such service is performed outside of all the places of business of the enterprise for which such service is performed; and "(C) Such individual is engaged in an independently established trade, occupation, profession or business." 

That part of this chapter dealing with the employment relationship will be devoted to an application of the principles stated above and of the principles used by the Bureau of Internal Revenue in determining the existence of an employment relationship in certain well defined groups, trades and special callings. 

Professional Employment. The performance of services by individuals who follow what is usually known as a learned profession are the usual examples of services performed outside of the employment relationship. Lawyers, physicians, dentists, accountants and auditors, veterinarians, auctioneers, public stenographers, and members of other professions who offer their services to any member of the public desiring them, are the most common examples of services which satisfy not only the tests of the old master-servant relationship, but also the three statutory tests set out

by the state unemployment compensation laws. It must be recognized, however, that the exclusion pertains to the nature of the services and not to the individual performing them. The fact that an individual may be a lawyer or a doctor does not mean that the services he performs are necessarily performed outside of the employment relationship. An attorney who is engaged by a business establishment to perform certain of their legal duties for a fixed monthly salary, and who is required to work certain hours of each day, is an employee of the establishment even though as a matter of fact no control is exercised over his activities. It is presumed that the right to control his activities is present because of the fact that he is furnished a place to work, whatever materials he may need, and from the fact that there exists a right to supervise the manner of his performance. However, an attorney who maintains his own offices and who is engaged by a business establishment to defend it in any suit that may be brought against it during the year, for which he is paid a fixed retainer fee, is not an employee since his contract does not require the actual daily performance of services or that he give the firm the right to his exclusive services. An attorney engaged by a law firm to perform whatever services may be assigned to him is considered to be in employment even though a large degree of freedom is allowed him. 

He may handle certain cases assigned to him by the law firm from which he receives the full fees in addition to his regular salary, but even as to the conduct of those cases he will be considered as an employee, and the full amount of his remuneration from all sources will be considered as taxable wages. 

A common practice of many law firms is to designate certain individuals as "associates" or "special" partners, thereby designating them as being in a different category than those attorneys who are admitted to be employees. Such junior partners may have the authority to bind the firm, may conduct affairs of the firm on their own initiative without any more than general supervision, and their remuneration may be determined in part by the net profits of the firm. Although there are not many opinions of state agencies on this point, it may be doubted whether these junior partners are in reality partners in the same sense as the original members of the firm, so that they may be considered as employers rather than employees. The contract between the firm and the junior partners usually provides that the assets of the firm are to remain the property of the original members of the firm and that the share of the profits to be paid to the junior members may be changed at any time by the senior partners. It is doubtful whether an arrangement of this sort will create a partnership arrangement that will serve to exclude the junior partners as employees, in view of the fact that the Bureau of Internal Revenue regards any partnership of more than four or five members with suspicion.

Such partnerships do not usually conform to the general statutes on the formation of partnerships, since the junior members do not enter into any relationship with each other but only enter into a relationship as individuals with the senior members as an entity. 

Doctors or physicians who have their own practices, maintain offices, and are free to use their time in whatever manner they prefer, are not considered to be in employment under any definition. Certain problems have arisen, however, in the case of physicians who enter into arrangements with industrial plants whereby they agree to render medical services to all the employees of the plant whenever called upon to do so. In one ruling of the Bureau of Internal Revenue, the plant furnished all medicine and dressings and paid the necessary expense of hospitalization for its employees who were injured in the course of their employment. The physician employed two assistants who could be sent out to render medical aid if the contracting physician was not available. The physician conducted his own private practice with which the company had nothing whatever to do. The company paid him a regular monthly salary for whatever services he might perform. The bureau ruled that he was not an employee of the company, basing their opinion on the grounds that the company did not have the right to exercise sufficient control over the manner and means in which the physician performed his duties, while the regular monthly salary, which would usually be considered as sufficient indication of an employment relationship, was in this case regarded as a fee by which the company retained first call on his services, much as a retainer fee to an attorney. 

In another case the bureau ruled that the physician was an employee of the company where he was required to report each morning at a fixed hour, to remain on duty as long as his services were needed, and where, furthermore, he was required to examine all individuals applying for work. He was furnished with office space and facilities, and the combination of all these factors led the bureau to the conclusion that the company had the right to exercise control over the method of performance of the physician's services.

Lodges and fraternal organizations frequently arrange for a physician to examine their members applying for insurance and to call on members who require medical aid. The California agency ruled that a physician performing services for a fraternal organization was in employment where he received a fixed quarterly compensation for his services and where it was required that the physician personally had to attend all members who were on the sick list within twelve hours after notice and to file immediately a written report showing the result of all medical services rendered. The fact that the physician may have had a private practice in addi-

tion, or that in fact the services performed for the fraternal order were only a small part of his total activities, was not considered as affecting the conclusion.

The element of how much time was devoted to work for the supposed employer was gone into in a situation where the physician was on a retainer basis for a medical insurance association. 

The California agency held that the fact that a substantial part of the physician's time and his office facilities were given to his work for the medical insurance association went to prove that the association must, as a matter of fact, have the right to exercise control. It should be noted that the inference of the right to exercise control from the fact that an individual devotes most of his working time to performing services for one employer is frequently relied upon by all state agencies. Their feeling seems to be that an individual cannot be said to be independently established when his activities are almost exclusively devoted to one or a very few employers, and the fact that he must rely for his livelihood on the good will of a single employer establishes rather conclusively that the employer has the power or right to require that his services be performed in a certain fashion. 

As a general rule, auctioneers, because of their professional character, are classed as independent contractors. That rule applies where the individual occasionally offers his services to the general public, and is engaged by various persons with respect to specific transactions in which such persons do not control or direct the performance of his service. That rule does not apply, however, where the individual enters into employment, even though temporary, in which his employer controls and directs his professional services. Thus, where an auctioneer is engaged temporarily by a company in the auctioneering business, an employment relationship will be held to exist.

The amusement industry is one that has raised a good deal of question concerning the status of individuals performing services in it. Such individuals are likely to be well trained and to have unique talents which do not readily allow their actions to be controlled by the purchaser of their services. Furthermore, difficulty arises under state unemployment compensation laws because of the intermittent nature of their employment and the number of states in which their services may be performed. The Bureau of 

Internal Revenue has summarized the common types of amusements, and has indicated generally whether the individual performing the services will be considered to be in employment and if so, who will be deemed to be subject to the payroll taxes for his wages. As yet, definite information cannot be given as to whether the states will follow the federal rulings in all these matters, but it can be assumed that they will, out of a desire to conform to the same taxing base as the federal government.

In determining the status of radio entertainers, the bureau has taken into consideration the contention that such individuals are performing their services as independent contractors. It has refused to treat them as such even though they are selected for their known ability to perform in a particular fashion, and no attempt may be made to change any such characteristic. The bureau based its conclusion that such artists were in employment on the grounds that the right to control necessarily existed since the artists are engaged to apply their training and personal accomplishments in the interests of the purchaser. Furthermore, individuals appearing on radio programs are not free to designate the hours of broadcasts or rehearsals and are not free to control the time when their respective parts will be rendered during the actual broadcast, inasmuch as the services of each individual must be coordinated into a complete and smoothly timed program. 

The problem of who is the employer of such radio artists still remains to be settled. All radio programs may be divided into two general classes: sustaining programs and sponsored programs.

The former are those containing no advertising matter, and, hence, from which the broadcaster derives no revenue, and for which he generally pays all talent costs. A sponsored program may be one that is arranged and produced by an advertising agency for a sponsor or those created by a broadcasting company or by an independent producer and delivered to the sponsor as a complete unit, or what is commonly known as a "package" program. The radio performer may have contracted with a manager or booking agency to perform his services exclusively under their direction. 

In return for attaining publicity and employment for the artists, the manager or booking agency receives a designated percentage of the artist's earnings. Because the manager's interest usually extends only to securing employment for the artists in return for such percentage of his earnings, and as a matter of fact he does not exercise control or have the right to exercise control over the manner of the artist's performance, it is the usual conclusion that such manager or booking agency is not the employer. The broadcasting company or network is considered to be employer of the artists, musicians, and other individuals who render services on sustaining programs.

A broadcasting company may agree with a sponsor to furnish a program of a particular type, to occupy the time which the sponsor has purchased in order to advertise his products or services. Under this arrangement the broadcasting company agrees to deliver to the sponsor a "complete package" for a predetermined price. The sponsor has nothing to do with the selection of the artists, the presentation of the program, or the direction or control exercised over the talent appearing therein, although it may pass on the script as it is prepared. The same sort of program may be

planned and produced by an independent producer and may be sold as a unit for a specified sum to a person desiring to utilize radio as an advertising medium. Here the radio talent will be considered to be in the employment of the independent producer rather than of the sponsor who eventually buys the program as a unit. 

By far the greater number of sponsored radio programs are arranged for the sponsor by an advertising agency. The agreement between the agency and the sponsors generally contemplates that the agency shall make all contracts in the name of the sponsor, shall give all orders, and generally do everything necessary to be done to best promote the sponsor's interest in the use of radio as an advertising medium. The contracts for the purchase of time from the broadcaster and the contracts for talent are made between the agency as agent for the sponsor and the broadcasting company or managers of the radio talent. The agency supervises the production of the actual performance, and the sponsor has the right, whether or not such right is exercised, to be present at rehearsals and to make suggestions as to changes in script and the general make-up of the program. On the basis of these facts, the bureau consistently holds that the sponsor of a commercial program is the employer of the individuals rendering services on such program. Although the functions of the advertising agency include the engaging of the entertainers and the controlling and directing of their activities, the advertising agency cannot be considered to be the employer since the performance of its functions are for and on behalf of the sponsor.

Because of the fact that musicians always operate in groups under the guidance of a leader, the question has been presented as to whether musicians are in employment and, if they are, as to who is the employer. There has been no serious argument presented that musicians are not in the employ of someone, and both the bureau and the state agencies hold consistently that musicians are not independent contractors. Before attempting to decide whether the leader of the band or the ultimate purchaser of the band's services is the employer, the method of engaging musicians must be examined. The Bureau of Internal Revenue attempts to make a distinction between "name" orchestras and "non-name" orchestras for the purpose of determining who the employer of the musicians might be. Determination of whether any particular group of musicians constitutes a so-called "name" orchestra will not rest upon the mere adoption of a name by such organization, or on the fame of its leader. In general, the bureau considers an orchestra to be a "name" orchestra when, in addition to being designated by a name, it has a fixed personnel extending from engagement to engagement and holds itself out to the public as a permanent business organization. "Name" orchestras may also be distinguished from other orchestras by the fact that the leader of the orchestra

contracts with the members thereof for their services at a fixed salary, either by term or by engagement, and by the fact that the leader is liable for payment of such salary without reference to the discharge of the obligation by the purchaser of the music. It is the purpose of the bureau's distinction between "name" and "non-name" orchestras to hold that in the case of the former, the leader of the orchestra and not the purchaser is the employer subject to the payment of the payroll taxes. It will be noted that in general two types of organizations may be classified as "name" orchestras: (1) A nationally known band, usually incorporated, frequently with a distinctive style of music immediately recognizable to initiates. Persons well informed in music circles have estimated that there are not more than twenty such bands in the United States. (2) Orchestras which are well known in a limited locality, fixed in personnel because they are organized on a cooperative basis, that is, in the nature of a partnership with each member sharing equally, except that the leader or whoever secures the engagements may receive an additional amount for such extra services. Such a band may be known either by the name of its leader or under a collective name. 

No serious difficulty arises in holding that a "name" band of the first type should not be considered as in employment with the purchaser, since the leader as a matter of fact selects and discharges the members of his orchestra and alone controls and directs their conduct. He is subject to the payment of a fixed salary to each member of his organization regardless of whether they have engagements to play. Members of such bands usually receive much more than the scale price for musicians. In the case of the cooperative band, there is little reason for saying that the leader is the employer of the members comprising the organization. As a matter of fact, the bureau is in a dilemma in this regard. Once they found that such a cooperative band is a "name" band they are constrained to hold that the members of the band are not in employment of anyone, since the leader of the band is nothing more than "first among equals," without any authority to hire or discharge his associates or to control their activities except in so far as such authority is delegated to him. 

The distinction made by the bureau between "name" and "nonname" orchestras has found little favor with the state agencies, thereby creating a situation where an individual band leader may be an employer for purposes of the federal tax and an employee for purposes of the state unemployment compensation law. Some state agencies have regarded members of an orchestra as in the employment of the leader without attempting to determine whether the orchestra is a "name" or "non-name" orchestra within the bureau's definition. This has led to the conclusion that the orches-

tra men may not be covered at all, since they may not work long enough in any one state to subject the band leader to liability in that state.

The attitude of the musicians' unions is that the purchaser of the music, that is, the hotel, cafe, night club, etc., should bear the cost of the payroll taxes based on the services of all the members of the orchestra, including the leader. This would include both types of "name" orchestras, since it is the union's attitude that the purchaser who derives the ultimate benefit from the performance of the orchestra's services should bear the cost since he is in the best position of passing on its burden. The legal basis for holding that the purchaser of music is the employer in all cases, is that he has the right to control the hours of performance, the periods of intermission, and even the style of music or particular numbers to be played on any occasion. For purposes of most state acts, there is the further feature that the services of the orchestra are performed on the premises of the purchaser and in his usual course of business. A few states, in holding that the purchaser of the music is not the employer of the members of the orchestra, have said that the furnishing of music is no part of the usual business of running a hotel, night club, or cafe. This reasoning is patently absurd where the purchaser holds himself out as furnishing entertainment or a place to dance for the general public. There might be a little more reasoning in such a contention if the purchaser is a club or fraternity running one or two dances or entertainments annually and picking up a band for such occasions only. Even here, however, since the services are performed on the premises of the purchaser, there would seem to be small reason for a state agency to rule that the members of the band are not in the employment of the purchaser of the music. 

It is the present opinion of the bureau that in general, actors on the legitimate stage, vaudeville artists, and specialty performers of all sorts are in employment of the management, irrespective of the fact that such actors and artists may have been engaged for a stated period at a fixed amount of compensation, or that the sole consideration for the engagement was the unique and wellknown ability of the artists to entertain in their own particular manner. In such cases the management ordinarily designates the time of the performance and usually retains the right to approve or change any particular performance or portion thereof, and to require that such performance as a whole conform to the standards of the establishment.

Musical conservatories may have two types of teachers performing services for them. They may be hired to give classes to students regularly enrolled in the conservatory, their hours of work may be regulated, and the number of students in each class may be definitely fixed. Teachers of this type are deemed to be

in employment. The second type of teacher instructs his own pupils in space provided by the conservatory, for which a certain percentage of the teacher's gross income may be paid. They usually control their own pupils, who are required to sign up as students in the conservatory, which may then handle the registration, the collection of fees, and certain other details for the teacher. The Bureau of Internal Revenue has ruled that individuals in this second class are not in employment within the meaning of the taxing provision of the Social Security Act. It is fairly certain that most state agencies will agree with this determination. 

Commercial artists have given rise to the same sort of problem as music teachers in conservatories. A commercial artist who does not have his own studio may have quarters in a large studio, the management of which makes it its business to contact purchasers of commercial art for orders. These orders are then turned over to the artists to fill, and the artists may be paid either a percentage of the price or on an hourly basis. In many instances the artist may be permitted to use the studio even when working on jobs which he has secured through his own efforts. Whether or not commercial artists will be held to be employees of the studio depends on such factors as who provides the drawing materials, whether the artist is allowed to do his own work whenever he pleases, and whether he is controlled as to the manner of the performance of his work. Most state agencies will also make an effort to determine whether the artist can be said to be independently established in his own profession in the sense that his livelihood does not depend upon the jobs that may be turned over to him by the studio.

Accountants and auditors are usually cited as examples of individuals who are not employees. As a general statement this may be true, but no specific ruling can be given in any case unless all the facts are determined. The Bureau of Internal Revenue has held that individuals who perform services of a bookkeeping nature, as distinct from accounting or auditing, are to be considered as in employment even though their services may be intermittent. 

The reason for this ruling is that bookkeeping operations, including therein such functions as making payroll reports, making entries, and posting to ledgers or journals are all services involving no independent discretion and, therefore, of a nature that the employer has a right to control. However, when one gets into accounting, including therein the making of balance sheets and profit and loss statements, it is less apparent that control may be inferred from the nature of the operations. As for auditing and the preparation of complex tax returns, it is almost universally agreed that the performance of such services, when done by an individual who is independently established, will not lead to the imposition of liability through the employment relationship. Accountants who

are registered or certified usually maintain their own separate establishments, in which they hire their own employees and from which they offer their services to the public. A registered or certified accountant when engaged in preparing books or tax returns to which he certifies cannot be under the control of the person using his services, in so far as the performance of those services is concerned. A registered accountant may, however, be in employment when his services are limited to one business establishment which controls him as to the hours of work he must maintain and the books he is to keep.

Many small employers find their accounting needs adequately served by bookkeepers who come around to their establishments once a week, or even as rarely as once a month, to enter receipts and disbursements, make up profit and loss statements and balance sheets. This work may be done either at the employer's residence or in the bookkeeper's home or business establishment. 

Because they are not employed continuously, and because they may have a large number of accounts of this type, employers have usually maintained that such individuals are independent persons who need not be counted in determining their liability and on whose remuneration no contributions should be paid. The state agencies, however, have not always seen eye to eye with this contention, but on the contrary have ruled that the bookkeepers are in employment for each account whom they service. Whether they will be deemed to be in employment continuously or merely on the days on which they do their work is not always clear, since the written opinions of the state agencies do not always raise that problem. 

Since the states are going rather far in holding these individuals in employment on the grounds that a right to control exists, even when, as a matter of fact, no control may be exercised, it is probable that these agencies will consider the matter more leniently as far as the employer is concerned, by ruling that the bookkeeper is to be counted as in employment only on those days on which he actually performs work. 

An arrangement which is common in barber shops and beauty shops is for the owner of the equipment to "lease" out a booth or chair to the operator who, in some cases, furnishes his own equipment and, in others, uses the equipment and supplies of the owner. 

Such arrangements, however, have been looked upon with disfavor by all agencies which have ruled thereon, and the opinion definitely is that such individuals are in employment. A decision of the Supreme Court of Washington treats of this problem and comes to the same conclusion. Porters in barber shops may be allowed to operate a shoe-shining concession in the shop in return for doing the porter work without pay. A ruling of the Bureau of Internal Revenue says that such porters are subject to direction and control over the performance of their services and that they must be consid-

ered in employment. The problem of what their wages are is more difficult, since they do not actually receive money from the owner of the shop. The above ruling considered the remuneration of the porter as being the fair rental value of the shoe-shining concession. Although this will be difficult to determine, it is possible to make an estimate of its value, which amount should be reported as the remuneration of the porter.

The most common and difficult type of problem arising under the definitions of employment is that concerning salesmen on a strictly commission basis. It is the contention of employers that such commission salesmen are on their own, not being subject to direction or control, since their remuneration depends entirely on their own efforts. They are masters of their own time and are usually not required to report at any definite or stated intervals, but merely to forward their orders, when taken, to the central office of the establishment for whom they work. Insurance agents are probably the most common class involved. The history of rulings as to insurance agents is a complicated one and is likely to become more so in the future. The Federal Bureau of Internal Revenue first ruled that insurance agents performing services for life insurance companies which used the general agency type of organization were in employment since the usual contract between the company and the agent provided that the agent was to work exclusively for that company and could not write insurance with any other life insurance company unless the policies were first turned down by his own company. The life insurance companies protested, submitting additional facts to show that such contracts were not enforced and that, as a matter of fact, the agent was free to engage in other businesses, and was free to use his time and efforts in whatever manner he deemed best. The bureau then reversed itself and found that no substantial control was exercised by the general agent or by the home office over the activities of life insurance agents. This finding was sufficient under the Federal 

Social Security Act since, as was mentioned previously, that Act covers only the employment relationship as it was defined at common law. This ruling, however, did not apply to industrial insurance, since salesmen selling that type of insurance usually work on a "debit" and are strictly controlled as to their hours of work and methods of performing their services.

But as to the straight life insurance salesmen, the question was presented as to whether the state agencies would follow the rulings of the bureau in view of the fact that many of the state acts contain a definition of employment differing from that of the federal regulations. These state acts contain, in the usual case, three provisions which must be shown to exist concurrently before the performance of services can be said to be done outside of the employment relationship: The individual performing such

services must be free from direction and control; his services must be done either outside the usual course of business or outside of all the premises where the employer's business is usually conducted; and the individual performing the services must be engaged in an independently established trade, occupation, profession or business. It will be noted that the first of these provisions is the same as that which the federal bureau requires, but that the other two are only incidentally considered by the federal government. 

Since the state and federal acts do differ in this regard, and because the state agency is entitled to decide for itself on such questions, it was still open to the states to rule either way on whether life insurance agents are in employment or not. The bureau, however, exerts a considerable indirect influence, because of its prestige and because of the fact that the state agencies were reluctant to consider individuals in employment under the state act who were not considered in employment under the federal Act. 

The result was that many state agencies ruled out the insurance agents from the definition of employment on the ground that no control was exercised by the company over the performance of the agent's services, without going very thoroughly into the matter of determining whether such agents were, as a matter of fact, independently established, or whether they performed their services outside of the premises of the company. A few states, however, refused to follow the bureau's ruling and took the matter into court. Early in 1939 the Supreme Courts of Colorado and Connecticut handed down decisions on the status of life insurance agents.

The Colorado Supreme Court held the life insurance agents of a typical insurance company operating on the agency plan as in employment within the meaning of their unemployment compensation law. Their reasoning was that their decision had to be based on the statutory definition of employment rather than on the common law definition of employment, which considered only the control test. Limiting themselves, then, to the statutory definition of employment which enumerated the three requirements mentioned previously, the Colorado Supreme Court said that they could not say that the Colorado Unemployment Compensation

Commission was unreasonable in finding that a right to control existed, and that the agent was not independently established. 

They adopted, in effect, the finding of the commission on these points, and said that since the commission was reasonably justified in its position, the court would not overturn their finding that the conditions were not satisfied.

The Connecticut Supreme Court, on the other hand, refused to consider life insurance agents of the same company, operating in their state, as in employment. The Connecticut act does not contain the three requirements, but stresses only the question of

whether a right to control exists. This might seem to be a valid distinction between the two cases if an attempt were made to reconcile their contrary holdings, since Connecticut did not go into the question of whether the services were performed outside the premises of the company, or whether the agents were independently established in their own profession. As a matter of fact, the distinction is not a good one and the cases are contrary, since the Connecticut Supreme Court in ruling that these life insurance agents are not in employment, found, as a matter of fact, that no right to control existed, while the Colorado Supreme Court on an identical set of facts found that the commission was justified in holding that the right to control did exist. It is possible that the 

Colorado decision will induce those states which have not as yet ruled on the question to go ahead with plans to get a court decision on the point, thereby relieving themselves of the burden of deciding in the first instance that life insurance agents are not in employment. However, even in those states which have ruled that insurance agents are not in employment, they have qualified their decision by stating that it was made on an ex parte presentation of facts, subject to review if any life insurance agent should come in and claim benefits and prove that he was in employment within the meaning of the state law.

The matter of the life insurance agents is only a specialized example of the much wider problem of all sales agents. The factors which are gone into in determining the status of sales agents, or whatever other name the distributors of the firm's products are given, are much more varied than in the case of insurance agents. 

In one matter, the Bureau of Internal Revenue held a sales agent as in employment for a manufacturer under the following set of facts: The agent had to adhere strictly to the prices fixed by the company and to be governed by the rules and regulations in existence when the contract went into effect and any additions or amendments thereto. All sales made by the agent were subject to acceptance and approval of the company. The agent was allowed to maintain an office, and if he did so the name of the company was required to be placed on the office door and in the telephone directory. The agent was to maintain warehouse space suitable to and approved by the company for the purpose of storing such stock as was consigned to him. He was to furnish an inventory of the consigned stock each month and make such other reports as the company required. The agent could make no deliveries of stock except in accordance with the instructions of the company, and he could not engage in the sale or distribution of competitive products during the term of the contract. In return for his efforts, the agent received a commission on the retail prices of the products sold by him. Under such circumstances the bureau found

that control existed both under the contract and as a matter of fact. In other cases, however, the indicia of control have not been so clear, and closer questions have arisen for decision. 

In another matter decided by the bureau, the facts were that the sales agents of a certain firm were engaged by the district manager of that company and were given exclusive rights to sell goods of that company in their respective communities. The agent furnished a certain amount of capital with which to purchase his equipment. The company agreed to furnish merchandise at wholesale prices, and the agent agreed to purchase such merchandise and to sell it at list retail prices. The difference between the two prices constituted the agent's profit. The merchandise sold was shipped only to the sales agent and the company had no direct contact with the customers. The agent paid for the goods on a cash, C.O.D., or thirty-day credit basis. He paid all his own expenses and received no allowance from the company for reimbursements. He was not required to work certain hours, or to make any reports at all to the company. If he had an office, he had to pay his own rent, the salaries of his own employees, and all other expenses. Even though he had to fulfill a reasonable quota of sales and, under his contract, could not become interested directly or indirectly in any competitive enterprise, the bureau felt that on the whole, the facts showed that no right to control existed on the part of the company. As can be seen from the two examples given above, no one factor will be sufficient to determine whether control does or does not exist, but if a few of such factors are present, as restrictions in territory, imposition of a sales quota, restrictions in price or customers, requiring of reports of employers, carrying customer's accounts, then it is extremely probable that the bureau will consider the agents to be in employment. The fact that they pay all their own expenses and hire their own help is only presumptive evidence that they are not in employment, but such evidence is easily rebutted if it is proved that some of the factors listed above do exist. 

As a general proposition it may be stated that the state agencies are stricter in their requirements than the federal bureau. 

This is due to the fact that most state acts contain the three provisions of no control, services performed outside the premises, and an independent establishment, before the agency can consider the individual in question not to be in employment. It is, therefore, still an open question as to how a state agency will decide, even after the bureau has ruled that certain sales agents are not in employment. The state agency may give considerable weight to the ruling of the bureau, but it is quite within its province to disagree with the bureau's ruling.

A good example of a situation in which the states may differ from the rulings of the Bureau of Internal Revenue is found in the

case of real estate salesmen. The facts as stated in a bureau ruling were that licensed real estate salesmen who operated under the terms of written contracts work, upon listings of the company or upon leads which they personally obtain. They are not required to call upon particular customers or prospects or to make a specified number of calls during the day or month. They are furnished with office space, desk space, and other facilities but all the other expense incurred by the salesmen in making sales are borne by them. The only reports filed by these individuals are those concerning listings of the company and their activities in connection therewith. The salesmen are prohibited by law from performing services for any brokerage firm other than that for which they are registered. The bureau considered these individuals not to be in employment for the company with whom they were licensed. A few states, however, have considered that such individuals could not maintain that they were engaged in an independently established trade or profession of their own, and on that ground consider them to be in employment.

The federal government has ruled out many types of magazine salesmen who work on a strictly commission basis, who pay their own expenses, and who are not required to submit any reports except completed subscription blanks. As in all other cases of salesmen, many states have preferred to follow the ruling of the bureau, even though such magazine salesmen are not independently established in their own business, since they possess nothing but their ability to sell. A few states have refused to follow the ruling of the bureau in this regard since they consider that there are certain elements of control exercised in the fact that many of these salesmen travel in crews led by a salaried supervisor of the publishing company. Country correspondents of newspapers have uniformly been considered not to be in employment, both by the states and the federal government, where their remuneration is based on the number of lines of news items accepted by the paper and where they are under no obligation to submit material at stated intervals. 

The Bureau of Internal Revenue does not consider newsboys as employees if they purchase newspapers from the publisher and sell them in such manner and at such time and place as they choose. 

The newsboy retains as his profit the difference between the purchase price and the selling price of the newspapers. Where a publisher designates an individual as a "distributor agent” who engages carriers to deliver newspapers during certain hours each day to customers in a particular territory, the bureau has ruled that the carrier is an employee. In such cases the carrier is required to render statements showing the amounts owed by customers, but he does not collect such amounts. The list of customers is subject to change from time to time and is not the property of the carrier. Whether the carrier is an employee of the distributor

agent or of the publisher depends on the status of the former. If he himself is an independent contractor, then he is the employer of the carriers; otherwise the publisher is the employer. It should be noted that under those state acts containing a contractor clause which imposes liability on the principal employer where a contractor does work in the usual course of the employer's business, the carriers would be considered in employment for the publisher unless he and the distributor agent were both employers subject to the state law.

The type of work done by home workers is usually such as to require control being exercised by the employer, in that definite instructions are given as to the manner in which the work is to be performed. In such instances, of course, the home workers are in employment for the purpose of all payroll taxes, even though the remuneration is based on the amount of work done rather than on the number of hours expended, and even though more than one member of a family may help in doing the work. Certain individuals skilled in fine needle work are accustomed to doing such work at home, performing their services for several companies. In fact, they may themselves let some of the work out to less skilled operators. They are, in a sense, small business people and can be regarded as independently established in that trade, and if no control is exercised over the manner of the performance of their duties, they will be considered not to be in employment for any of the companies for whom they are performing such services. 

Whether taxicab drivers are in employment is strictly dependent on the facts in each individual case. There are, however, certain types of arrangements which are fairly common throughout the country and on which the Bureau of Internal Revenue has ruled. In one case the taxicab company owned the taxicabs, which were operated by drivers under a contract providing that the company was to furnish taxicabs suitable for operation on a rental basis and was to furnish at its own expense all oil and other supplies, except gasoline; to make all needed repairs, except those resulting from an accident; to furnish garage space, office and telephone and an employee to receive calls; and to furnish liability insurance covering the passengers carried for hire by the driver; the driver was to agree to pay the rental specified and to operate the cab for the purpose of carrying persons for hire only; to report every accident in writing within twenty-four hours; and to obey in every particular the existing rules of the company. The fact that the remuneration of the driver was not fixed but was based on the amount he could earn over and above the rental and cost of gasoline was not considered to be controlling in view of the minute instructions which the company imposed on its drivers. Many states have ruled drivers of cabs not to be in employment where they pay a daily rental fee and where the taxicab company fur-

nishes them with nothing but an employee who receives calls which the driver is free to accept or not as he sees fit. No control is exercised over these drivers except that they may be required to carry liability insurance in favor of the taxicab company. It is not possible to state how any state agency will rule in a particular case involving taxicab drivers, since slight differences in fact may very well cause a change in ruling. The bureau has furnished an opinion that taxicab drivers are not in employment under the following set of facts: The company or taxi association is paid a daily fee for telephone service by the drivers in question. The association enters into a conditional sales agreement with the taxicab operators in which it is provided that the association shall sell and the owner-driver shall buy a certain taxicab and pay for taxicab telephone calls. The owner-driver agrees to pay all taxi license fees and charges for the operation of the taxicab, and to use it solely for passenger hire. On making a down payment for the cab, the driver executes a note for the unpaid balance and agrees to make specified daily payments until the taxicab is paid for in full. The only interest that the owner-driver has in the association is the privilege of purchasing telephone service. No control is exercised over the management of the taxicab or the operator. 

A truck owner who entered into an agreement with a business firm to furnish a truck and driver and to pay all expense of operation for a stipulated sum per month was held by the Bureau of Internal Revenue to be in employment where the contract provided that the truck and driver were to be available to the company for exclusive use for delivery purposes between the hours of eight o'clock in the morning and five o'clock in the afternoon. The owner usually drove the truck, but at certain times during the year, due to other business interests, furnished a driver, whom he employed himself, for the truck. The truck owner does not furnish similar service to any other concern. The company instructs the driver as to where and in what order deliveries are to be made, and may actually control the activities of the owner or his substitute driver. Even though no actual control were established, it is probable that most state agencies would consider both the owner and the substitute driver as in employment for the company, since the owner was not independently established. The fact that he owns only one truck which is available during business hours for one company only would tend to prove this fact, even though it might be shown that after business hours the truck was used for other jobs.

Large trucking companies which, at certain rush times, lease trucks and other equipment from their individual owners have been held to be the employers of such truck owners in the following circumstances: The owners might drive the trucks themselves or engage substitutes; while being used by the trucking company

they bear lettering identifying them as being operated in the name of that company; the trucking company requires the drivers to observe the regulations issued by the Interstate Commerce Commission and the laws of the various states through which they travel; under the terms of the certificate granted to the company by the Interstate Commerce Commission, the drivers are required to present their specified routes between designated locations and to have certain information marked on the trucks; on long distance hauls there are specified stations where the drivers are required to register in order that the company may know the location of its freight.

A problem of the utmost importance which all the state agencies must meet in the near future is that of gasoline station operators. Before 1934 it was the common practice in the industry for the large oil companies to have paid employees in the great majority of their owned or leased properties. Due to the imposition of chain store taxes in many states, the oil companies attempted to divest themselves of the indicia of ownership or control over such stations by leasing them out to individuals, in many instances to the former salaried managers of the stations. Whether the lessee operator of a gasoline station will be considered to be in employment for an oil company will depend largely on whether the lease is considered to be a bona fide arrangement or merely an attempt to evade the imposition of chain store and payroll taxes. 

Thirty or sixty-day leases, with a clause providing for cancellation on twenty-four hour notice, will be considered by the state agencies as almost irrebuttable evidence that the company still maintains control over the "lessee," because of its power of economic life and death. If, however, the lease is for six months or more, and does not incorporate a sales contract for the exclusive use of the company's gasoline or oil, it is probable that most state agencies will consider such leases as valid leases of property rather than merely franchises to run the gasoline station until it pleases the gasoline company to dispossess the operator. The means of control used by certain large oil companies are subtle and extremely hard to determine in any actual case. Many of the state agencies have not as yet ruled on the problem of whether the oil companies are the employers of all the individuals performing services in stations leased from them, but prefer to wait until an individual claims benefits on the ground that he was in employment for the oil company. This procedure would enable the state agency to get the employee's side of the picture, which is an important consideration where so many thousands of individuals are involved. 

Bulk plants owned by oil companies and used for the distribution and sale of their products in a prescribed territory have been held by the bureau to impose the employment relationship on the operator. The facts in one such case were that the operator or

agent for the oil company was required to devote his full business time and best efforts to the performance of his duties for the company and was prohibited from engaging directly or indirectly in any occupation that might interfere with such duties. The agent was further required to sell a certain prescribed minimum quantity of the company's products, and all sales were made by him according to the company's prevailing prices and on terms and conditions satisfactory to the company. All sales were made pursuant to written contracts between the company and the customers. The company delivered to the agent, on consignment, such quantities of its products as in its own discretion were an adequate and suitable supply, such stock remaining the company's property until delivered by the agent to the purchaser. Accounts and records covering the agent's activities had to be maintained as the company directed. 

He was also required to render full and complete reports to the company within twenty-four hours after making any deposit, entering into any contract, or receiving any order. The company prescribed the forms on which the agent's accounts must be kept, and it could inspect all of the records and accounts of the agent at any time. Not only the agent but also all his employees were considered to be in employment for the oil company. 

The problems relating to the employment relationship under the federal and state payroll taxes will eventually bring about great changes in the old master-servant relationship. The states are working gradually toward a new concept on which to impose payroll taxes, namely, that the absence of actual physical control over the manner of performance of services by an individual is not sufficient to establish a relationship of no employment, but that where one person has the power to deprive another, although temporarily, of his sole means of livelihood through the selling of his services, he should be considered to be that person's employer. 

Compensation for Personal Services. The basis on which payroll taxes are computed is the amount of wages or compensation for personal services which an employer pays to his employees in employment not excluded under the acts. It becomes important, therefore, to determine what items of remuneration are taxable and what items are deductible either as payments for other than personal services or as not arising from the employment relationship. Wages are uniformly defined by state unemployment compensation laws or regulations and by the taxing provisions of the Social Security Act as all remuneration for employment whether payable in money or something other than money. The name by which the remuneration is designated is immaterial. Thus, salaries, commissions on sales, fees, bonuses, prizes, gifts, "profits" on sales, are all wages if they are payable by an employer to his employee as compensation for non-exempted services. It must be remem-

bered, however, that the employment relationship is a status not necessarily dependent on the payment of remuneration, and the absence of wages is not material in determining that status. Thus, individuals who are actually performing services, even though such services are without pay, are in employment. Officers of subsidiary corporations who are paid by the holding corporation are in employment for each subsidiary, even though they may not be paid either directly or indirectly by the subsidiary. 

Not all remuneration, however, is included as taxable wages, even when it is admitted to be for personal services performed in the employment relationship. Thus, the Old Age Insurance provisions exclude from the definition of taxable wages all amounts paid to any employee in excess of $3,000 from any one employer in one calendar year. The Railroad Retirement Act and the Railroad Un employment Insurance Act do not consider as wages any remuneration in excess of $300 per month. Some of the state laws also exclude remuneration above a certain amount from the taxable basis. 

(See Chapter IV, page 87.) 

Since wages under the definitions in payroll tax legislation may be paid only to employees, the drawings or salaries (by whatever name known) of employers are not taxable. Thus, individual proprietors, partners, trustees and receivers, executors and administrators, since they are not employees, are not considered as receiving taxable remuneration. Where a partner is entitled to a salary, in addition to his percentage of the profits, the question has arisen as to whether such additional remuneration is taxable. It is the opinion of those state agencies, such as Texas and Connecticut, which have discussed this problem, that they are not taxable on the ground that all remuneration paid to a partner is paid to him as an employer and not as an employee. 

Salaries of officers, however, are taxable even though the officer may be the controlling stockholder or even the sole owner. If such officer draws his remuneration in the form of dividends and is able to substantiate by his accounting records that such is the case, such payments will not be deemed to be for personal services but merely profits from the business, on which no payroll tax is due. 

Directors' fees, which are not excessive in amount, are not wages, but if the payment of such fees is deemed to be an attempt by which officers draw their remuneration for services, the state agency or the bureau will consider the entire amount taxable. If, in addition to serving as a director, an individual performs extra services for the corporation, any payment he receives therefor will be treated as wages.

Royalties are not wages but only a payment for the right to use an invention. Even though the person to whom the royalties are paid is in employment, the employer need consider as taxable only the remuneration payable for personal services.

One of the major differences between the federal and state interpretations of what constitutes wages concerns tips or gratuities. These are considered by practically all state unemployment compensation laws as wages where they are customarily received by an individual in the course of his work from persons other than his employing unit. Under the federal provisions, tips or gratuities paid directly to an employee by a customer of the employing unit and not in any way accounted for by the employee to his employing unit do not constitute wages. If the employee merely furnishes his employer with a written statement of the amount of his tips for the purpose of enabling the employer to comply with the requirement of a state unemployment compensation law, it will not be considered to be such an "accounting" as was contemplated by the federal regulations. An example of an accounting to an employer of tips received by his employee, within the meaning of the federal regulations, is when a hotel or restaurant guarantees its waiters a fixed income, consisting partly of remuneration paid by the restaurant and partly of tips. Inasmuch as the employer bases the remuneration of his employees on the amount of such tips, and since the employee himself informs the employer of the amounts of tips received in order that the latter may compute the balance due to the employee, it is clear that there has been such an accounting as will make the tips taxable. 

Where a club does not permit the tipping of employees but in lieu thereof adds ten per cent to the cafe charges made against the members' accounts, and disburses the added amounts monthly to the club's waiters, the sums so disbursed constitute wages. Such amounts are not considered to be tips or gratuities payable by the members since the ten per cent charge is an arbitrary one fixed by the club which the member is required to pay whether he wishes to or not. But where a member of a club, instead of directly giving the waiter a tip, writes the amount thereof on his check and the hotel pays the tip to the waiter, charging it to the member's account, the payment constitutes a tip that is not taxable under the federal regulations. In all the examples given above, the additional payments to the waiters would be considered to be taxable wages under the state rules since they are customarily received by the individual in the course of his work, either from his employer or from other persons.

For the purposes of state unemployment compensation laws it is incumbent upon the employer to ascertain or to estimate, as best he can, the amount of tips paid to his employees so that he can compute the contributions due. The regulations of most state laws forbid the employer to require his employees to disclose the exact amount of tips, and if the employee does not willingly do so, the employer must use whatever reasonable basis of estimating such amounts as are available to him. The character of the establish-

ments, that is, whether it is one where tips are customarily paid, and the number of people served, will have a bearing on the reasonableness of the employer's estimate of tips payable.

It is a fairly common occurrence in business for payments to be made even though no immediate personal services were rendered by the recipient of such amounts. Pensions to retired employees are one of the most common examples. If the employee performs no services and is not required to do so under the terms of the pension, it is the ruling of all the state agencies and the Bureau of Internal Revenue that such amounts are not taxable. 

They are paid because of the former existence of an employment relationship, but no personal services are rendered at the time of the payment. Individuals who are permanently disabled but receive remuneration are also considered not to be presently employed, and the remuneration paid them is not taxable. However, if the individual is only temporarily disabled and has not been dismissed from his employment, any payment made to him for such disability is considered to be wages by the bureau, and presumably the state agencies will follow this reasoning. Sick pay falls in the same category as payments for temporary disability, and must be included in the computation of the tax or contribution due. Because of the existence of the employment relationship, employees who receive vacation pay must be counted as in employment for the weeks of their vacation, and the remuneration received is considered as taxable wages.

Workmen's compensation benefits for either temporary or permanent disability are not wages since they are not received because of the employment relationship. If the employer makes up the difference between the workmen's compensation payment and the employee's regular salary, such excess will be considered to be wages. Payments to a widow of a former employee are treated as gifts on which no contribution is due, since the payment is made to a person not in employment. If the payment to the widow, however, is not a gift but consists of amounts due to the husband before his death, such as commissions on sales, it is not certain whether such amounts should be treated as taxable wages or not. 

Some states treat the commissions as being payable for personal services of the husband and, therefore, taxable as his wages. It will probably be correct to adopt the following principle in cases of this sort: That if the payment would not have been required to be made to the husband, it may be treated as a gift to the widow on which no contributions will be due.

Payments made to an employee while he is serving in the National Guard, to bring up his pay during such weeks to his normal wage, have been considered to be made because of the employment relationship and, therefore, taxable. There have been a few rulings on the question of whether amounts paid by an employer to an

employee on his marriage, or on the birth of a child, constitute wages, and the uniform opinion is that such payments are not taxable since they are not paid for services rendered in the employment relationship.

Two items of remuneration on which there is little consistency in the rulings are dismissal wages and wages in lieu of notice. 

Except in New York, such items are treated as taxable wages even though the payment is not made for services rendered, or because of the employment relationship. The bureau attempted to justify its holding that dismissal wages are taxable, on the ground that they represented remuneration for the separation. 

A more logical position, and one in stricter conformity to the actual facts, would be that dismissal wages and wages in lieu of notice should not be considered as taxable since the employment relationship has ended and the payment is not rendered for services performed. Since, however, the majority opinion of the state agencies is that such items are taxable wages, the question arises as to how they should be treated for reporting purposes, and for purposes of disqualification for benefits. If they are remuneration for past services, they should be reported for such past periods in which the services were performed, but this would lead to the absurd conclusion that the payment would be allocated back over the entire period of employment. The bureau considers them as remuneration at the moment of dismissal, and requires them to be reported at that time. The state agencies also treat it in this manner, but regard such payments as disqualifying an individual for benefits in those succeeding weeks to which it is applicable. If the dismissal wage is not based on the weekly salary but is paid as a lump sum, based on past years of service, it is difficult to see to what periods of time after the separation such payments should be related.

Prizes and gifts are usually given by an employer as additional compensation in the nature of a bonus for extra effort or faithful service. As such it is remuneration arising out of the em ployment relationship and, therefore, taxable. Neither the prize nor gift need be in cash; but whatever form it may take, from an extra five dollar bill at Christmas to the most expensive automobile given in a sales contest, it must be considered as part of the tax base and contributions or federal payroll taxes paid thereon. 

If not in cash its value must be reduced to monetary terms in the light of its fair market value.

The most common form of non-monetary remuneration is board and lodging. This is included as taxable wages for all payroll tax purposes and each state agency has issued rules determining what is the value of board and lodging in the absence of a specific agreement between the employer and employees. The amount set by the state agency is a minimum amount and no em-

ployer may value the board and lodging given employees at less than that figure unless he has received authorization from the agency. For purposes of determining the federal tax on board and lodging, the bureau will allow employers to use the same figure used for the state unemployment compensation law. In the case of employers subject to the Old Age Insurance provisions, but not to a state unemployment compensation law, it is probable that the bureau will allow them to use whatever standard has been adopted for liable employers in that state. 

If facilities such as board and lodging are given to the employee as a convenience to the employer and not as additional remuneration to the employee, the bureau may allow the value of such board and lodging to be considered as non-taxable. The state agencies have frequently ruled the same way, even though their rules and regulations do not mention that remuneration in kind is not subject to contributions if given only as a convenience to the employer. In one federal ruling the bureau allowed the value of lunches served by a company to its employees to be considered as given solely for the convenience of the employer and, therefore, not taxable. In that case no reduction was made in the remuneration of the employees when the lunch room privileges were first afforded, full remuneration being paid to each employee whether or not he availed himself of this privilege. If the company decided to close the lunch room, or charged for the lunches, such event would not affect the contract of employment or change the scale of remuneration of the employees. The company furnished free lunches so as to reduce the duration of the midday lunch period to a minimum and because during the noon hour much business was transacted. In another case, however, the bureau ruled that board and lodging given employees of a sanitarium located in an isolated mountainous region was additional remuneration. When these employees were hired it was with the express understanding that they were to remain available for duty on call and that they were to accept the board and lodging provided. The salaries of many of those employees who did not live at the sanitarium were in excess of those paid to individuals performing similar service who did live at the sanitarium.

The federal regulations dealing with what items of remuneration are not taxable because they are given only as a convenience to the employer, are as follows: "Ordinarily the facilities or privileges (such as entertainment, cafeterias, restaurants, medical services, or so-called 'courtesy' discounts on purchases) furnished or offered by an employer to his employees generally, are not considered as remuneration for services if such facilities or privileges are offered or furnished by the employer merely as a con-

venience to the employer or as a means of promoting the health, good-will, contentment, or efficiency of his employees.' 

It will be noted that the use of the parenthetical expressions above was meant to limit the facilities or privileges referred to, to items which are insubstantial in character and value. It seems, therefore, to be the latest opinion of the bureau as expressed in the ruling on employees of a sanitarium that this provision will not ordinarily have application to such items as board and lodging. 

It is a common practice in certain types of establishments, such as barber shops, to give an individual, who is in employment to the owner of such shop, a concession whereby he may earn money for himself and in return for which he pays either a small rental or else offers certain other services to the owner of the establishment in return. Once it is determined that the individual is in employment it becomes necessary to set some value on his remuneration, which is considered to be the value of the concession given to him. Since the value of the concession depends on what he can make from it, the contributions should be paid on such amount as is estimated to be the fair rental value of such concession.

It is the practice in many corporations to offer employees as additional remuneration for their services, stocks or bonds either given outright or sold at a reduced price. In the latter situation, the additional remuneration to the employee, which must be reduced to monetary terms, is the difference between the fair market price of the stocks or bonds and the price at which it is offered to the employee. If the stocks are given outright to him the tax should be paid on their fair market value. If a regular amount is deducted periodically from an employee's salary, which is to go toward purchase of stock in the company at the regular market price, the tax should be paid on the total salary before deduction. 

In that case, when the stock is issued to the employee on completion of payment, there would be no further payroll tax liability, and no need to determine the value of the stock. 

Whenever the value of the stock must be determined for payroll tax purposes, its fair market value will be fixed by means of the following types of evidence: Actual sales at or about the date of valuation will usually show the value that is most reasonable. 

But where sales are infrequent and in small quantities, or occur quite a while before or after valuation date, the importance of sales as indications of value is reduced. But even where sales alone are considered, there is still the problem of choosing between different sales prices on or about valuation date. The federal estate tax regulations provide that the value of stocks or bonds shall be obtained by taking the mean between the highest and lowest

the sions to, to

SURE ging ents ear the idual his 

DIGES 

TO SE Such es as the tion De re art fered e tar tis to arke ction mole Ciley meble ce di aloxe state shal quoted selling price upon the date of death. There are no analogous regulations either for the income tax or payroll tax statutes. Even if there were, however, such a rule would be of value only in the case of an active stock quoted on a stock exchange. What is to be done in the case of fairly inactive stocks when the sales price is relied on as evidence of fair market value? It is quite proper in such cases to take a range of prices over a considerable period of time, although averages of such a range of prices might be of less value.

Although market prices are the best evidence of value, they are not conclusive on that point. To overcome a presumption that market quotations are the best evidence of value, taxpayers have endeavored to prove that the prices were influenced by a stock market boom, and hence were not an index of "true values," or that the stock was rendered less valuable by restrictive agreements against sale. Since many stock sales to employees are made with agreements not to sell except under specified conditions, it might seem that this would lessen the fair market value of the stock. 

The courts, however, have not been much influenced by such an argument, preferring to hold that market quotations were the best index of value even though the taxpayer could not himself have sold at that price.

In the case of close corporations where there has been no public offering of the stock, the prices at which whatever sales there were have been made, do not have the same probative effect as to value, as in the case of an actively traded stock. In close corporations the sales are often made, not to the highest bidder, but to favored individuals such as present or prospective officers, and to hold that they indicate the fair value of the stock would often be contrary to the true facts. Methods other than sales prices or market quotations must be resorted to for the determination of value in such instances.

For stocks not listed but traded in "over the counter," or listed but inactive, there is frequently offered as evidence of fair value the bid and asked prices. Apart from the consideration that bid and asked prices are not always accurately reported, there is the fact that no indication is given in such quotations of what quantity could be purchased or sold at the price range in question. An offer to dispose of a controlling interest might bring a much higher bid per share than an offer to sell a minority interest. Even if that were adjusted, there still remains the fact that bid and asked prices merely set the range in which the value might fall, but do not fix the value. When the courts do rely on bid and asked prices, they will usually take the average between them as the best indication of the price at which a willing seller could be induced to sell and a willing buyer to purchase.

Under the laws of most states, a corporation may not legally issue stock for less than par in money or money's worth. This would imply that when the corporation was organized it would have assets at least equal to the par value of its issued stock. 

Beyond this, the inference has no validity. There may be more or less water in the stock, the assets may be dissipated shortly after organization, or the stock may even have been sold at a premium. In any of these events, the par value of the stock may be far removed from its fair market value.

The same considerations would lessen the evidentiary significance to be given the book value of the stock in determining its fair value. In many of the stock valuation cases, book values are usually taken only as a starting point from which to add or subtract, in line with other evidence, in arriving at the value of the shares. Only in those rare cases when no other evidence as to value is offered will the courts be tempted to take the unweighted book values as the true valuation.

The past or prospective earnings of a corporation can be used in arriving at the valuation of stock in one of two ways-by figuring the earnings as a basis on which to value the corporate assets in their entirety. The value of the stock is then derived from the asset value on a pro rata share. The other method is to calculate directly the earnings applicable to each share and to value the share at ten or twenty times the annual income. Dividends, the prospect of continued profitable operation, good will, and the reputation of the management are other factors which have more or less weight in reaching the fair value of shares of stock. 

Items such as tuition which the employer pays for the employee are considered as additional remuneration on which contributions must be paid. If the employee pays for the tuition in the first instance and then is reimbursed by his employer, such payment will also be considered to be taxable since it is given only because of the employment relationship.

However, if an employee incurs expenses which are accounted for to the employer and such expenses arise because of business efforts, such items when reimbursed are not considered as wages. 

If the employer pays the employee a flat sum out of which the employee must pay all his business expenses, the employer may deduct the actual expenses incurred by the employee if they are accounted for to him. If they are not accounted for, they are not deductible even though they were actually expended. Although there are no formal rulings on what period the accounting may cover, it seems reasonable to expect that a state agency will not allow an employer to deduct expenses if the employee accounts at as long an interval as one year. The employer should require periodical accounting on at least a monthly basis in order to avail himself of such deduction.

Any special equipment which the employee may have to buy and which can be used only in his employment can be considered as a business expense which the employee has to bear, and which is, therefore, not taxable since it is not paid for personal services. 

Thus, amounts representing expenditures for supplies purchased by a miner either from the mine company or from outside sources to be used in mining operations may be deducted in computing his wages for payroll tax purposes, provided such expenditures are accounted for to the employer. Amounts representing charges or expenditures for explosives, fuses, cartridge paper, lamps, smithing service, tools, and safety clothing are not remuneration for employment and hence are not wages. However, charges by the mining company for supplies or service to the miner for his personal use, such as rent, fuel and clothing, taxes, dues, fees, medical, hospital and bath house expenses, must be included in the computation of taxable wages.

If an employee has to work overtime and is thus required to expend extra money for his supper, which amount is reimbursed him, it will not be considered as wages for personal services and, therefore, would not be taxable. If, however, lunches are furnished to employees, they will be considered as taxable since they represent remuneration to the employee for an expense which he would otherwise be required to pay himself.

The premiums paid by an employer on a policy of life insurance covering the life of an employee constitute wages unless the employer himself is the beneficiary under the policy.
