If the employer pays premiums on policies of group life insurance covering the lives of his employees, they will not be considered to be wages if the employee does not have an option to take such amounts of the premiums instead of accepting the insurance, and if the employee has no equity in the policy such as the right of assignment or the right to the surrender value on the termination of his employment. If an employer makes payments into a stock, bonus, pension, or profit-sharing fund, and such payments inure to the exclusive benefit of the employee, and may be withdrawn by him at any time or upon resignation or dismissal, such payments will be considered as additional remuneration to the employee and, therefore, taxable. Further, if the contract of employment requires such payments to be made by the employer, they will be considered as additional remuneration. If such payments do not inure to the exclusive benefit of any employee, as is usually the case in funds for the purpose of paying sick wages, the question arises as to whether payments from the fund will be considered as wages to a sick employee. If the employer maintains the fund, it is probable that all state agencies would consider such payments as wages. Where, however, the employer pays into a pension fund controlled by trustees who are not controlled by the company, it is

doubtful whether such payments could be considered to be wages unless the employee could withdraw the sums paid by his employer at any time, or upon resignation or dismissal. 

The opinion of the Bureau of Internal Revenue, which is in this case the concensus of the state agencies, is that payments by an employer of his employees' payroll tax liability does not constitute additional wages to the employee. Thus, if the employer pays the employees' Old Age Insurance tax, he need not consider it as an additional payment to the employee on which an additional tax is to be paid.

CHAPTER VIII PAYROLL TAXES AND ACCOUNTING 

Payroll taxes are as much a problem for the accountant and auditor as they are for the attorney. Once a determination has been made as to whether certain services are performed in the employment relationship or outside, or that a certain item of remuneration is taxable or not, there still remain the difficulties in reflecting such legal decisions on the books of the employer. Certain elementary or fundamental information which all employing units are required to keep is set out by the state unemployment compensation regulations. These require, in general, that the employer keep in his records the names and Social Security account numbers of all individuals performing services for him; the amount of wages they receive; the amount of time spent; the place of employment; and the wages payable for each pay period. The employer should take into account in determining what records will be adequate, not only his own needs but also the requirements placed on him by the various state and federal tax and regulatory statutes, such as the Wages and Hours law, and the various minimum wage or maximum hours laws of the states in which he operates. If different types of records are now kept to comply with these different statutes, a definite savings can be accomplished by revising the accounting procedures so as to combine the differing requirements into one set of books. 

For payroll tax purposes, the employer or his accountant should clearly segregate taxable from nontaxable items. If employees work outside the state, that fact should be indicated; if employees work both within and without the state, some note should be kept as to what are the varying percentages of time spent in each state and where their base of operations is located. 

Since the federal payroll taxes cover only wages for service performed within the United States, it is important to show what proportion of an employee's wages are payable for services outside the country.

If payment is made in a lump sum to cover both personal services and the use of equipment, such as trucks, etc., the employer's records must disclose what percentage is in payment for personal services and what percentage is for the rental of the 213

equipment. If no agreement has been reached between the parties it may be well to arrive at some definite understanding as to what part of the lump sum shall be considered as payment for personal services and is, therefore, taxable. The state agencies and the bureau are not requiring complete breakdowns of actual expenses incurred by owners of equipment such as trucks who perform personal services, so that an agreement between the parties as to what is the rental value of such equipment will usually be sufficient, unless the price agreed upon for payment of personal services is extremely low, e.g., below 25 per cent of the total payment. If part of the remuneration paid to an employee is for supplies necessary for his work, the employer need not pay any payroll tax on such amounts and should keep payroll records to substantiate his claim. 

Employees who hire assistants, either with the actual or constructive knowledge of the employer, should be instructed to report the names, Social Security numbers, and amount of wages earned by each such assistant, since they will be regarded as employees of the employer. It will not be sufficient for payroll tax purposes to show these amounts as a lump sum, since the state agencies require the wages payable to each employee to be listed. 

If an employer engages in both covered and exempted employment, his records should disclose the occupations of such individuals, and if the same individuals work in both types of employment, the amount of time and wages earned with respect to each such type. Thus, florists and nursery owners with some employment considered to be exempted under the federal or state payroll taxes should clearly indicate on their records whether an individual is engaged in planting or transplanting agricultural products or is engaged in a non-exempted type of employment as a fireman, bookkeeper, or stenographer.

Employers who have salesmen and who expect to deduct such salesmen's expenses from the amount of taxable wages must insist on receiving current accounts of all business expenses incurred, since it is only through an accounting by the employee to his employer that the employer will be allowed to take credit for such deductions. In general, the same items that would be deductible from a salesman's gross income for income tax purposes will be deductible as business expenses in determining the net remuneration for his personal services.

Salesmen working on a commission basis frequently are charged back with commissions earned on sales which have been cancelled. This operates to reduce their earnings for the quarter in which the charge-back is made, and many employers have reported only the amount of the earnings subsequent to such deductions. For purposes of the Title IX tax, this will make no difference unless the charge-back is made in a calendar year subsequent to the one when the commission was first earned. Since

PAYROLL TAXES AND ACCOUNTING 

215 this tax is based on the services which are performed for which wages become payable, it is clear that the tax should be paid for that year in which the services were performed. If the wages payable for such services are reduced in a subsequent year, the Bureau of Internal Revenue requires the employer to file a claim for credit or refund for the excess amount of commissions paid in the prior year and to report the full amount of commissions earned before charge-backs on cancelled sales.

The problem becomes even more aggravated in considering unemployment compensation contributions for the state agencies, since such reports are made either monthly or quarterly. If the same procedure were required to be followed it would mean that the employer would have to report the gross amount of commissions each quarter and if there were any cancelled sales for which charge-backs were entered, credit could be taken for them only by filing claims for adjustments or refunds. Illinois, for example, requires this procedure to be followed. Other states, however, permit the employer to make his own adjustment without the necessity of filing a formal claim with the agency. Wherever such adjustments are made, it is important for the employer to keep adequate records explaining the nature of such adjustments and the periods for which they are applicable.

A system of reporting based on wages payable requires the employer to accrue his payroll liabilities at the end of the reporting period and pay taxes based on the full amount of his liability, even though at such time the wages have not yet been paid. The Title 

IX tax, being on the wages payable basis, must be based on all wages which have been paid, or which are now payable, or which will at any time in the future become payable if they are for services performed in the current taxable year. For one thing, the employer will have to accrue the wages payable between his last payroll period in any year and December 31 of that year. For example, if wages are paid through December 27, the Title IX tax, and all state unemployment compensation contributions, will be based on all wages paid, plus such amount as is payable for the period up to and including December 31.

Further, if it is contemplated that bonuses or other special payments will be made in the next year based on services performed in the current taxable year, an estimate of such special payments must be made and the required contributions and tax paid thereon. If the estimate is not made, the employer will not have paid on the full amount of his liability, and since he can only take credit against the full amount of his Title IX tax for payments made before the due date of filing his federal return, he will not have availed himself of his full credit in not making such an estimate. In other words, if an employer has a $100,000 payroll for the year 1939, his contributions to a state agency will

be $2,700 and his Title IX tax, after deduction for payments to a state fund, will be $300, or a total tax liability of $3,000. If this employer makes a special bonus payment of $25,000 in April of 1940 which he did not estimate for his 1939 tax returns, he will have to pay the state agency 2.7 per cent of that sum and, in addition, must pay the full 3 per cent on the same sum as a federal tax, since his payment to the state will not entitle him to any credit, having been made after the due date of filing his federal tax return.

Where special payments are made before the end of a year, based on services performed during that year, no question of an estimate arises for federal purposes. The state agencies, however, require the bonus or special payment to be reported on separate wage reporting forms, so that the proper amounts will be allocated to each quarter for which the bonus represents extra payment. 

The purpose of such allocation is to make sure that each employee will be credited with all the wages, including extra remuneration, which he has earned during each quarter. Even though his total wages may not be increased during the year by such a procedure, it may serve to spread his earnings more evenly during the individual quarters and thereby insure him against having abnormally high earnings in any one quarter. Employers also are interested in preventing too high earnings in one quarter in those states where the weekly benefit amount is figured as a percentage of the earnings in that quarter in which the employee's wages were highest.

The old age insurance taxes imposed by Title VIII of the 

Social Security Act are on a wages paid basis. An employer must report only the actual amount of wages he has paid during the calendar quarter, except that he has the option of including in his tax report for any quarter the amount of wages paid for a payroll period beginning in such calendar quarter but ending in the month following such quarter. If an employer seeks to use his state unemployment compensation return as a carbon copy of his federal form SS-1a, the tax return for the old age insurance taxes, he should not exercise his option since that would make a difference between the amounts of wages reported for the two taxes. 

State contributions are based on wages earned for all payroll periods ending within the quarter. Strict adherence to the state regulations means that an employer could not report on his state return, wages earned during a payroll period ending in the month subsequent to the close of the calendar quarter, while under the option given by the federal old age insurance regulations he could include such amount in his tax return.

A few states, including Illinois, Connecticut, and New Jersey, allow certain employers to report the wages earned by their employees when such employees are separated from the payroll, in-

PAYROLL TAXES AND ACCOUNTING 

217 stead of reporting the wages payable to each employee every quarter. This may save large employers a considerable amount of time and bookkeeping expense, although the federal old age insurance return must still be made quarterly. The states permitting separations reporting instead of current individual wage reports grant such permission only if the employer can satisfy stringent requirements dealing with financial stability, showing that reports will be submitted promptly and accurately when required, and most important of all, a showing that the employer has a stable employment experience. If an employer has a large turnover, the state agency would have to handle many separation notices instead of one consolidated quarterly report, such as all other employers in the state are required to fill out. If, however, the employment is stable, it means much less work for the state agency, since the number of reports handled and wage records made are reduced. Some state agencies, where all employees' base periods are uniform, are planning a system whereby those employers who might ordinarily be permitted to report on a separations basis will be given permission to file wage reports yearly instead of quarterly, although the individual employees' wage records might still be broken down on a quarterly basis. This plan has not as yet been adopted by any state, and in any event can be adopted only by those states which operate on the uniform base period and benefit year system, since only then is it unnecessary to have an employee's wages until the end of his base period, after which all employees' benefit years begin. It can be seen that if an individual employee were entitled to use wage credits earned almost immediately prior to his separation an annual reporting plan would be impossible since the employee's wage records would not be available at the time he applied for benefits.

Employers in those states which have a merit rating system are particularly interested in the benefits paid to their former employees. It is on the basis of such benefits paid out that the employer's reserve ratio is determined, which serves to fix his rate of contributions for succeeding years. The method used by states which have merit rating to determine which employers should be charged for benefits paid to their former employees will be found in the tabular material of Chapter V. The difficulty in making such charges against employers' accounts immediately on the payment of benefits can be appreciated when it is remembered that it is usually the most recent employer who is to be charged while, as a matter of fact, the state agency may have no information on the amount earned by an unemployed individual from his most recent employer if his employment fell outside his base period. 

This administrative difficulty and others make it extremely difficult for state agencies to inform employers of how much, if anything, has been charged against their account for merit rating

purposes. Because of this administrative difficulty, employers must soon realize that a more simplified system of merit rating must be adopted if it is to be made to work at all, and that such simplification must be mainly in the direction of basing an employer's rate on some other basis than his individual experience. Such a simplified plan has already won some favor recently. Texas, for example, has already adopted it, and Illinois, at the date of publication, is considering its adoption. The plan in essence provides that all employers in an individual's base period will be charged with the maximum amount of benefits an unemployed individual may receive immediately on his receipt of the first week of benefits. The amount of such charges are then compared with the total benefit charges made against all employers for that year and the employer's rate is then based, by means of a table, on how much he falls above or below the average for all the state's employers as a whole. The simplification in this scheme is obvious, in that the charges against the employers' accounts are made as soon as the first benefit check is paid instead of waiting until the employee has drawn benefits through his benefit year. 

The employer's payroll taxes are deductible either as business expenses or taxes, for the purpose of determining net income under the income tax laws. The employees' old age insurance tax is not deductible for purposes of income taxes, but in those states where employees' unemployment compensation contributions are levied the amount collected from an employee is deductible from his federal income tax either as a business expense or as a tax imposed by another jurisdiction, depending on whether the state law considers the exaction as a tax or not. 

Up to now employers have not paid much attention to the accounting problems inherent in payroll taxes, but, as it is realized that such payments form the single largest item in the employers' tax bill, closer attention will be paid to the problems disclosed. 

The first step toward the solution of such questions is the knowledge that the employer's books properly reflect the true state of affairs of the employer's business, so that any plans devised for merit-rating or tax-saving purposes will not fail through the lack of proper knowledge and information.
